Age cutoff for nursingḤanafī: nursing within 2.5 lunar years. Mālikī: 2 years + a small grace. Shāfiʿī/Ḥanbalī: strict 2 lunar years. After cutoff, no prohibition is created.
Father's lawful intercourse partnersAll schools: a woman lawfully possessed by the father is also forbidden — including (in classical law) a slave-concubine. This is no longer practically relevant.
Related verses
Q 4:24 (continuation: also forbidden are married women, except what your right hand possesses). Q 24:31 (list of male maḥārim before whom a woman may unveil). Q 33:50 (the women lawful to the Prophet ﷺ).
Quran 4:24
Marriage validity Mahr obligation
وَالْمُحْصَنَاتُ مِنَ النِّسَاءِ إِلَّا مَا مَلَكَتْ أَيْمَانُكُمْ ۖ ... وَأُحِلَّ لَكُم مَّا وَرَاءَ ذَٰلِكُمْ أَن تَبْتَغُوا بِأَمْوَالِكُم مُّحْصِنِينَ غَيْرَ مُسَافِحِينَ ۚ فَمَا اسْتَمْتَعْتُم بِهِ مِنْهُنَّ فَآتُوهُنَّ أُجُورَهُنَّ فَرِيضَةً
"And (forbidden also) are married women, except those (captives) whom your right hand possesses ... Beyond these, all others are lawful to you, that you may seek them with your wealth in honest wedlock, not in fornication. As to those with whom you have consummated marriage, give them their dowries as a duty (farīḍa)."
Plain meaning
Closes the prohibited list — married women are forbidden; all other women (outside the prohibited categories above) are lawful for marriage. The verse then states three conditions: (1) seek marriage with your wealth — meaning paying mahr; (2) muḥṣinīn — in honest wedlock, not casual relations; (3) the mahr after consummation is farīḍa — an obligation, not optional.
Majority view
All four schools: marriage requires (1) two parties who are not in the prohibited categories, (2) consent, (3) two witnesses (Ḥanafī requires Muslim witnesses; others vary), (4) mahr (a value, even minimal). The phrase fa-mā istamtaʿtum bihi in this verse is used by Twelver Shia as the basis for mutʿa (temporary marriage) — universally rejected by the four Sunni schools, who hold that mutʿa was permitted briefly and then abrogated by the Prophet ﷺ (ḥadīth of ʿAlī, Muslim).
Where the schools differ
Sunni four schoolsMutʿa (temporary marriage with a set end-date) is ḥarām. The verse refers to ordinary marriage; istamtaʿtum = "the enjoyment of marriage", not a special temporary form.
Twelver Shia (Jaʿfarī)Mutʿa is lawful with conditions: explicit duration, explicit mahr, no inheritance, no maintenance. Cite this verse plus a different reading of the abrogation reports.
"Married women" + captivesClassical schools allowed marriage to captured women whose pagan husbands were left behind — applied in conquest contexts. Inoperative today; the principle that married women are otherwise forbidden remains absolute.
Related verses
Q 4:25 (those who cannot afford to marry free women may marry believing maids). Q 2:235 (do not contract marriage during ʿidda). Q 5:5 (lawful to marry chaste women of the People of the Book).
Quran 4:25
Marriage when poor Believing maids
وَمَن لَّمْ يَسْتَطِعْ مِنكُمْ طَوْلًا أَن يَنكِحَ الْمُحْصَنَاتِ الْمُؤْمِنَاتِ فَمِن مَّا مَلَكَتْ أَيْمَانُكُم مِّن فَتَيَاتِكُمُ الْمُؤْمِنَاتِ ۚ ... وَأَن تَصْبِرُوا خَيْرٌ لَّكُمْ
"Whoever among you cannot afford to marry the free chaste believing women, then (he may marry) from the believing maids whom your right hands possess ... But that you should be patient (and abstain) is better for you."
Plain meaning
A historically situated verse — addressing the case of a free Muslim man who could not afford the mahr and maintenance required for a free believing woman. In that context, the verse permitted marriage to a believing female slave, with the slave's owner's permission and proper mahr. The closing note is striking: patience is better. Marriage to a slave was treated as a concession, not an ideal.
Majority view
All four schools accept this as a concession applicable when slavery existed. With the global abolition of slavery, this verse's direct application has ended. Its enduring principle: financial hardship is not a reason to engage in zinā. The verse's closing — patience is better — pairs with Q 24:33 (let those who cannot afford marriage remain chaste until Allah enriches them).
Related verses
Q 24:32–33 (community help marriage; chastity until Allah enriches). Q 4:24 (mahr is a duty). Q 2:221 (marry believing women — even a slave-woman is better than a polytheist).
Quran 4:29
Wrongful taking Mutual consent Suicide forbidden
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا لَا تَأْكُلُوا أَمْوَالَكُم بَيْنَكُم بِالْبَاطِلِ إِلَّا أَن تَكُونَ تِجَارَةً عَن تَرَاضٍ مِّنكُمْ ۚ وَلَا تَقْتُلُوا أَنفُسَكُمْ ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ كَانَ بِكُمْ رَحِيمًا
"O believers, do not consume one another's wealth in falsehood, unless it be by trade with mutual consent. And do not kill yourselves — Allah is most merciful to you."
Plain meaning
Two foundational principles in one verse: (1) wealth: all property transfers between Muslims must be either lawful (sale, gift, inheritance) or by mutual consent in trade — anything else is bāṭil; (2) life: do not kill yourselves, and (per the majority) do not kill one another, since Muslims are "one body".
Majority view
All four schools cite this as the foundational verse of commercial law. From it derive: validity of sales, prohibition of fraud, prohibition of forcing transactions, prohibition of riba (consumption without exchange), legitimacy of khiyār (right to cancel). The closing phrase grounds the prohibition of suicide (and, in fiqh, self-mutilation, refusing necessary medical care to the point of death, etc.). The Prophet ﷺ: "Whoever kills himself with something in this world will be tortured with it on the Day of Resurrection" (Bukhārī, Muslim).
Related verses
Q 2:188 (do not consume wealth between you wrongfully or use it to bribe judges). Q 4:161 (taking ribā while forbidden). Q 2:195 (do not throw yourselves into destruction by your own hands). Q 5:32 (whoever kills a soul, it is as if he killed all mankind).
Quran 4:32
No envy Earned share
وَلَا تَتَمَنَّوْا مَا فَضَّلَ اللَّهُ بِهِ بَعْضَكُمْ عَلَىٰ بَعْضٍ ۚ لِّلرِّجَالِ نَصِيبٌ مِّمَّا اكْتَسَبُوا ۖ وَلِلنِّسَاءِ نَصِيبٌ مِّمَّا اكْتَسَبْنَ ۚ وَاسْأَلُوا اللَّهَ مِن فَضْلِهِ
"Do not covet that by which Allah has favored some of you over others. To men is a share of what they have earned, and to women is a share of what they have earned. Ask Allah of His bounty."
Plain meaning
Revealed (per ʿĀʾisha and Umm Salama) when women asked the Prophet ﷺ why men get larger inheritance shares and the chance to fight in jihād. The verse responds: don't envy what Allah has differently assigned. Then it states something rarely emphasized: both men and women have their own share of their own earnings. A woman's earned wealth is hers, not her husband's or guardian's.
Majority view
All four schools: a woman's earnings, inheritance, and mahr are her absolute property. The husband has no claim. She has no obligation to spend on household expenses (his obligation); any contribution she makes is voluntary. The verse also grounds the principle: instead of envying others' shares, supplicate Allah for His bounty in your own life.
Related verses
Q 4:7 (women inherit obligatorily). Q 2:233 (the father is responsible for maintenance of mother and child). Q 65:7 (let the man of means spend according to his means).
Quran 4:34
Qiwāma Nushūz Marital discipline
الرِّجَالُ قَوَّامُونَ عَلَى النِّسَاءِ بِمَا فَضَّلَ اللَّهُ بَعْضَهُمْ عَلَىٰ بَعْضٍ وَبِمَا أَنفَقُوا مِنْ أَمْوَالِهِمْ ۚ ... وَاللَّاتِي تَخَافُونَ نُشُوزَهُنَّ فَعِظُوهُنَّ وَاهْجُرُوهُنَّ فِي الْمَضَاجِعِ وَاضْرِبُوهُنَّ ۖ فَإِنْ أَطَعْنَكُمْ فَلَا تَبْغُوا عَلَيْهِنَّ سَبِيلًا
"Men are qawwāmūn over women by what Allah has favored some over others and by what they spend of their wealth ... Those (wives) from whom you fear nushūz (severe disloyalty / mistreatment): admonish them, separate from them in beds, and (then) strike them. If they obey you, seek no further course against them."
Plain meaning
The most debated verse in the Quran. Qawwām ≠ "ruler" — it carries the sense of one who maintains, protects, manages financially. The reasoning is given in the verse: it is grounded in the man's financial obligation (maintenance, mahr) — not inherent superiority. Nushūz here is serious wifely disloyalty (not minor disagreement). The verse outlines a three-step escalation: (1) verbal advice, (2) separation in sleeping arrangements, (3) ḍarb. The Prophet ﷺ himself never struck a woman, and ḥadīth specify if any "strike" occurs it must be non-injurious, not on the face, and "with a siwāk (twig)".
Majority view
All four classical schools: the three-step process is sequential — admonition first, then separation in bed, then a symbolic, non-harming strike only as a last resort to save the marriage from breakdown. Striking that injures (bruise, broken skin, fear) is universally ḥarām — it constitutes ḍarar (harm) and gives the wife grounds for judicial divorce with full mahr. Modern scholars (Ibn ʿĀshūr, Ramadan al-Būṭī, contemporary Mālikīs) emphasize the verse permits nothing more than symbolic gesture and many argue the third step is functionally inoperative since the conditions (non-harm, last resort, no anger) make it nearly impossible to discharge correctly.
Where the schools differ
All four classical schoolsPermission for non-injurious strike exists in principle as a last-resort measure. Hitting that causes any mark or injury is sinful and grounds for criminal liability and judicial divorce.
Modern reformistMany read iḍribūhunna as "separate from them" (a less common but linguistically valid meaning of ḍ-r-b, as in "ḍaraba fī l-arḍ" — travel). On this reading, the three steps are: words, bed-separation, full separation.
Application todaySeveral Muslim-majority states (Tunisia, Morocco family code reforms) and most contemporary jurists hold that any striking of a wife is impermissible given universally evolved standards of harm.
Related verses
Q 4:19 (live with them in kindness). Q 30:21 (between you love and mercy). Q 2:228 (men have a degree over them — contextually about divorce procedure, not absolute superiority). Q 4:128 (when a husband shows nushūz — the verse uses the same word symmetrically).
Quran 4:35
Marital arbitration Family mediation
وَإِنْ خِفْتُمْ شِقَاقَ بَيْنِهِمَا فَابْعَثُوا حَكَمًا مِّنْ أَهْلِهِ وَحَكَمًا مِّنْ أَهْلِهَا إِن يُرِيدَا إِصْلَاحًا يُوَفِّقِ اللَّهُ بَيْنَهُمَا
"If you fear a breach between the two (spouses), appoint an arbiter from his family and an arbiter from her family. If they (the arbiters / couple) intend reconciliation, Allah will bring them to harmony."
Plain meaning
Before divorce: mandatory mediation. One arbiter from each side — meaning, not "lawyers" but trusted family members who know the spouses and will negotiate in good faith. The purpose is iṣlāḥ (reconciliation), not legal partisanship.
Majority view
All four schools: arbitration is required before judicial intervention, when reconciliation has failed at the couple level. The arbiters' role: investigate, recommend, and (in classical Mālikī view) bind the couple to their judgment, including divorce if irreconcilable. This verse is the basis for modern family courts in Muslim-majority countries requiring mediation before granting divorce.
Where the schools differ
Ḥanafī/ShāfiʿīThe arbiters are advisors only. Their recommendation, even to divorce, requires the husband's consent (since divorce belongs to him).
Mālikī/ḤanbalīThe arbiters have binding authority. They may decree separation if reconciliation is impossible — even without the husband's consent — and the wife receives full mahr.
Related verses
Q 4:128 (if a woman fears nushūz from her husband — reconciliation is best). Q 49:9–10 (between two parties — reconcile them with justice).
Quran 4:36
Rights of others Parents Neighbors
وَاعْبُدُوا اللَّهَ وَلَا تُشْرِكُوا بِهِ شَيْئًا ۖ وَبِالْوَالِدَيْنِ إِحْسَانًا وَبِذِي الْقُرْبَىٰ وَالْيَتَامَىٰ وَالْمَسَاكِينِ وَالْجَارِ ذِي الْقُرْبَىٰ وَالْجَارِ الْجُنُبِ وَالصَّاحِبِ بِالْجَنبِ وَابْنِ السَّبِيلِ وَمَا مَلَكَتْ أَيْمَانُكُمْ
"Worship Allah and associate nothing with Him. Be good to parents, relatives, orphans, the poor, the near neighbor, the distant neighbor, the companion at your side, the wayfarer, and those whom your right hand possesses (dependents)."
Plain meaning
A complete map of obligations to others in one verse, ranked from closest (parents) outward: parents → relatives → orphans → poor → near neighbor → distant neighbor → companion at one's side → traveler → dependents. The opening pairs worship of Allah with excellent treatment (iḥsān) of these groups — they are presented together as parts of one duty.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) birr al-wālidayn — kindness to parents is an absolute obligation; (2) silat al-raḥim — kinship ties; (3) ḥuqūq al-jār — neighbor's rights (the Prophet ﷺ said Jibrīl repeatedly emphasized neighbors to him until he thought they would inherit). "Companion at the side" = travel companion / spouse / coworker — anyone in close proximity. "Wayfarer" grounds hospitality obligations.
Related verses
Q 17:23–24 (do not say "uff" to parents — lower wing of humility). Q 31:14 (carried in weakness upon weakness). Q 2:177 (true piety includes spending on relatives, orphans, the needy).
Quran 4:43
Intoxication ban (stage 2) Tayammum
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا لَا تَقْرَبُوا الصَّلَاةَ وَأَنتُمْ سُكَارَىٰ حَتَّىٰ تَعْلَمُوا مَا تَقُولُونَ ... وَإِن كُنتُم مَّرْضَىٰ أَوْ عَلَىٰ سَفَرٍ أَوْ جَاءَ أَحَدٌ مِّنكُم مِّنَ الْغَائِطِ أَوْ لَامَسْتُمُ النِّسَاءَ فَلَمْ تَجِدُوا مَاءً فَتَيَمَّمُوا صَعِيدًا طَيِّبًا
"O believers, do not approach prayer while intoxicated, until you know what you are saying ... If you are ill, on a journey, or have come from relieving yourselves, or have had intimate contact with women, and find no water — then perform tayammum with pure earth..."
Plain meaning
The second of three stages restricting alcohol — earlier Q 2:219 said "in them is some benefit but greater sin"; this verse bans prayer while drunk; Q 5:90 finally bans alcohol entirely. The verse also legislates tayammum — dry purification with clean earth when water is unavailable, sick, or harmful — a permanent ease (rukhṣa).
Majority view
All four schools accept that this alcohol prohibition was superseded — alcohol is now banned at all times, not just before prayer (per Q 5:90). On tayammum: required by all schools when water is absent, when water would harm the user's health, or when water is far enough that seeking it would cause harm or missing prayer. Lāmastum ("touched"): differing interpretations across schools.
Where the schools differ
ḤanafīLāmastum = intercourse only. Mere skin contact between man and woman does not break wuḍū. Tayammum: one strike on earth, wipes face and arms.
ShāfiʿīLāmastum = any skin contact between non-maḥram man and woman breaks wuḍū. Tayammum: two strikes — one for face, one for arms to elbows.
MālikīLāmastum = touch with desire breaks wuḍū; incidental touch does not. Tayammum: one strike, may extend to wrists only as obligatory; up to elbows is recommended.
ḤanbalīSimilar to Mālikī (touch with desire); tayammum to wrists.
Related verses
Q 2:219 (alcohol — some benefit but greater sin). Q 5:90–91 (final alcohol prohibition). Q 5:6 (parallel tayammum verse).
Quran 4:48
Shirk unforgivable Tawḥīd
إِنَّ اللَّهَ لَا يَغْفِرُ أَن يُشْرَكَ بِهِ وَيَغْفِرُ مَا دُونَ ذَٰلِكَ لِمَن يَشَاءُ ۚ وَمَن يُشْرِكْ بِاللَّهِ فَقَدِ افْتَرَىٰ إِثْمًا عَظِيمًا
"Allah does not forgive that anything be associated with Him, but forgives anything less than that for whom He wills. Whoever associates anything with Allah has fabricated a tremendous sin."
Plain meaning
A foundational verse of Islamic theology with direct legal implications. Shirk — assigning partners or rivals to Allah — is the only sin Allah will not forgive if one dies upon it. All other sins, however major, are forgivable. The verse implicitly clarifies: anyone who turns from shirk and repents is forgiven — the prohibition is on dying in shirk.
Majority view
All schools and creeds: this verse is the legal foundation for treating shirk as the ultimate violation. From it derive: invalidity of inheritance between a Muslim and a non-Muslim relative (classical view), inadmissibility of testimony from polytheists in Muslim courts (classical view), the obligation to invite to tawḥīd as the highest priority. It also grounds Islamic optimism about forgiveness — every other sin, however large, is forgivable.
Related verses
Q 4:116 (verse is repeated almost verbatim — emphasizing the rule). Q 31:13 (shirk is the great wrong — ẓulm ʿaẓīm). Q 39:53 (do not despair of Allah's mercy — He forgives all sins). Q 25:68–70 (those who repent — Allah will replace their sins with good deeds).
Quran 4:58
Trusts (amāna) Just rulership
إِنَّ اللَّهَ يَأْمُرُكُمْ أَن تُؤَدُّوا الْأَمَانَاتِ إِلَىٰ أَهْلِهَا وَإِذَا حَكَمْتُم بَيْنَ النَّاسِ أَن تَحْكُمُوا بِالْعَدْلِ ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ نِعِمَّا يَعِظُكُم بِهِ
"Allah commands you to return trusts (amānāt) to their owners, and when you judge between people, to judge with justice. Excellent indeed is what Allah counsels you."
Plain meaning
Two foundational duties: (1) amāna — anything entrusted to you (money, secrets, public office, responsibilities) must be returned to its proper owner; (2) ʿadl — judging between people, in any capacity, must be done with justice. Revealed (per most reports) about the key to the Kaʿba, which the Prophet ﷺ returned to its non-Muslim custodian even after conquering Makkah.
Majority view
All four schools regard this as a foundational verse of governance and judicial ethics. From amāna: public office is a trust, embezzlement is a major sin, deposits must be returned even to enemies, secrets shared in confidence must be kept. From ʿadl: judges must be impartial regardless of party — including against their own kin, against the powerful, against fellow Muslims if the case favors the non-Muslim. Q 4:135 reinforces: "be witnesses in justice, even against yourselves, parents, or kin".
Related verses
Q 4:135 (stand firm in justice, witnesses for Allah, even against yourselves). Q 5:8 (do not let hatred of a people lead you to injustice — be just; that is closer to taqwā). Q 38:26 (judge between people in truth, do not follow desire). Q 33:72 (the amāna was offered to the heavens — humans accepted it).
Quran 4:59
Obedience Authority Dispute resolution
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا أَطِيعُوا اللَّهَ وَأَطِيعُوا الرَّسُولَ وَأُولِي الْأَمْرِ مِنكُمْ ۖ فَإِن تَنَازَعْتُمْ فِي شَيْءٍ فَرُدُّوهُ إِلَى اللَّهِ وَالرَّسُولِ إِن كُنتُمْ تُؤْمِنُونَ بِاللَّهِ وَالْيَوْمِ الْآخِرِ ۚ ذَٰلِكَ خَيْرٌ وَأَحْسَنُ تَأْوِيلًا
"O believers, obey Allah, obey the Messenger, and those in authority among you. If you dispute over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger — if you truly believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is better and more excellent in outcome."
Plain meaning
The foundational verse of Islamic governance. Three levels of authority in descending order: (1) Allah (the Quran), (2) the Messenger ﷺ (Sunnah), (3) ūlū l-amr — those given authority (rulers, scholars). Note the wording: "obey Allah and obey the Messenger" — but for ūlū l-amr, no separate "and obey" — meaning their obedience is conditional on the first two. When disputes arise, the appeal is back to Allah (Quran) and the Messenger (Sunnah) — not to ūlū l-amr.
Majority view
All four schools: obedience to legitimate rulers is obligatory in matters of obedience to Allah. The Prophet ﷺ: "There is no obedience to a creature in disobedience to the Creator" (Aḥmad). Disputes over legal interpretation are resolved by appeal to Quran and Sunnah — this is the verse that establishes ijtihād (independent reasoning from sources) as the method when sources do not yield a clear answer. Ūlū l-amr, in classical readings, includes both political rulers and scholars (ʿulamāʾ) — Ibn ʿAbbās and Mujāhid favored scholars; others, rulers.
Where the schools differ
Sunni schoolsObedience to a ruler is required even if unjust, as long as he does not order disobedience to Allah and the basic markers of Islam are maintained — to avoid civil war (fitna). Rebellion is permitted only against open kufr (visible disbelief in office).
Mūʿtazila / classical KhārijīsObedience to an unjust ruler is itself wrong; commanding right against him is obligatory even by force.
Twelver Shia"Ūlū l-amr" = the infallible imams. Disputes are resolved through them. Different basis for governance entirely.
Related verses
Q 3:32 (obey Allah and the Messenger). Q 4:80 (whoever obeys the Messenger obeys Allah). Q 24:54 (if you obey the Messenger, you will be rightly guided). Q 42:38 (their affairs are by mutual consultation).
Quran 4:65
Submission to Prophet's judgment Faith
فَلَا وَرَبِّكَ لَا يُؤْمِنُونَ حَتَّىٰ يُحَكِّمُوكَ فِيمَا شَجَرَ بَيْنَهُمْ ثُمَّ لَا يَجِدُوا فِي أَنفُسِهِمْ حَرَجًا مِّمَّا قَضَيْتَ وَيُسَلِّمُوا تَسْلِيمًا
"By your Lord, they cannot truly believe until they make you judge in what disputes them, then find no resistance in themselves to what you have decided, and submit with complete submission."
Plain meaning
A startlingly direct verse on the conditions of faith. Three layers in ascending order: (1) making the Prophet ﷺ the judge (external submission), (2) internal acceptance with no lingering resentment (heart-submission), (3) full taslīm — total submission (will-submission). Anything short of all three falls short of true belief.
Majority view
All four schools: this verse grounds the obligation to accept the rulings of the Quran and Sunnah, even where they conflict with personal preference. It is foundational for the legitimacy of Sunnah as law-bearing — not merely advice. The verse was revealed (per most reports) about a Companion who disputed with Zubayr over irrigation; when the Prophet ﷺ ruled in Zubayr's favor, the man resented it. The verse warns that such resentment itself is incompatible with faith.
Related verses
Q 33:36 (no choice for any believer when Allah and His Messenger have decided). Q 24:51 (the saying of believers is: we hear and we obey). Q 4:115 (whoever opposes the Messenger after guidance is clear...).
Quran 4:69
Companionship in Paradise Four ranks of believers
وَمَن يُطِعِ اللَّهَ وَالرَّسُولَ فَأُولَٰئِكَ مَعَ الَّذِينَ أَنْعَمَ اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِم مِّنَ النَّبِيِّينَ وَالصِّدِّيقِينَ وَالشُّهَدَاءِ وَالصَّالِحِينَ ۚ وَحَسُنَ أُولَٰئِكَ رَفِيقًا
"Whoever obeys Allah and the Messenger — they are with those Allah has blessed: the prophets, the truthful (ṣiddīqīn), the martyrs (shuhadāʾ), and the righteous (ṣāliḥīn). What excellent companions they are!"
Plain meaning
A consoling promise. Obedience to Allah and His Messenger — even from the most ordinary believer — places one in the company of the four highest classes of the saved: (1) prophets; (2) ṣiddīqīn — those whose entire being is honesty; (3) shuhadāʾ — martyrs (both literal and figurative); (4) ṣāliḥīn — the righteous. The verse closes with what may be the most beautiful sentence in scripture: ḥasuna ulāʾika rafīqan — "what excellent companions they are".
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the hierarchy of believers in the Hereafter — used in tafsīr and ḥadīth literature; (2) the consolation that obedience — not extraordinary status — is the qualification for elevated company; (3) Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq is the foremost example of ṣiddīqīn — those whose truth-telling extends even to immediate belief in the Night Journey. The verse pairs with Q 4:80: obeying the Messenger ﷺ is obeying Allah.
Related verses
Q 4:80 (whoever obeys the Messenger has obeyed Allah). Q 57:19 (those who believe in Allah and His messengers are the ṣiddīqīn and the shuhadāʾ before their Lord). Q 1:7 (the path of those You have blessed — referenced by every Muslim in every Fātiḥa).
Quran 4:75
Fighting for the oppressed Mustaḍʿafīn
وَمَا لَكُمْ لَا تُقَاتِلُونَ فِي سَبِيلِ اللَّهِ وَالْمُسْتَضْعَفِينَ مِنَ الرِّجَالِ وَالنِّسَاءِ وَالْوِلْدَانِ الَّذِينَ يَقُولُونَ رَبَّنَا أَخْرِجْنَا مِنْ هَٰذِهِ الْقَرْيَةِ الظَّالِمِ أَهْلُهَا وَاجْعَل لَّنَا مِن لَّدُنكَ وَلِيًّا وَاجْعَل لَّنَا مِن لَّدُنكَ نَصِيرًا
"Why should you not fight in Allah's way and for the oppressed (mustaḍʿafīn) — men, women, and children — who say: 'Our Lord, take us out of this town whose people are oppressors; appoint for us from Yourself a protector and a helper'?"
Plain meaning
Striking framing: combat is not just for Allah but also for the oppressed. The verse describes specifically the early Muslims trapped in Mecca, calling out to Allah for rescue. By extension, fighting in defense of victims of oppression — regardless of their religion or relation — is a religiously sanctioned cause.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the moral and legal grounds for humanitarian intervention in classical Islamic political ethics — protecting the oppressed is a legitimate casus belli; (2) the principle that Muslim ethical obligation extends beyond fellow Muslims to all who are oppressed; (3) the related obligation of rescue — financial, diplomatic, military — for those facing systematic harm. The verse closes a long discussion in Sūrat al-Nisāʾ about the moral conditions of fighting.
Related verses
Q 8:72 (those who emigrated and strove for those who emigrated). Q 22:39–40 (permission to fight those wronged). Q 4:97 (the angels said to those who wronged themselves — was Allah's earth not spacious?). Q 4:98 (the truly weak — men, women, children — with no ability to escape).
Quran 4:80
Authority of the Messenger Sunnah
مَّن يُطِعِ الرَّسُولَ فَقَدْ أَطَاعَ اللَّهَ ۖ وَمَن تَوَلَّىٰ فَمَا أَرْسَلْنَاكَ عَلَيْهِمْ حَفِيظًا
"Whoever obeys the Messenger has obeyed Allah. And whoever turns away — We have not sent you over them as a guardian."
Plain meaning
A short, decisive verse. Obedience to the Messenger ﷺ is obedience to Allah — they cannot be separated. Likewise, disobedience to the Messenger is disobedience to Allah. The Prophet's ﷺ role, however, ends with delivering the message; he is not the cosmic enforcer of compliance — that is Allah's domain.
Majority view
All four schools and the entire Sunni tradition: this verse establishes the binding nature of the Sunnah. A Muslim who accepts the Quran but rejects authentic ḥadīth claims to obey Allah while disobeying the Messenger — the verse explicitly forbids this separation. From it derives the entire science of ḥadīth as a source of law equal in authority to the Quran (though Quran's verses are more certain in transmission). Quranists who reject ḥadīth are universally considered outside the Sunni mainstream.
Related verses
Q 59:7 (what the Messenger gives you, take; what he forbids you, abstain from). Q 33:21 (in the Messenger of Allah is an excellent example). Q 4:69 (those who obey Allah and the Messenger are with the prophets, the truthful, the martyrs, the righteous).
Quran 4:86
Returning greetings Better or equal
وَإِذَا حُيِّيتُم بِتَحِيَّةٍ فَحَيُّوا بِأَحْسَنَ مِنْهَا أَوْ رُدُّوهَا ۗ إِنَّ اللَّهَ كَانَ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَيْءٍ حَسِيبًا
"When you are greeted with a greeting, respond with a better one — or (at least) return the same. Allah keeps account of all things."
Plain meaning
A short, foundational verse on the ethics of greeting. When greeted, two options: (1) respond with better than what was offered (e.g., "salām" with "wa-ʿalaykumu s-salām wa-raḥmatu Llāhi wa-barakātuh"); (2) at minimum, return the same. Ignoring a greeting is not an option. The verse closes with a financial-accounting term: Ḥasīb — every interaction is logged.
Majority view
All four schools: returning a greeting is obligatory (wājib) when one is offered. The initial greeting itself is recommended but not mandatory. The Prophet ﷺ specified: when only one person is present, returning is individually obligatory; when many are present and someone responds, the obligation is discharged for all. Schools differ slightly on whether the greeting must be voiced verbally or whether nodding is sufficient — majority view: voiced is required unless one cannot.
Related verses
Q 24:27 (greet upon entering a home). Q 6:54 (when those who believe in Our signs come to you, say: peace upon you). Q 24:61 (when you enter houses, greet one another — a greeting from Allah, blessed and good).
Quran 4:92–93
Manslaughter Murder Diya / Kaffāra
وَمَا كَانَ لِمُؤْمِنٍ أَن يَقْتُلَ مُؤْمِنًا إِلَّا خَطَأً ۚ وَمَن قَتَلَ مُؤْمِنًا خَطَأً فَتَحْرِيرُ رَقَبَةٍ مُّؤْمِنَةٍ وَدِيَةٌ مُّسَلَّمَةٌ إِلَىٰ أَهْلِهِ ... وَمَن يَقْتُلْ مُؤْمِنًا مُّتَعَمِّدًا فَجَزَاؤُهُ جَهَنَّمُ خَالِدًا فِيهَا وَغَضِبَ اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَلَعَنَهُ وَأَعَدَّ لَهُ عَذَابًا عَظِيمًا
"It is not for a believer to kill another believer — except by mistake. Whoever kills a believer by mistake: the freeing of a believing slave and blood-money (diya) paid to the victim's family ... Whoever kills a believer intentionally: his recompense is Hell, dwelling therein forever — Allah's anger and curse are upon him, and a great torment is prepared for him."
Plain meaning
The Quran's most direct legislation on homicide. Manslaughter (khaṭāʾ) — accidental killing — requires: (1) kaffāra (atonement) of freeing a believing slave (today: 60 days of consecutive fasting per the parallel kaffāra pattern); (2) diya — blood-money paid to the victim's heirs unless they remit it. Intentional murder (ʿamd) — its threat in the Quran is the most severe of any sin: eternal Hell, Allah's wrath, His curse. Sunni scholars qualify "eternal" as not literally permanent for a believer who repents.
Majority view
All four schools categorize homicide into three (or sometimes five) types: (1) ʿamd — intentional, with a typically-lethal weapon: qiṣāṣ (retaliation) or diya if family forgives; (2) shibh ʿamd — quasi-intentional, with a typically non-lethal instrument: diya only, no qiṣāṣ (per Ḥanafī/Ḥanbalī; Mālikī collapses this into intentional); (3) khaṭāʾ — accidental: kaffāra + diya. Diya is paid by the killer's ʿāqila (paternal kin / today: insurance equivalent), not the killer alone — in classical fiqh.
Where the schools differ
ḤanafīThree categories (intentional, quasi-intentional, accidental). Diya for full intentional homicide is paid only if family chooses pardon over qiṣāṣ.
MālikīTwo main categories — intentional or accidental. Quasi-intentional folded into intentional or accidental depending on context.
Shāfiʿī/ḤanbalīSimilar three categories as Ḥanafī. Diya amount: 100 camels (or equivalent) for a free Muslim male. Female: half. Non-Muslim under Muslim protection: varies (1/3 to full, depending on school).
Related verses
Q 2:178–179 (qiṣāṣ established). Q 5:32 (whoever kills a soul... it is as if he killed all mankind). Q 17:33 (do not kill a soul except by right; the heir of the murdered has authority).
Quran 4:97
Emigration Was Allah's earth not spacious?
إِنَّ الَّذِينَ تَوَفَّاهُمُ الْمَلَائِكَةُ ظَالِمِي أَنفُسِهِمْ قَالُوا فِيمَ كُنتُمْ ۖ قَالُوا كُنَّا مُسْتَضْعَفِينَ فِي الْأَرْضِ ۚ قَالُوا أَلَمْ تَكُنْ أَرْضُ اللَّهِ وَاسِعَةً فَتُهَاجِرُوا فِيهَا ۚ فَأُولَٰئِكَ مَأْوَاهُمْ جَهَنَّمُ ۖ وَسَاءَتْ مَصِيرًا
"Those whom the angels take while they were wronging themselves — (the angels) will say: 'What was your state?' They will say: 'We were weak in the land.' (The angels) will say: 'Was Allah's earth not spacious — so that you might have emigrated in it?' Such — their refuge is Hell, an evil destination."
Plain meaning
A piercing scene of post-death accountability. Those who lived under oppression that prevented them from practicing their faith — yet who did not emigrate when emigration was possible — are questioned. The angels' rebuke: "Was Allah's earth not spacious?" The implication: where staying compromises faith, moving is an obligation. The next verses (4:98–99) exempt those who genuinely could not move — the elderly, women and children without means, those with no route.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the obligation of hijra (migration) from environments that prevent practice of Islam — when a viable alternative exists; (2) the parallel exemption for the truly weak — Allah does not burden beyond capacity; (3) the broader principle that environmental responsibility for one's faith is part of religious duty. Modern application: parents considering emigration for religious freedom, women trapped in unjust marriages where exit is possible, workers in compromised industries — all interface with this verse.
Related verses
Q 4:75 (fighting for the oppressed). Q 4:98–99 (those truly weak — exempt). Q 16:41 (those who emigrated for Allah after being wronged — We will surely settle them in a good place). Q 29:56 (My earth is spacious — so worship Me alone).
Quran 4:101–103
Travel prayer Fear prayer
وَإِذَا ضَرَبْتُمْ فِي الْأَرْضِ فَلَيْسَ عَلَيْكُمْ جُنَاحٌ أَن تَقْصُرُوا مِنَ الصَّلَاةِ ... وَإِذَا كُنتَ فِيهِمْ فَأَقَمْتَ لَهُمُ الصَّلَاةَ فَلْتَقُمْ طَائِفَةٌ مِّنْهُم مَّعَكَ ... فَإِذَا اطْمَأْنَنتُمْ فَأَقِيمُوا الصَّلَاةَ ۚ إِنَّ الصَّلَاةَ كَانَتْ عَلَى الْمُؤْمِنِينَ كِتَابًا مَّوْقُوتًا
"When you travel through the land, there is no blame on you if you shorten the prayer ... When you are with them and lead them in prayer, let a party of them stand with you ... When you are at ease, establish prayer in full. Prayer is, for the believers, prescribed at fixed times."
Plain meaning
Three rulings: (1) qaṣr — shortening of four-rakʿa prayers to two during travel; (2) ṣalāt al-khawf — fear/battlefield prayer, performed in shifts so half the group can still keep watch; (3) prayer is kitāban mawqūtan — an obligation at fixed times that cannot be neglected even in war.
Majority view
All four schools: qaṣr applies on a journey meeting minimum distance (varies). Some hold it is obligatory (Ḥanafī); others recommended permission (Shāfiʿī, Mālikī, Ḥanbalī — the traveler may pray in full but qaṣr is preferred). Praying is required even on the battlefield — but in a modified form. The principle: no situation justifies skipping prayer entirely.
Where the schools differ
ḤanafīQaṣr is obligatory, not optional, for travelers. Minimum travel distance: ~88 km. Traveler who prays full is sinful.
MālikīQaṣr is a strongly emphasized Sunnah, almost obligatory. Minimum: ~85 km.
ShāfiʿīQaṣr is permission, not obligation. Minimum: ~88 km. Praying full is permissible.
ḤanbalīPermission. Minimum: ~88 km. Combining (jamʿ) Ẓuhr+ʿAṣr or Maghrib+ʿIshāʾ is also permitted on journey.
Related verses
Q 2:239 (if you fear, then pray walking or riding). Q 4:43 (do not approach prayer while drunk). Q 11:114 (establish prayer at the two ends of the day).
Quran 4:114
Secret counsel When private talk is good
لَّا خَيْرَ فِي كَثِيرٍ مِّن نَّجْوَاهُمْ إِلَّا مَنْ أَمَرَ بِصَدَقَةٍ أَوْ مَعْرُوفٍ أَوْ إِصْلَاحٍ بَيْنَ النَّاسِ ۚ وَمَن يَفْعَلْ ذَٰلِكَ ابْتِغَاءَ مَرْضَاتِ اللَّهِ فَسَوْيُفَ نُؤْتِيهِ أَجْرًا عَظِيمًا
"There is no good in much of their secret counsels — except for one who urges charity, kindness, or reconciliation between people. Whoever does this seeking the pleasure of Allah, We shall give him an immense reward."
Plain meaning
A precision rule for speech, particularly najwā — private/secret conversations. Most of what people share in private is harmful — gossip, plotting, complaint. Three exceptions where private speech is good: (1) urging charity, (2) urging maʿrūf (right conduct), (3) reconciling between people — and even then, only if motivated by seeking Allah's pleasure, not social positioning.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse on the ethics of speech and the special status of mediation. Notably: iṣlāḥ bayna n-nās (peacemaking) is described in Sunnah as superior in reward to nawāfil prayer and fasting. The Prophet ﷺ said: "Shall I tell you of something greater in rank than prayer, fasting, and charity? Reconciling between people, for resentment is the shaver — it shaves religion, not hair" (Tirmidhī). The verse also explicitly permits strategic deception in the context of reconciliation: lying to bring two estranged people together is not considered lying.
Related verses
Q 58:9 (when you do consult privately, do not consult about sin and aggression — only about righteousness and taqwā). Q 49:9–10 (between two parties of believers, reconcile them justly). Q 4:128 (reconciliation is best — between spouses).
Quran 4:115
Way of believers Consensus
وَمَن يُشَاقِقِ الرَّسُولَ مِن بَعْدِ مَا تَبَيَّنَ لَهُ الْهُدَىٰ وَيَتَّبِعْ غَيْرَ سَبِيلِ الْمُؤْمِنِينَ نُوَلِّهِ مَا تَوَلَّىٰ وَنُصْلِهِ جَهَنَّمَ ۖ وَسَاءَتْ مَصِيرًا
"Whoever opposes the Messenger after guidance has been made clear to him, and follows other than the way of the believers (sabīl al-muʾminīn) — We will turn him to what he has turned to, and admit him to Hell. What an evil destination."
Plain meaning
A doctrinally critical verse. Two requirements for the saved path: (1) not opposing the Messenger ﷺ after the truth is clear; (2) following the way of the believers — i.e., the collective path. To violate either is to walk away from guidance.
Majority view
All four Sunni schools cite this verse as the foundational textual proof for the authority of ijmāʿ (consensus of the Muslim community). The verse: not just the Messenger, but the way of the believers is a binding source. Imām al-Shāfiʿī argued this verse alone establishes ijmāʿ as a source of law. The classical Sunni position: when the scholarly community reaches unanimous agreement on a matter, departing from it is religiously prohibited.
Where the schools differ
Sunni four schoolsConsensus (ijmāʿ) is a binding source of law, after Quran and Sunnah. Some schools require only ijmāʿ of the Companions; others extend to scholarly consensus of any generation.
Modern reformist (small)Argues ijmāʿ should be limited to clearly-established matters; not a license for scholarly elites to bind the community on every issue. Mainstream view: ijmāʿ on clear doctrinal and legal matters is binding.
Related verses
Q 3:103 (hold to the rope of Allah, all of you, and do not be divided). Q 4:59 (refer disputes back to Allah and the Messenger). Q 9:100 (those who followed the early Muslims — Allah is pleased with them).
Quran 4:127
Orphan girls Justice for women
وَيَسْتَفْتُونَكَ فِي النِّسَاءِ ۖ قُلِ اللَّهُ يُفْتِيكُمْ فِيهِنَّ ... فِي يَتَامَى النِّسَاءِ اللَّاتِي لَا تُؤْتُونَهُنَّ مَا كُتِبَ لَهُنَّ وَتَرْغَبُونَ أَن تَنكِحُوهُنَّ وَالْمُسْتَضْعَفِينَ مِنَ الْوِلْدَانِ وَأَن تَقُومُوا لِلْيَتَامَىٰ بِالْقِسْطِ
"They ask you for a ruling on women. Say: Allah gives you a ruling about them ... concerning orphan girls to whom you do not give what is prescribed for them, yet desire to marry them; concerning weak children; and that you maintain justice for orphans."
Plain meaning
Returning to the theme that opened the surah (Q 4:3). Guardians of orphan girls would sometimes withhold their proper mahr, marry them for their property, or refuse to give them their inheritance. This verse restates: orphans' rights — particularly girls' — are non-negotiable, regardless of guardian's wishes.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for three rulings: (1) a guardian who wishes to marry his orphan ward must pay her standard mahr for her status, not less; (2) orphans' inheritance must be released to them on maturity (Q 4:6); (3) "weak children" includes both orphans and minors whose property is held by guardians — all are protected by the same justice principle.
Related verses
Q 4:3 (if you fear injustice toward orphans, marry others). Q 4:6 (test orphans; release their property at maturity). Q 4:10 (consuming orphans' wealth wrongfully).
Quran 4:128–130
Husband's nushūz Reconciliation Divorce
وَإِنِ امْرَأَةٌ خَافَتْ مِن بَعْلِهَا نُشُوزًا أَوْ إِعْرَاضًا فَلَا جُنَاحَ عَلَيْهِمَا أَن يُصْلِحَا بَيْنَهُمَا صُلْحًا ۚ وَالصُّلْحُ خَيْرٌ ۗ ... وَلَن تَسْتَطِيعُوا أَن تَعْدِلُوا بَيْنَ النِّسَاءِ وَلَوْ حَرَصْتُمْ ۖ ... وَإِن يَتَفَرَّقَا يُغْنِ اللَّهُ كُلًّا مِّن سَعَتِهِ
"If a woman fears mistreatment or aversion from her husband, there is no blame on them in reconciling between themselves — and reconciliation is best ... You will never be able to be perfectly just between wives, however much you try ... If they separate, Allah will provide for each from His abundance."
Plain meaning
The mirror image of Q 4:34 — but now husband's nushūz against wife. Three movements: (1) negotiate — she may agree to relinquish some right (e.g., reduce his maintenance burden) to preserve marriage; (2) emotional equality between wives is impossible — so a husband must focus on not tilting wholly away from one; (3) if separation occurs, Allah promises provision — divorce is not financial ruin.
Majority view
All four schools: when a marriage breaks down due to the husband's actions, reconciliation is preferred but not forced. The wife may "negotiate" — historically, this often meant Sawda's choice (the Prophet's ﷺ wife) to give her night to ʿĀʾisha to retain her place in marriage. Q 4:129 is critical: it concedes that emotional justice between wives is impossible, requiring only material justice. Modern jurists read this verse alongside Q 4:3 to severely restrict polygamy in practice.
Where the schools differ
All schoolsSettlement (ṣulḥ) between spouses is encouraged. Wife may waive certain rights voluntarily — though not core rights like mahr (which is hers absolutely after consummation).
On Q 4:129Unanimous: emotional equality is humanly impossible. Husband is held only to material/time equality.
Related verses
Q 4:34 (mirror image — wife's nushūz). Q 4:3 (justice condition for polygamy). Q 2:229 (release in kindness or retain in kindness). Q 65:7 (Allah will grant ease after hardship).
Quran 4:135
Justice Even against self Witness
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا كُونُوا قَوَّامِينَ بِالْقِسْطِ شُهَدَاءَ لِلَّهِ وَلَوْ عَلَىٰ أَنفُسِكُمْ أَوِ الْوَالِدَيْنِ وَالْأَقْرَبِينَ ۚ إِن يَكُنْ غَنِيًّا أَوْ فَقِيرًا فَاللَّهُ أَوْلَىٰ بِهِمَا ۖ فَلَا تَتَّبِعُوا الْهَوَىٰ أَن تَعْدِلُوا
"O believers, be steadfast in justice — witnesses for Allah — even against yourselves, your parents, or your kin. Whether (the party) is rich or poor, Allah is most worthy of both. So do not follow desire, lest you stray from justice."
Plain meaning
One of the most demanding verses in the Quran. Justice (qisṭ) is owed even against oneself (a stunning command), parents, and relatives. Wealth or poverty of the parties is not a reason to tilt: Allah's claim on both is greater than any human consideration. The verse pinpoints the root cause of injustice — hawā, personal desire/bias.
Majority view
Foundational verse for: judicial impartiality, witness ethics, prohibition of favoritism in office, and the Islamic principle that no relationship overrides justice. The Prophet ﷺ refused to intercede for a noble Makhzūm woman who had stolen, saying: "Those before you perished because they punished the lowly for stealing and pardoned the noble. By Allah, if Fāṭima daughter of Muhammad had stolen, I would have cut off her hand" (Bukhārī, Muslim). The verse also requires self-testimony against one's interest — admissions in court are binding.
Related verses
Q 5:8 (do not let hatred of a people lead you to injustice). Q 4:58 (judge between people with justice). Q 6:152 (be just, even if it concerns a relative). Q 16:90 (Allah commands justice and excellence).
Quran 4:138–139
Honor with Allah Munāfiqūn warning
بَشِّرِ الْمُنَافِقِينَ بِأَنَّ لَهُمْ عَذَابًا أَلِيمًا ۝ الَّذِينَ يَتَّخِذُونَ الْكَافِرِينَ أَوْلِيَاءَ مِن دُونِ الْمُؤْمِنِينَ ۚ أَيَبْتَغُونَ عِندَهُمُ الْعِزَّةَ فَإِنَّ الْعِزَّةَ لِلَّهِ جَمِيعًا
"Give the hypocrites the news that there is for them a painful punishment — those who take disbelievers as awliyāʾ instead of believers. Do they seek honor (ʿizza) with them? Then surely all honor belongs to Allah."
Plain meaning
A direct rebuke of the hypocrites: those who outwardly profess belief but whose strategic loyalty lies with the disbelievers — especially in seeking social, political, or material advancement. The verse asks the piercing question: do you seek honor with them? Then asserts: real ʿizza (honor, dignity, power) belongs to Allah entirely. Aligning with what is opposed to Allah for honor is a strategic delusion.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the psychology of hypocrisy — outward belief paired with hidden allegiance to the dominant cultural-political order; (2) the foundational concept that true honor comes from Allah, not from social proximity to power; (3) the warning against compromising religion to gain favor with the powerful — historically applied to Muslims who feigned belief for political advantage and to modern Muslims who downplay faith for social advancement.
Related verses
Q 35:10 (whoever desires honor — all honor belongs to Allah). Q 63:8 (honor belongs to Allah and to His Messenger and to the believers). Q 5:51 (do not take Jews and Christians as awliyāʾ in war contexts).
Quran 4:141
No path for kuffār over believers Sovereignty
... فَاللَّهُ يَحْكُمُ بَيْنَكُمْ يَوْمَ الْقِيَامَةِ ۗ وَلَن يَجْعَلَ اللَّهُ لِلْكَافِرِينَ عَلَى الْمُؤْمِنِينَ سَبِيلًا
"... Allah will judge between you on the Day of Resurrection. And Allah will never give the disbelievers a path (sabīl) over the believers."
Plain meaning
A theological-political statement. Ultimately, judgment between believers and rejecters is in Allah's hands on the Day of Resurrection. In the meantime, Allah promises that kuffār will never gain a permanent sabīl (path / authoritative dominance) over believers as believers. Worldly defeat may happen — but it is never the final verdict, and it never legitimately transfers authority over Islam to those who reject it.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse in two contexts: (1) guardianship in marriage — a non-Muslim cannot be the marriage guardian (walī) of a Muslim woman; (2) inheritance — classical view: a non-Muslim does not inherit from a Muslim relative (and vice versa, though some schools differ). Modern jurists (esp. in minority contexts) emphasize the verse is about ultimate, not worldly, authority — Muslims living under non-Muslim legal systems is not a violation, it is a circumstance.
Where the schools differ
Marriage guardianAll four schools: non-Muslim cannot be walī for a Muslim woman's marriage. If the father is non-Muslim, guardianship passes to the next Muslim male relative or to the judge.
Inheritance across religionMajority (Ḥanafī/Mālikī/Shāfiʿī/Ḥanbalī): no inheritance between Muslim and non-Muslim. Ibn Taymiyya and some others: a Muslim may inherit from a non-Muslim relative (but not vice versa) — minority view.
Related verses
Q 47:35 (do not lose heart and call for peace when you have the upper hand — Allah is with you). Q 22:40 (had Allah not driven back some by others, monasteries and mosques would have been destroyed). Q 5:57 (do not take as protectors those who mock religion).
Quran 4:147
Allah doesn't need to punish Gratitude and faith
مَّا يَفْعَلُ اللَّهُ بِعَذَابِكُمْ إِن شَكَرْتُمْ وَآمَنتُمْ ۚ وَكَانَ اللَّهُ شَاكِرًا عَلِيمًا
"What does Allah gain from your punishment if you are grateful and believing? Allah is appreciative (shākir), all-knowing."
Plain meaning
A theologically remarkable verse. Allah gains nothing from punishing anyone — He is not vengeful or in need of human suffering. Punishment exists as the just response to persistent rejection, not as Allah's pleasure or need. Two qualities — gratitude and faith — completely remove the basis for punishment. The verse names Allah Himself as shākir — appreciative — using the very word humans use for human gratitude.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the theological corrective to punitive images of religion — Allah's mercy precedes His wrath; (2) the principle that gratitude is itself protective — the grateful believer cannot, in essence, be a candidate for divine wrath; (3) one of the most beautiful uses of Allah's name al-Shakūr (the Appreciative) — Allah responds to even small sincere gratitude with great increase. Pairs powerfully with Q 39:53 (no despair of mercy).
Related verses
Q 14:7 (if you are grateful, I will increase you). Q 35:30 (He gives them their reward in full and increases them from His grace — He is forgiving, appreciative). Q 64:17 (if you lend Allah a beautiful loan, He will multiply it — Allah is appreciative, forbearing).
Quran 4:148
Public complaint Right of the wronged
لَّا يُحِبُّ اللَّهُ الْجَهْرَ بِالسُّوءِ مِنَ الْقَوْلِ إِلَّا مَن ظُلِمَ ۚ وَكَانَ اللَّهُ سَمِيعًا عَلِيمًا
"Allah does not love the public utterance of evil speech — except by one who has been wronged. Allah is all-hearing, all-knowing."
Plain meaning
A short verse with significant implications for the ethics of complaint. The general rule: do not speak ill of others publicly. Exception: the one who has been wronged may speak about the wrong done to them — even if doing so identifies and exposes the wrongdoer. This is the Quran's grounding of the legal right to publicly accuse a wrongdoer.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the wronged person is exempt from the general prohibition on backbiting (ghība) — they may name their abuser, fraudster, oppressor, etc., publicly when seeking redress; (2) public testimony in court, complaints to authorities, warning others about a dangerous person — all flow from this verse; (3) however, the exemption applies only to the actual wrong — one may not use being wronged as license to speak ill of unrelated matters. Modern application: whistleblowing, abuse-survivor testimony, and consumer reviews of fraudulent businesses all fall within this verse's permission.
Related verses
Q 49:12 (do not backbite one another — general prohibition). Q 42:41–42 (no blame on those who defend themselves after wrongdoing — blame is on those who oppress). Q 4:135 (be witnesses in justice, even against yourselves).
Quran 4:176
Kalāla Sibling inheritance
يَسْتَفْتُونَكَ قُلِ اللَّهُ يُفْتِيكُمْ فِي الْكَلَالَةِ ۚ إِنِ امْرُؤٌ هَلَكَ لَيْسَ لَهُ وَلَدٌ وَلَهُ أُخْتٌ فَلَهَا نِصْفُ مَا تَرَكَ ۚ وَهُوَ يَرِثُهَا إِن لَّمْ يَكُن لَّهَا وَلَدٌ ۚ فَإِن كَانَتَا اثْنَتَيْنِ فَلَهُمَا الثُّلُثَانِ مِمَّا تَرَكَ ۚ وَإِن كَانُوا إِخْوَةً رِّجَالًا وَنِسَاءً فَلِلذَّكَرِ مِثْلُ حَظِّ الْأُنثَيَيْنِ
"They ask you for a ruling. Say: Allah gives a ruling about kalāla (one who dies with no children and no parents). If a man dies childless and has a sister, she gets half of what he left; he inherits from her if she has no child. If there are two sisters, they get two-thirds. If there are siblings — men and women — the male's share is that of two females."
Plain meaning
The final verse of An-Nisāʾ, closing the inheritance section. Specifies the share of full siblings or paternal half-siblings when the deceased left no parents and no children (kalāla). Distinct from Q 4:12 which covers maternal half-siblings (always 1/6 or 1/3 shared). This verse — full/paternal siblings — uses the 2:1 male/female ratio.
Majority view
All four schools agree on the breakdown: Q 4:12 = maternal half-siblings (regardless of gender, share equally); Q 4:176 = full siblings and paternal half-siblings (2:1 ratio). This was the last verse on inheritance to be revealed — ʿUmar said the Prophet ﷺ "would have liked it more detailed" but it was sufficient. The science of farāʾiḍ (inheritance) is built on Q 4:11, 4:12, and 4:176 as its three foundational verses.
Where the schools differ
Order of priorityFull siblings inherit before paternal half-siblings if both exist. Maternal half-siblings inherit alongside both, with their own fixed share.
When grandfather is aliveDisagreement on whether siblings inherit alongside grandfather or are excluded. Abū Bakr: grandfather excludes siblings entirely (Ḥanafī follows). ʿUmar/ʿAlī/Zayd: siblings inherit with grandfather (Mālikī/Shāfiʿī/Ḥanbalī follow this).
Related verses
Q 4:11 (children and parents shares). Q 4:12 (spouse and maternal half-sibling shares). Q 4:7 (foundational right of women to inherit).
Quran 5:1
Contracts Lawful animals Iḥrām
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا أَوْفُوا بِالْعُقُودِ ۚ أُحِلَّتْ لَكُم بَهِيمَةُ الْأَنْعَامِ إِلَّا مَا يُتْلَىٰ عَلَيْكُمْ غَيْرَ مُحِلِّي الصَّيْدِ وَأَنتُمْ حُرُمٌ ۗ إِنَّ اللَّهَ يَحْكُمُ مَا يُرِيدُ
"O believers, fulfill your contracts (ʿuqūd). Lawful to you are the grazing animals — except what is recited to you — but you may not consider hunting lawful while you are in iḥrām. Allah decrees what He wills."
Plain meaning
The opening verse of Sūrat al-Māʾida — the last surah revealed in full, and the most legally dense after Al-Baqara. Three rulings in one verse: (1) fulfill all contracts — the broadest possible category, covering sales, marriage, treaties with non-Muslims, and the foundational covenant with Allah; (2) grazing animals (sheep, cattle, camels, goats, and similar) are lawful by default; (3) hunting in iḥrām (pilgrim's sacred state) is forbidden.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for the universal principle: auf bi-l-ʿuqūd — keep your agreements. The Prophet ﷺ: "The Muslims are bound by their conditions, except a condition that makes lawful what is forbidden or forbids what is lawful" (Tirmidhī). On hunting in iḥrām: kaffāra is required — Q 5:95 specifies an equivalent animal sacrificed at the Kaʿba, or feeding the poor, or fasting an equivalent.
Where the schools differ
Scope of "contracts"All schools: any agreement that is not in itself sinful must be honored. Ḥanafī especially strict on commercial contracts. Mālikī famous for honoring even informal commercial promises if relied upon.
"Grazing animal" exceptionsQ 5:3 specifies the excluded categories: carrion, blood, pork, etc. All schools agree on the core list.
Hunting in iḥrāmAll schools: forbidden. Disagreements on kaffāra are technical (Mālikī: pilgrim chooses among the three; Ḥanafī: based on land-based animals only).
Related verses
Q 16:91 (fulfill the covenant of Allah when you have made one). Q 17:34 (the covenant — surely the covenant is questioned about). Q 5:3 (forbidden food items). Q 5:95–96 (penalties for hunting in iḥrām; sea-hunt is permitted).
Quran 5:2
Cooperation in good No cooperation in sin
... وَتَعَاوَنُوا عَلَى الْبِرِّ وَالتَّقْوَىٰ ۖ وَلَا تَعَاوَنُوا عَلَى الْإِثْمِ وَالْعُدْوَانِ ۚ وَاتَّقُوا اللَّهَ ۖ إِنَّ اللَّهَ شَدِيدُ الْعِقَابِ
"... Cooperate in righteousness and God-consciousness, but do not cooperate in sin and aggression. Be mindful of Allah — Allah is severe in punishment."
Plain meaning
A foundational verse on the ethics of collective action. The verse is binary: every cooperative act is either birr/taqwā (righteousness/God-mindedness) or ithm/ʿudwān (sin/aggression). There is no neutral category. The verse also strongly implies: someone may not be the direct doer of a sin, but assisting it carries its own weight.
Majority view
All four schools cite this as the basis for the legal principle of iʿāna ʿalā l-ithm — assisting in sin is itself prohibited. From it derive: prohibition of selling alcohol/pork to anyone, even non-Muslims (Ḥanafī differs partially); prohibition of riba transactions even as facilitator/witness; prohibition of working for businesses whose primary income is haram. Positively: it grounds the legitimacy of cooperative institutions — mosques, charities, mutual aid societies, scholarly councils.
Where the schools differ
Selling haram to non-MuslimsMajority (Mālikī/Shāfiʿī/Ḥanbalī): forbidden, since alcohol/pork are universally haram for sale by Muslims. Ḥanafī: a Muslim may sell to a dhimmī (non-Muslim under Muslim governance) what is lawful for them — minority view, narrowly applied.
Indirect assistanceAll schools: direct facilitation of clear sin is prohibited. Remote/incidental connection (e.g., a Muslim baker selling bread to someone who happens to drink wine with it) is permitted.
Related verses
Q 3:104 (community calling to good). Q 9:71 (believing men and women command good and forbid evil — mutual support). Q 60:1 (do not take My enemies and yours as allies). Q 28:17 (Mūsā's prayer: I will never be a helper to wrongdoers).
Quran 5:3
Forbidden foods Religion perfected
حُرِّمَتْ عَلَيْكُمُ الْمَيْتَةُ وَالدَّمُ وَلَحْمُ الْخِنزِيرِ وَمَا أُهِلَّ لِغَيْرِ اللَّهِ بِهِ وَالْمُنْخَنِقَةُ وَالْمَوْقُوذَةُ وَالْمُتَرَدِّيَةُ وَالنَّطِيحَةُ وَمَا أَكَلَ السَّبُعُ إِلَّا مَا ذَكَّيْتُمْ وَمَا ذُبِحَ عَلَى النُّصُبِ ... الْيَوْمَ أَكْمَلْتُ لَكُمْ دِينَكُمْ وَأَتْمَمْتُ عَلَيْكُمْ نِعْمَتِي وَرَضِيتُ لَكُمُ الْإِسْلَامَ دِينًا ۚ فَمَنِ اضْطُرَّ فِي مَخْمَصَةٍ غَيْرَ مُتَجَانِفٍ لِّإِثْمٍ فَإِنَّ اللَّهَ غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ
"Forbidden to you are: carrion, blood, pork, that on which other than Allah's name was invoked, the strangled, the beaten to death, the fallen, the gored, and what a wild beast ate — unless you slaughter it (while alive), and what was sacrificed on stone altars ... Today I have perfected your religion for you, completed My favor upon you, and chosen Islam as your religion. Whoever is compelled by hunger, with no intent toward sin — Allah is forgiving, merciful."
Plain meaning
The most comprehensive food-prohibition verse. Eight categories of forbidden meat: (1) carrion, (2) blood, (3) pork, (4) non-Islamic ritual slaughter, (5–9) various forms of unslaughtered death (strangled, beaten, fallen, gored, attacked). The verse then contains the announcement of religious completion — revealed on the Day of ʿArafa during the Farewell Pilgrimage. Closes with the compulsion principle (iḍṭirār): one starving may eat what is otherwise forbidden, without sinful intent.
Majority view
All four schools: this list is exhaustive of the meat prohibitions in the Quran. Q 6:145 lists four (carrion, blood, pork, non-Islamic-slaughter); this verse expands the categories and is taken as final. Tadhkiya (proper Islamic slaughter — cutting throat, draining blood, with Allah's name) reverses some of the prohibitions: if you reach a wounded animal still alive and properly slaughter it, it becomes lawful. Iḍṭirār rule: in absolute hunger, even pork or carrion may be eaten in the minimum amount needed for survival.
Where the schools differ
Bismillāh on slaughterḤanafī/Mālikī: required and forgetting invalidates — but not deliberate omission lightly. Shāfiʿī: highly recommended, not invalidating. Ḥanbalī: required; deliberate omission invalidates the meat.
Meat of People of the BookAll schools (per Q 5:5): lawful if the slaughterer was a Jew or Christian and slaughter was carried out as per their tradition. Modern industrial slaughter raises questions — many scholars hold mechanical/electrical stunning before throat-cutting can be acceptable if the animal is alive when cut; others reject it.
Pre-stunned/non-bismillah industrial meatConservative scholars: only certified halal-slaughtered meat is permissible. More permissive: kosher meat or Christian-slaughtered meat with bismillāh said before eating is acceptable per Q 5:5.
Related verses
Q 2:173 (the four basic prohibitions). Q 6:145 (the same four, named directly). Q 16:115 (the same — repeated). Q 5:96 (sea hunting and food is lawful even in iḥrām).
Quran 5:5
Food of People of the Book Marriage to chaste kitābiyya
الْيَوْمَ أُحِلَّ لَكُمُ الطَّيِّبَاتُ ۖ وَطَعَامُ الَّذِينَ أُوتُوا الْكِتَابَ حِلٌّ لَّكُمْ وَطَعَامُكُمْ حِلٌّ لَّهُمْ ۖ وَالْمُحْصَنَاتُ مِنَ الْمُؤْمِنَاتِ وَالْمُحْصَنَاتُ مِنَ الَّذِينَ أُوتُوا الْكِتَابَ مِن قَبْلِكُمْ إِذَا آتَيْتُمُوهُنَّ أُجُورَهُنَّ مُحْصِنِينَ غَيْرَ مُسَافِحِينَ
"Today the wholesome things have been made lawful for you. The food of those given the Scripture is lawful for you, and your food is lawful for them. (Lawful for you are) chaste believing women and chaste women from those given the Scripture before you — when you have given them their dowries, in honest wedlock, not in fornication."
Plain meaning
Two important permissions, revealed alongside the prohibitions of 5:3 — both sides need to be stated. (1) Food of Jews and Christians is mutually lawful; (2) marriage to chaste Jewish or Christian women is permitted, conditional on paying mahr and on honest marriage (not exploitation).
Majority view
All four schools accept both permissions. Food: meat slaughtered by a Jew or Christian according to their religious tradition is halal — provided the animal is one of the lawful species. Marriage: a Muslim man may marry a chaste Jewish or Christian woman; the reverse (Muslim woman marrying non-Muslim man) is universally prohibited per Q 2:221. Several major Companions (ʿUmar, Ibn ʿUmar, Ibn ʿAbbās in some narrations) discouraged the practice even though they accepted its legality, especially in Muslim-minority contexts where the woman's faith might be lost or children might not be raised Muslim.
Where the schools differ
All four schoolsPermissible. The condition is that she be muḥṣana (chaste, of established sexual morality). Polytheists (mushrikāt) remain off-limits — only People of the Book qualify.
CaveatsSome scholars say this permission applies only when the man can preserve his and his children's religion. In Muslim-minority lands or when faith is weak, scholars often discourage it. Several Companions distinguished between permission and advisability.
Modern reformistSome argue the reverse (Muslim woman + kitābī man) should also be permitted under modern conditions. The four schools all reject this — including modern mainstream scholars — as direct violation of Q 2:221 and other verses.
Related verses
Q 2:221 (do not marry polytheist women / do not marry off your women to polytheist men, until they believe). Q 60:10 (believing women coming from disbelievers — they are not lawful for the disbelievers). Q 24:3 (the fornicator marries only a fornicator or polytheist).
Quran 5:6
Wuḍū Ghusl Tayammum
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا إِذَا قُمْتُمْ إِلَى الصَّلَاةِ فَاغْسِلُوا وُجُوهَكُمْ وَأَيْدِيَكُمْ إِلَى الْمَرَافِقِ وَامْسَحُوا بِرُءُوسِكُمْ وَأَرْجُلَكُمْ إِلَى الْكَعْبَيْنِ ۚ وَإِن كُنتُمْ جُنُبًا فَاطَّهَّرُوا ۚ وَإِن كُنتُم مَّرْضَىٰ أَوْ عَلَىٰ سَفَرٍ ... فَلَمْ تَجِدُوا مَاءً فَتَيَمَّمُوا صَعِيدًا طَيِّبًا
"O believers, when you rise for prayer: wash your faces, your hands up to the elbows, wipe your heads, and wash your feet up to the ankles. If you are in a state of major impurity, purify yourselves (with ghusl). If you are ill or on a journey... and find no water, perform tayammum with pure earth."
Plain meaning
The Quran's most detailed verse on ritual purification. Wuḍū: wash face, wash arms to elbows, wipe head, wash feet to ankles. Ghusl: required after major impurity (intercourse, menstruation, postnatal bleeding). Tayammum: dry purification with clean earth when water is unavailable or harmful. The verse closes: "Allah does not wish to put you in difficulty — He wishes to purify you and complete His favor."
Majority view
All four schools agree on the obligatory acts of wuḍū: (1) wash face, (2) wash arms to and including elbows, (3) wipe head, (4) wash feet to and including ankles. Each must be done in order, without long delay between. Disagreements are on additional sunnah elements (rinsing mouth/nose, washing hands first, etc.) and on the extent of "wiping" the head.
Where the schools differ
ḤanafīWiping head: minimum one-quarter of the head. Order between obligatory acts not required (preferred but not invalidating). Niyya (intention): not obligatory — washing for cleanliness counts if subsequent prayer follows.
MālikīWiping head: entire head. Order obligatory. Niyya obligatory. Continuity (muwālāt) obligatory — no long pauses.
ShāfiʿīWiping head: any small portion (even a few hairs). Order obligatory. Niyya obligatory.
ḤanbalīWiping head: entire head, including ears. Order and continuity obligatory. Niyya obligatory.
Twelver ShiaReads arjulakum in genitive case (wiped, not washed) — feet are wiped, not washed. All Sunni schools read it accusative (washed) per consensus Companion practice.
Related verses
Q 4:43 (parallel statement of tayammum). Q 2:222 (do not approach women during menstruation; menstruation requires ghusl). Q 9:108 (mosque founded on taqwā — men love to purify themselves).
Quran 5:8
Justice with enemies Taqwā
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا كُونُوا قَوَّامِينَ لِلَّهِ شُهَدَاءَ بِالْقِسْطِ ۖ وَلَا يَجْرِمَنَّكُمْ شَنَآنُ قَوْمٍ عَلَىٰ أَلَّا تَعْدِلُوا ۚ اعْدِلُوا هُوَ أَقْرَبُ لِلتَّقْوَىٰ ۖ وَاتَّقُوا اللَّهَ ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ خَبِيرٌ بِمَا تَعْمَلُونَ
"O believers, be steadfast for Allah — witnesses in justice. Let not the hatred of a people prevent you from being just. Be just — that is closer to God-consciousness. Be mindful of Allah — Allah is aware of what you do."
Plain meaning
The companion verse to Q 4:135 — but addressing a different axis. Where 4:135 commands justice even against yourselves and family, this verse commands justice even toward enemies and those you hate. Personal animus is the most common route to injustice — and Allah lists this as one of the things closest to taqwā.
Majority view
All four schools cite this as the foundational verse on the law of war, treatment of non-Muslim litigants, and foreign relations. Even in armed conflict: prisoners must not be tortured, civilians not targeted, agreements with the enemy honored. In courts: a Muslim judge ruling between two non-Muslims, or between a Muslim and a non-Muslim, must apply the same standard to both. Ibn Taymiyya: "Allah supports the just state even if non-Muslim, and overthrows the unjust state even if Muslim."
Related verses
Q 4:135 (justice even against yourselves and kin). Q 60:8 (Allah does not forbid you from showing kindness to those who do not fight you). Q 16:90 (Allah commands justice, excellence, and giving to kin). Q 5:42 (if you judge between them, judge with justice).
Quran 5:32
Sanctity of life Saving life
مِنْ أَجْلِ ذَٰلِكَ كَتَبْنَا عَلَىٰ بَنِي إِسْرَائِيلَ أَنَّهُ مَن قَتَلَ نَفْسًا بِغَيْرِ نَفْسٍ أَوْ فَسَادٍ فِي الْأَرْضِ فَكَأَنَّمَا قَتَلَ النَّاسَ جَمِيعًا وَمَنْ أَحْيَاهَا فَكَأَنَّمَا أَحْيَا النَّاسَ جَمِيعًا
"For this reason We decreed for the Children of Israel: whoever kills a soul — except for (killing) a soul or for corruption in the land — it is as if he killed all mankind. And whoever saves a life, it is as if he had saved all mankind."
Plain meaning
A theologically and ethically immense verse, framed as a citation from earlier scripture but now decreed on all believers through this Quran. Killing one soul = killing all humanity in moral weight. Saving one soul = saving all humanity. The verse acknowledges two limited exceptions: (1) lawful retaliation (qiṣāṣ for an established murder), (2) execution of one spreading fasād (open corruption/lethal banditry — see 5:33).
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse: (1) for the prohibition of vigilante killing — only the legitimate state may carry out qiṣāṣ; (2) for the principle that murder of a single innocent is equivalent in moral gravity to genocide; (3) for the immense reward of saving life — used to justify doctors, rescue workers, charity supporting health, and (in modern law) organ donation. This is one of the most-cited Quranic verses globally, including by non-Muslims.
Related verses
Q 4:92–93 (manslaughter and murder). Q 6:151 (do not kill a soul Allah has forbidden — except by right). Q 17:33 (do not kill a soul except in right). Q 5:33 (the punishment for those who wage war on Allah and His Messenger and spread corruption).
Quran 5:33–34
Ḥirāba Armed robbery / terror Repentance clause
إِنَّمَا جَزَاءُ الَّذِينَ يُحَارِبُونَ اللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُ وَيَسْعَوْنَ فِي الْأَرْضِ فَسَادًا أَن يُقَتَّلُوا أَوْ يُصَلَّبُوا أَوْ تُقَطَّعَ أَيْدِيهِمْ وَأَرْجُلُهُم مِّنْ خِلَافٍ أَوْ يُنفَوْا مِنَ الْأَرْضِ ۚ ... إِلَّا الَّذِينَ تَابُوا مِن قَبْلِ أَن تَقْدِرُوا عَلَيْهِمْ
"The recompense for those who wage war against Allah and His Messenger and strive for corruption in the land is that they be killed, or crucified, or have their hands and feet cut off from opposite sides, or be exiled from the land ... except those who repent before you overpower them — know that Allah is forgiving, merciful."
Plain meaning
This is the verse of ḥirāba — what we'd call "highway robbery" or "armed terror" in modern terms: organized violent crime that disrupts public safety. The verse lists four graduated punishments and (per majority view) each is applied based on what the criminal actually did: killed + took property → death; only took property → cutting; only terrorized → exile/imprisonment. The verse closes with a striking amnesty clause: if the perpetrator repents before being apprehended, the ḥadd punishment is waived (though private rights — money owed to victims — remain).
Majority view
All four schools agree this verse covers a category distinct from ordinary theft (5:38). Ḥirāba requires: (1) a group or armed individual, (2) public road or open area, (3) use of weapons or threat, (4) intent to deprive or terrorize. Punishment is mandatory if proven. The repentance clause is universal — turning oneself in before capture commutes the ḥadd. Modern Muslim jurists apply this verse to terrorism, organized crime, and serious public-safety crimes.
Where the schools differ
ḤanafīThe judge selects the punishment based on severity — not a mechanical correspondence to the act. Allows wide discretion.
MālikīStrong "graduation" view: punishments correspond directly to the crime (killed → death; only took → cutting; only frightened → exile).
Shāfiʿī/ḤanbalīCloser to Mālikī's graduated reading. Shāfiʿī adds detailed conditions on the form of the punishment.
Related verses
Q 5:32 (sanctity of life — the immediately preceding verse). Q 5:38 (ordinary theft — different punishment). Q 49:9 (against those who transgress upon believers — fight them).
Quran 5:35
Wasīla Striving
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا اتَّقُوا اللَّهَ وَابْتَغُوا إِلَيْهِ الْوَسِيلَةَ وَجَاهِدُوا فِي سَبِيلِهِ لَعَلَّكُمْ تُفْلِحُونَ
"O believers, be mindful of Allah, seek the means (wasīla) of nearness to Him, and strive in His way — that you may succeed."
Plain meaning
Three commands: (1) taqwā — God-consciousness; (2) seek a wasīla — a means of drawing closer to Allah; (3) jihād in His way — strive earnestly. Wasīla here refers to good deeds, sincere worship, repentance — anything Allah has prescribed as a way to His pleasure.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the principle that nearness to Allah is achieved through specific acts He has prescribed, not through arbitrary innovations; (2) the obligation to perform obligatory acts and increase in voluntary ones; (3) historical Sunni rejection of using created beings (deceased saints, righteous individuals) as intermediaries — wasīla is the act, not the person. The Prophet ﷺ asked Allah to grant him the highest station (al-wasīla) in Paradise.
Related verses
Q 17:57 (those they invoke also seek means of nearness to their Lord). Q 96:19 (prostrate and draw near). Q 24:30 (Allah is Knower of what you do). Q 29:69 (those who strive in Us, We will surely guide them to Our ways).
Quran 5:38
Theft (sariqa) Cutting of hand Ḥadd
وَالسَّارِقُ وَالسَّارِقَةُ فَاقْطَعُوا أَيْدِيَهُمَا جَزَاءً بِمَا كَسَبَا نَكَالًا مِّنَ اللَّهِ ۗ وَاللَّهُ عَزِيزٌ حَكِيمٌ
"As for the thief — male or female — cut their hand: a recompense for what they have earned, an exemplary punishment from Allah. Allah is mighty, wise."
Plain meaning
The verse legislating the ḥadd for theft. Important: this is a ḥadd — meaning the state, not the victim, executes it; meaning it is only applied when extremely strict conditions are met; meaning it is intended as a public nakāl (exemplary deterrent), not retribution for personal grievance.
Majority view
All four schools impose extremely strict conditions on application: (1) the goods must reach a niṣāb (minimum value — varies: Ḥanafī 10 dirhams ≈ small amount of silver; Shāfiʿī/Mālikī/Ḥanbalī much less); (2) goods must be taken from secured storage (ḥirz) — not picked up from open street; (3) thief must be adult, sane, knowing it was forbidden; (4) no doubt in evidence; (5) no need-driven theft. ʿUmar suspended the punishment during the famine year. Most cases historically were handled under taʿzīr (discretionary punishment), not ḥadd.
Where the schools differ
Niṣāb (minimum)Ḥanafī: 10 dirhams (relatively higher bar). Mālikī/Shāfiʿī/Ḥanbalī: 1/4 dīnār or 3 dirhams (much lower bar).
"Hand"All schools: the right hand cut at the wrist on first conviction. Repeat offense: left foot. Third offense: differ — Mālikī/Shāfiʿī: again the left hand; Ḥanafī/Ḥanbalī: imprisonment, no further cutting.
"Need theft"All schools: theft driven by genuine survival need is not subject to ḥadd. ʿUmar's suspension during famine is the classic precedent.
Related verses
Q 5:39 (repentance after theft — Allah accepts). Q 12:76–77 (the false-theft narrative in the story of Yūsuf — establishing the historical principle of cutting). Q 4:29 (not consuming wealth wrongfully).
Quran 5:42–45
Qiṣāṣ Torah's law Charity for the giver
وَكَتَبْنَا عَلَيْهِمْ فِيهَا أَنَّ النَّفْسَ بِالنَّفْسِ وَالْعَيْنَ بِالْعَيْنِ وَالْأَنفَ بِالْأَنفِ وَالْأُذُنَ بِالْأُذُنِ وَالسِّنَّ بِالسِّنِّ وَالْجُرُوحَ قِصَاصٌ ۚ فَمَن تَصَدَّقَ بِهِ فَهُوَ كَفَّارَةٌ لَّهُ
"We prescribed for them in (the Torah): a life for a life, an eye for an eye, a nose for a nose, an ear for an ear, a tooth for a tooth, and equal retribution for wounds. Whoever forgoes it as charity, it shall be expiation for him."
Plain meaning
The Quran cites the Torah's law of qiṣāṣ (equal retaliation) — and adopts the same principle for Muslims. The structure: life for life, eye for eye, etc. But the verse concludes with what was missing in legalistic readings of the Torah: forgiveness is described as a kaffāra for the forgiver — the act of letting go is itself spiritually transforming.
Majority view
All four schools accept this verse as confirming qiṣāṣ for Muslims while emphasizing the preferability of mercy. The victim's family always has the right to: (1) demand qiṣāṣ, or (2) accept diya (blood-money), or (3) forgive outright. The Prophet ﷺ encouraged the third whenever possible. The verse also undergirds the principle that qiṣāṣ for wounds (not just death) is a possibility — though in practice rarely applied due to difficulty of exact equivalence.
Where the schools differ
Cross-religion qiṣāṣḤanafī: a Muslim is killed for killing a non-Muslim under Muslim protection. Mālikī/Shāfiʿī/Ḥanbalī: only diya, not qiṣāṣ, applies cross-religion — minority view that many modern jurists are revising.
Wound qiṣāṣAll schools: theoretically permissible if exact equivalence can be assured; in practice replaced by diya in most cases since exact equivalence (e.g., breaking a specific bone identically) is rarely achievable.
Related verses
Q 2:178–179 (qiṣāṣ established for Muslims; "life for you in qiṣāṣ"). Q 16:126 (if you punish, punish proportionally; but patience is better). Q 42:40 (recompense for evil is its equal — but whoever forgives and reconciles, his reward is with Allah).
Quran 5:48
Quran as criterion Religious diversity Race in good
وَأَنزَلْنَا إِلَيْكَ الْكِتَابَ بِالْحَقِّ مُصَدِّقًا لِّمَا بَيْنَ يَدَيْهِ مِنَ الْكِتَابِ وَمُهَيْمِنًا عَلَيْهِ ۖ فَاحْكُم بَيْنَهُم بِمَا أَنزَلَ اللَّهُ ... لِكُلٍّ جَعَلْنَا مِنكُمْ شِرْعَةً وَمِنْهَاجًا ۚ وَلَوْ شَاءَ اللَّهُ لَجَعَلَكُمْ أُمَّةً وَاحِدَةً ۖ ... فَاسْتَبِقُوا الْخَيْرَاتِ
"We sent down to you the Book in truth — confirming the scriptures before it and as a guardian (muhaymin) over them. So judge between them by what Allah has revealed ... For each of you We have appointed a law (shirʿa) and a way (minhāj). Had Allah willed, He would have made you one community, but (He willed otherwise) to test you in what He has given you. So race to do good."
Plain meaning
Three immense statements in one verse: (1) the Quran is confirming earlier scriptures, but also muhaymin over them — meaning it preserves what was true and corrects what was distorted; (2) different religious communities have different shariʿa and method — diversity itself is Allah's will; (3) the response is not lamenting differences but racing toward good (istibāq al-khayrāt).
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the legal authority of the Quran over earlier scriptures — earlier laws are valid only insofar as the Quran confirms them; (2) inter-religious justice — judging fairly even when applying Islamic law to non-Muslims; (3) the principle of religious pluralism within the framework of one truth — different paths to good are accepted but the standard is one. Modern usage: this verse is heavily cited for inter-faith ethics, cooperation in social good, and rejection of forced conversion.
Related verses
Q 2:256 (no compulsion in religion). Q 49:13 (made you peoples and tribes to know one another). Q 22:67 (for each community We have ordained a rite they should follow). Q 5:69 (those who believe and the Jews, Christians, Sabians — whoever believes and does good, no fear).
Quran 5:51
Awliyāʾ in war Political allegiance
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا لَا تَتَّخِذُوا الْيَهُودَ وَالنَّصَارَىٰ أَوْلِيَاءَ ۘ بَعْضُهُمْ أَوْلِيَاءُ بَعْضٍ ۚ وَمَن يَتَوَلَّهُم مِّنكُمْ فَإِنَّهُ مِنْهُمْ
"O believers, do not take the Jews and Christians as awliyāʾ — they are awliyāʾ of one another. Whoever among you takes them as such, is one of them."
Plain meaning
A heavily contextual verse. Awliyāʾ is plural of walī — meaning "protector, patron, military ally, intimate confidant". The verse was revealed during armed conflict with Madinan and surrounding Jewish/Christian tribes who had broken treaties or sided militarily with Quraysh. It forbids treating them as military and political confidants in the context of war against the Muslim community — not as friends, neighbors, business partners, or in-laws (which Q 5:5 explicitly permits regarding marriage and food).
Majority view
All four classical and modern schools: this verse does not prohibit ordinary friendship, business, neighborly relations, or family ties (recall Q 5:5 explicitly permits marriage). It prohibits military and political allegiance against fellow Muslims — taking non-Muslim states or factions as one's strategic protectors at the expense of the Muslim community. Q 60:8 explicitly permits kindness and just dealing with non-Muslims who do not fight you. Misreading 5:51 as banning friendship is a common error — corrected by reading the surrounding verses and other Quranic verses.
Related verses
Q 60:8 (Allah does not forbid you from kindness and justice to those who have not fought you). Q 5:5 (lawful to marry chaste women of People of the Book). Q 3:118 (do not take confidants from outside who plot harm). Q 5:57 (do not take as awliyāʾ those who mock your religion).
Quran 5:54
Replacement community Loving and beloved
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا مَن يَرْتَدَّ مِنكُمْ عَن دِينِهِ فَسَوْفَ يَأْتِي اللَّهُ بِقَوْمٍ يُحِبُّهُمْ وَيُحِبُّونَهُ أَذِلَّةٍ عَلَى الْمُؤْمِنِينَ أَعِزَّةٍ عَلَى الْكَافِرِينَ يُجَاهِدُونَ فِي سَبِيلِ اللَّهِ وَلَا يَخَافُونَ لَوْمَةَ لَائِمٍ
"O believers, whoever among you turns away from his religion — Allah will bring a people whom He loves and who love Him — humble before the believers, dignified before the disbelievers, striving in Allah's way, and not fearing the blame of any blamer."
Plain meaning
If a generation of Muslims abandons the faith, Allah is not bound to that generation. He will replace them with another community of five named qualities: (1) Allah loves them; (2) they love Allah; (3) humble with fellow believers; (4) dignified before disbelievers (the opposite expected by the world); (5) striving in Allah's way without fear of social criticism (lawmat lāʾim).
Majority view
