All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the principle that Islam itself is not dependent on any one generation — if some abandon it, others rise; (2) the qualities of true Muslim community: internal humility, external dignity, courage in following Allah's command, freedom from peer pressure; (3) "not fearing the blame of any blamer" — the verse explicitly licenses moral nonconformity when truth demands it. Abū Bakr is famously associated with this verse in his decisiveness during the apostasy wars.
Related verses
Q 9:38–39 (if you do not go forth, Allah will replace you with another people). Q 47:38 (you are those called to spend in Allah's cause — if you turn away, He will replace you). Q 5:55 (your walī is only Allah and His Messenger and the believers).
Quran 5:55
True walī Believers as allies
إِنَّمَا وَلِيُّكُمُ اللَّهُ وَرَسُولُهُ وَالَّذِينَ آمَنُوا الَّذِينَ يُقِيمُونَ الصَّلَاةَ وَيُؤْتُونَ الزَّكَاةَ وَهُمْ رَاكِعُونَ
"Your walī (protector / true ally) is only Allah, His Messenger, and those who believe — who establish prayer, give zakat, while bowing (in worship)."
Plain meaning
The positive complement to Q 5:51 (the warning against military allegiance with disbelievers). Real walāya — protection, alliance, and intimate partnership — is bound to three: Allah, the Messenger ﷺ, and believers who pray and give zakat. The triad is non-negotiable: anyone who claims to be your walī but rejects Allah, the Messenger, prayer, or zakat is not truly so.
Majority view
All four Sunni schools take this verse as a general statement: walāya within the Muslim community is the proper sphere of religious and political intimacy. Twelver Shia exegetes link the verse specifically to ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib (citing reports that he gave a ring while in rukūʿ) — a reading rejected by Sunni scholars on grounds of ḥadīth criticism and grammatical considerations. Sunni and Shia both agree the verse establishes Allah, His Messenger, and the believers as the true walīs — they differ on whether one believer in particular is named.
Related verses
Q 5:51 (do not take Jews and Christians as awliyāʾ in war). Q 60:8 (kindness to peaceful non-Muslims). Q 9:71 (believing men and women are awliyāʾ of one another). Q 5:56 (whoever takes Allah, His Messenger, and the believers as walī — they are the victorious party).
Quran 5:67
Prophet's mission Convey fully
يَا أَيُّهَا الرَّسُولُ بَلِّغْ مَا أُنزِلَ إِلَيْكَ مِن رَّبِّكَ ۖ وَإِن لَّمْ تَفْعَلْ فَمَا بَلَّغْتَ رِسَالَتَهُ ۚ وَاللَّهُ يَعْصِمُكَ مِنَ النَّاسِ
"O Messenger, convey what has been sent down to you from your Lord. If you do not, you have not conveyed His message at all. Allah will protect you from the people."
Plain meaning
A direct command to the Prophet ﷺ at a moment of difficulty — likely facing political pressure or threat. The verse is uncompromising: partial conveyance is not conveyance. Withholding any part of the revelation, however inconvenient, voids the entire prophetic duty. The promise of divine protection removes fear as an excuse for silence.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the completeness of the Quran — every revealed instruction was delivered; nothing was hidden by the Prophet ﷺ; (2) the model for scholars and preachers — they must convey what they know to be true, even when unpopular; (3) the rejection of esoteric/secret-knowledge traditions claiming the Prophet ﷺ "hid" knowledge for an elite. Sunni and most Shia exegetes treat this verse as general; some Twelver Shia readings link it specifically to Ghadīr Khumm and the announcement of ʿAlī as successor — a reading Sunni scholarship does not accept.
Related verses
Q 5:99 (the Messenger's duty is only conveyance). Q 16:35 (and is the Messenger responsible for anything but the clear conveyance?). Q 24:54 (if you obey the Messenger, you are rightly guided; on him is only clear conveyance).
Quran 5:69
Believers across traditions Faith + good action
إِنَّ الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا وَالَّذِينَ هَادُوا وَالصَّابِئُونَ وَالنَّصَارَىٰ مَنْ آمَنَ بِاللَّهِ وَالْيَوْمِ الْآخِرِ وَعَمِلَ صَالِحًا فَلَا خَوْفٌ عَلَيْهِمْ وَلَا هُمْ يَحْزَنُونَ
"Those who believe, those who are Jews, the Sabians, and the Christians — whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day and does good — no fear shall come upon them, nor shall they grieve."
Plain meaning
A near-verbatim parallel of Q 2:62 — repeated in this later Madinan surah. The repetition is significant: the principle is not incidental. Sincere faith in Allah and the Last Day combined with good action removes fear and grief. The verse names the historical religious communities of the Prophet's ﷺ time, then specifies the universal qualifications.
Majority view
All four schools, following the same reading as Q 2:62: this verse refers to (1) sincere followers of earlier prophetic traditions before the message of Muhammad ﷺ was clearly conveyed to them; (2) ahl al-fatra — those who never received a clear, intelligible conveyance of the final message. It does not abrogate Q 3:85 ("whoever seeks other than Islam as religion — it will not be accepted"); the two verses operate on different scopes.
Related verses
Q 2:62 (identical principle, slightly different wording). Q 3:85 (whoever seeks other than Islam). Q 22:17 (Allah will judge between Jews, Christians, Sabians, Magians, polytheists on the Day). Q 17:15 (We do not punish until We send a messenger).
Quran 5:87
No forbidding the lawful Anti-asceticism
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا لَا تُحَرِّمُوا طَيِّبَاتِ مَا أَحَلَّ اللَّهُ لَكُمْ وَلَا تَعْتَدُوا ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ لَا يُحِبُّ الْمُعْتَدِينَ
"O believers, do not forbid the good things Allah has made lawful for you, and do not transgress. Allah does not love transgressors."
Plain meaning
Revealed (per ḥadīth) when a group of Companions resolved to permanently fast every day, pray every night, abstain from meat, marriage, and sleep — believing such asceticism would bring them closer to Allah. The Prophet ﷺ was disturbed and reminded them: "I fast and I break the fast; I pray and I sleep; I marry women — whoever turns from my sunnah is not of me" (Bukhārī, Muslim). This verse came down as confirmation.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) prohibition of self-imposed haram — declaring lawful things forbidden as a "religious" gesture is itself a transgression; (2) Islam's rejection of monasticism (rahbāniyya) as a path to closeness with Allah; (3) the principle that lawful enjoyment in moderation is part of religious life, not opposed to it. Modern application: rejecting extreme asceticism, "no music ever / no fun ever" approaches that go beyond what the Sharīʿa requires.
Related verses
Q 7:32 (who has forbidden the adornments of Allah?). Q 5:88 (eat from the good lawful things Allah has provided). Q 57:27 (We did not prescribe monasticism — they invented it). Q 2:172 (eat the good things We have provided you).
Quran 5:89
Oaths Kaffāra
لَا يُؤَاخِذُكُمُ اللَّهُ بِاللَّغْوِ فِي أَيْمَانِكُمْ وَلَٰكِن يُؤَاخِذُكُم بِمَا عَقَّدتُّمُ الْأَيْمَانَ ۖ فَكَفَّارَتُهُ إِطْعَامُ عَشَرَةِ مَسَاكِينَ مِنْ أَوْسَطِ مَا تُطْعِمُونَ أَهْلِيكُمْ أَوْ كِسْوَتُهُمْ أَوْ تَحْرِيرُ رَقَبَةٍ ۖ فَمَن لَّمْ يَجِدْ فَصِيَامُ ثَلَاثَةِ أَيَّامٍ ۚ ذَٰلِكَ كَفَّارَةُ أَيْمَانِكُمْ إِذَا حَلَفْتُمْ
"Allah will not hold you accountable for thoughtless utterance in your oaths, but He holds you accountable for what you have firmly sworn. Its expiation: feeding ten poor people from the average of what you feed your families, or clothing them, or freeing a slave. Whoever cannot — three days of fasting. This is the expiation of your oaths when you swear."
Plain meaning
Two kinds of oaths are distinguished: (1) laghw — careless or rhetorical swearing ("by Allah I just bought it!"), without firm intent — these incur no penalty; (2) ʿaqd al-yamīn — a serious, deliberate oath that one then breaks. Breaking this requires a graduated kaffāra: feed 10 poor OR clothe 10 poor OR free a slave — and if none of these is possible, fast 3 days.
Majority view
All four schools take this verse as exhaustive of the categories of kaffāra. Three options of one's choosing, with fasting as a fallback only if the first three are impossible. The Prophet ﷺ added: "If you swear an oath, then see something better, do the better thing and atone for the oath" (Bukhārī, Muslim). The verse normalizes breaking a wrongly-made oath as a moral duty, with the kaffāra completing the resolution.
Where the schools differ
Quantity per personḤanafī: 1/2 ṣāʿ of wheat per person, or full ṣāʿ of dates/barley (or equivalent meal). Mālikī/Shāfiʿī/Ḥanbalī: 1 mudd of staple food per person (less than Ḥanafī's standard).
Combining optionsḤanafī: must choose ONE of the three (feed, clothe, free); not a mix. Other schools: any of the three fully suffices.
Cash paymentḤanafī: cash equivalent is acceptable. Others: prefer food/clothing in kind.
Related verses
Q 2:225 (Allah does not hold you accountable for thoughtless oaths). Q 16:91 (fulfill the covenant of Allah after you confirm it). Q 66:2 (Allah has prescribed for you the dissolution of your oaths — context: when something better presents itself).
Quran 5:90–91
Alcohol final ban Gambling Idols / divination
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا إِنَّمَا الْخَمْرُ وَالْمَيْسِرُ وَالْأَنصَابُ وَالْأَزْلَامُ رِجْسٌ مِّنْ عَمَلِ الشَّيْطَانِ فَاجْتَنِبُوهُ لَعَلَّكُمْ تُفْلِحُونَ ۚ إِنَّمَا يُرِيدُ الشَّيْطَانُ أَن يُوقِعَ بَيْنَكُمُ الْعَدَاوَةَ وَالْبَغْضَاءَ فِي الْخَمْرِ وَالْمَيْسِرِ ... فَهَلْ أَنتُم مُّنتَهُونَ
"O believers, intoxicants (khamr), gambling (maysir), stone altars, and divination arrows are filth — Satan's handiwork. Avoid them, so you may succeed. Satan wants to throw enmity and hatred among you through intoxicants and gambling ... Will you not now desist?"
Plain meaning
The final stage of the alcohol prohibition, after Q 2:219 (some benefit but greater sin) and Q 4:43 (not in prayer). Now: total abstention. Four things are grouped together: (1) khamr — any intoxicant; (2) maysir — gambling of any form; (3) anṣāb — stone altars of polytheist worship; (4) azlām — divination arrows. All are rijs (filth, spiritual impurity) and Satan's work. The verse ends with a striking rhetorical question: "Will you not now desist?"
Majority view
All four schools: khamr (Arabic for wine but generalized in fiqh to any intoxicating substance) is absolutely forbidden — to consume, sell, transport, present as gift, or earn money from. The Prophet ﷺ: "Allah has cursed khamr — the one who drinks it, the one who pours it, the one who sells it, the one who buys it, the one who carries it..." (Ibn Māja). Maysir includes lottery, casino games, sports betting, and any zero-sum money-on-chance arrangement. Anṣāb and azlām are practices of pre-Islamic divination — modern equivalents (astrology, fortune-telling, occult practices) are similarly forbidden.
Where the schools differ
Definition of khamrMajority (Mālikī/Shāfiʿī/Ḥanbalī): any substance that intoxicates in large amount is haram even in small amount. Ḥanafī (Abū Ḥanīfa's view): only wine of grapes is khamr by definition; other intoxicants are haram only when consumed in intoxicating quantity. The other two main Ḥanafī companions, Abū Yūsuf and Muḥammad al-Shaybānī, sided with the majority — and modern Ḥanafī practice follows them.
Insurance and gamblingConservative scholars: most conventional insurance contains maysir-like elements (uncertainty + zero-sum gambling on event outcome) and is haram. Many modern scholars: mutual/cooperative insurance (takāful) avoids this; commercial insurance for genuine risk-management is permissible by necessity.
Related verses
Q 2:219 (alcohol: some benefit but greater sin — first stage). Q 4:43 (do not pray drunk — second stage). Q 16:67 (mentions intoxicating drink and "wholesome provision" as separate — read by some as early warning). Q 47:15 (rivers of wine — pleasing to drinkers — in Paradise; wine of Paradise differs from earthly).
Quran 5:93
Past acts forgiven No retroactive sin
لَيْسَ عَلَى الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا وَعَمِلُوا الصَّالِحَاتِ جُنَاحٌ فِيمَا طَعِمُوا إِذَا مَا اتَّقَوا وَّآمَنُوا وَعَمِلُوا الصَّالِحَاتِ ثُمَّ اتَّقَوا وَّآمَنُوا ثُمَّ اتَّقَوا وَّأَحْسَنُوا ۗ وَاللَّهُ يُحِبُّ الْمُحْسِنِينَ
"There is no blame on those who believe and do good in what they consumed (before the prohibition) — provided they are God-conscious, believe, and do good, then continue in taqwā and faith, then continue in taqwā and iḥsān. Allah loves those who do iḥsān."
Plain meaning
Revealed after the alcohol ban (5:90–91). Companions asked: "What about our brothers who died before the prohibition while drinking?" The verse answers: no retroactive blame on those who acted in good faith before a prohibition came down. The principle: a person is judged by what they knew, when they knew it.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for the foundational legal principle: al-aṣl barāʾat al-dhimma — innocence is the default. No one is accountable for an act done before clear prohibition. The verse also establishes the spiritual ascent: taqwā + īmān → taqwā + good deeds → taqwā + iḥsān (the highest level, doing as if you see Allah).
Related verses
Q 17:15 (We do not punish until We send a messenger). Q 6:131 (your Lord would not destroy a community while its people are unaware). Q 65:7 (Allah does not burden a soul beyond what He has given it).
Quran 5:95–96
Hunting in iḥrām Sea food permitted
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا لَا تَقْتُلُوا الصَّيْدَ وَأَنتُمْ حُرُمٌ ۚ وَمَن قَتَلَهُ مِنكُم مُّتَعَمِّدًا فَجَزَاءٌ مِّثْلُ مَا قَتَلَ مِنَ النَّعَمِ ... أَوْ كَفَّارَةٌ طَعَامُ مَسَاكِينَ أَوْ عَدْلُ ذَٰلِكَ صِيَامًا ... أُحِلَّ لَكُمْ صَيْدُ الْبَحْرِ وَطَعَامُهُ مَتَاعًا لَّكُمْ وَلِلسَّيَّارَةِ ۖ وَحُرِّمَ عَلَيْكُمْ صَيْدُ الْبَرِّ مَا دُمْتُمْ حُرُمًا
"O believers, do not kill game while in iḥrām. Whoever does so intentionally: the penalty is an equivalent of what he killed from livestock ... or feeding the poor as kaffāra, or fasting an equivalent ... Lawful to you is the game of the sea and its food, as provision for you and the traveler. Forbidden to you is land game while you are in iḥrām."
Plain meaning
Detailed penalty for hunting violation in iḥrām. Three options of kaffāra: (1) equivalent livestock animal sacrificed at the Kaʿba, (2) feeding the poor (with grain equivalent to the animal's value), (3) fasting an equivalent number of days. Sea creatures, however, are fully permitted even in iḥrām — important for travelers and pilgrims along coasts.
Majority view
All four schools accept the three-option kaffāra structure with the pilgrim choosing. Estimation of "equivalent livestock" is made by two just judges from the community. Sea-game permission applies to fish and most aquatic creatures — but schools differ on which sea animals qualify.
Where the schools differ
ḤanafīOnly fish qualifies as sea-game; other sea creatures (lobster, crab, shellfish) are forbidden as they are not "fish".
Mālikī/Shāfiʿī/ḤanbalīAny sea creature is permitted (broad reading of "sea-game"), including crustaceans and mollusks. Shāfiʿī famously: "All in the sea is permitted, dead or alive".
Accidental killingḤanafī: kaffāra applies even to accidental killing. Other schools: only intentional. The verse says mutaʿammidan (intentionally), supporting the latter view textually.
Related verses
Q 5:1 (you may not consider hunting lawful while in iḥrām). Q 5:2 (do not violate the symbols of Allah's sacred months). Q 2:196 (rituals of Hajj and ʿUmra).
Quran 5:100
Quality over quantity Bad vs good not equal
قُل لَّا يَسْتَوِي الْخَبِيثُ وَالطَّيِّبُ وَلَوْ أَعْجَبَكَ كَثْرَةُ الْخَبِيثِ ۚ فَاتَّقُوا اللَّهَ يَا أُولِي الْأَلْبَابِ لَعَلَّكُمْ تُفْلِحُونَ
"Say: The impure (khabīth) and the pure (ṭayyib) are not equal — even if the abundance of the impure amazes you. So be mindful of Allah, O people of understanding — that you may succeed."
Plain meaning
A piercing statement against majority-as-truth. Just because something impure is widespread doesn't elevate its value; just because something pure is rare doesn't diminish it. The verse addresses both: (1) moral worth — pure and impure are categorically different; (2) practical evaluation — do not be swayed by the impressive volume of what is wrong.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the legal-ethical principle that halal earnings — even small — are categorically better than haram earnings — even large; (2) rejection of the popular-fallacy thinking ("everyone does it"); (3) the call to ūlū l-albāb — people of insight — implying that wisdom is needed to see past surface impressiveness. The verse interfaces with modern questions about ethical investing, lawful careers, and resisting normalized wrongdoing.
Related verses
Q 2:267 (give of the good — not the bad). Q 35:19–22 (the blind and the seeing are not equal). Q 39:9 (those who know and those who do not know are not equal). Q 6:116 (most of those on earth would lead you astray — they follow only conjecture).
Quran 5:101–102
Do not over-question Silence as wisdom
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا لَا تَسْأَلُوا عَنْ أَشْيَاءَ إِن تُبْدَ لَكُمْ تَسُؤْكُمْ ۚ وَإِن تَسْأَلُوا عَنْهَا حِينَ يُنَزَّلُ الْقُرْآنُ تُبْدَ لَكُمْ عَفَا اللَّهُ عَنْهَا ۗ وَاللَّهُ غَفُورٌ حَلِيمٌ ۝ قَدْ سَأَلَهَا قَوْمٌ مِّن قَبْلِكُمْ ثُمَّ أَصْبَحُوا بِهَا كَافِرِينَ
"O believers, do not ask about things which, if revealed to you, would distress you. If you ask about them while the Quran is being revealed, they will be revealed to you. Allah has pardoned (your past asking). Allah is forgiving, forbearing. A people before you asked similar questions, and then became disbelievers."
Plain meaning
A subtle but profound legal principle. Sometimes seeking specific clarification creates a new binding obligation that would not otherwise exist. The example in the Sunnah: when a man asked the Prophet ﷺ if Hajj was obligatory every year, the Prophet ﷺ replied with frustration: "If I said yes, it would become so. Leave me as long as I leave you" (Muslim).
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the principle of al-aṣl al-ibāḥa — the default in mundane matters is permissibility; over-questioning can create unnecessary obligation; (2) intellectual humility — not every theological question needs an answer; some are deliberately left general by the Lawgiver; (3) prohibition of excessive hypothetical jurisprudence — building elaborate rulings for situations that don't exist. Classical scholars contrast this with genuine learning questions, which are required.
Related verses
Q 5:99 (the Messenger's duty is only conveyance). Q 22:78 (no ḥaraj in religion). Q 2:67–71 (the cow story — the Israelites' over-questioning narrowed their options).
Quran 5:106–108
Wills Witnesses Oath of witness
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا شَهَادَةُ بَيْنِكُمْ إِذَا حَضَرَ أَحَدَكُمُ الْمَوْتُ حِينَ الْوَصِيَّةِ اثْنَانِ ذَوَا عَدْلٍ مِّنكُمْ أَوْ آخَرَانِ مِنْ غَيْرِكُمْ ...
"O believers, when death approaches one of you, the witness at the time of bequest: two just persons from among you — or two others from outside, if you are traveling and death befalls you ..."
Plain meaning
A relatively rare verse explicitly allowing non-Muslim witnesses in certain emergency situations — namely, when a Muslim is dying away from Muslim company. The verse then specifies the procedure if the witnesses are later suspected of dishonesty: they swear by Allah, but if their oath is challenged, the proper heirs may swear an opposing oath and the testimony is overturned.
Majority view
All four schools take this verse as the basis for: (1) requirement of two just witnesses for wills; (2) the principle that wills are testamentary acts requiring legal formality; (3) the possibility of oath-rejection by heirs as a procedural remedy. Some classical jurists read "two others from outside" as Muslims of another tribe (Ḥanafī), others as actual non-Muslim witnesses in emergency (Mālikī, Shāfiʿī).
Where the schools differ
Ḥanafī"Min ghayrikum" = from a different tribe of Muslims. Non-Muslim witnesses generally not accepted in Islamic courts.
Mālikī/Shāfiʿī/Ḥanbalī"Min ghayrikum" = non-Muslims, acceptable as witnesses to a Muslim's will specifically in travel/emergency. Not a general permission for non-Muslim testimony.
Related verses
Q 2:282 (the witnessing of contracts — debt verse). Q 2:180 (writing a will for parents and kin). Q 4:11 (inheritance shares — wills cannot override).
Quran 6:38
Animal communities Creature dignity
وَمَا مِن دَابَّةٍ فِي الْأَرْضِ وَلَا طَائِرٍ يَطِيرُ بِجَنَاحَيْهِ إِلَّا أُمَمٌ أَمْثَالُكُم ۚ مَّا فَرَّطْنَا فِي الْكِتَابِ مِن شَيْءٍ ۚ ثُمَّ إِلَىٰ رَبِّهِمْ يُحْشَرُونَ
"There is no creature on the earth, nor a bird that flies with its wings, but they are communities (umam) like you. We have neglected nothing in the Book. To their Lord they will be gathered."
Plain meaning
A theologically and ethically remarkable verse. Animals — land creatures and birds — are described as umam amthālukum: "communities like yours". They have their own social orders, languages, lives, and (the verse ends) will be gathered to their Lord. This frames human relationship to animals not as ownership over things but as shared stewardship of communities under Allah.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) basic animal-rights obligations — humane treatment, feeding/watering, no overburdening, no torture; (2) prohibition of cruelty to animals (the Prophet ﷺ: "a woman entered Hellfire for a cat she locked up — she did not feed it nor let it eat from the earth"); (3) prohibition of needless killing — hunting for sport is discouraged; killing only for food or genuine harm-prevention; (4) the Quranic framing that animals have eschatological value, not just utility.
Related verses
Q 16:5–8 (cattle He created for you — warmth, food, beauty). Q 81:5 (when the wild beasts are gathered). Q 6:142 (eat of His provision but do not be wasteful). Q 17:44 (everything glorifies Him, but you do not understand their glorification).
Quran 6:54
Greet believers Mercy prescribed Forgiveness
وَإِذَا جَاءَكَ الَّذِينَ يُؤْمِنُونَ بِآيَاتِنَا فَقُلْ سَلَامٌ عَلَيْكُمْ ۖ كَتَبَ رَبُّكُمْ عَلَىٰ نَفْسِهِ الرَّحْمَةَ ۖ أَنَّهُ مَن عَمِلَ مِنكُمْ سُوءًا بِجَهَالَةٍ ثُمَّ تَابَ مِن بَعْدِهِ وَأَصْلَحَ فَأَنَّهُ غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ
"When those who believe in Our signs come to you, say: 'Peace be upon you.' Your Lord has prescribed mercy upon Himself: whoever among you commits an evil out of ignorance, then repents after it and reforms — He is forgiving, merciful."
Plain meaning
Two distinct directives in one verse. (1) A specific greeting protocol: when believers come to the Prophet ﷺ (and by extension, to one another), the response is peace. (2) A profound theological statement: Allah has prescribed mercy upon Himself — meaning, He has bound Himself to forgive those who repent and reform after ignorance-driven wrong.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the Sunnah of greeting fellow believers with salām — a religious obligation when one enters a gathering or meets a believer; (2) the foundational principle of tawba (repentance) — sincere turning back with reform is universally accepted; (3) the comforting theological reality that Allah's mercy is not just available but self-imposed. The verse becomes a model for how teachers, leaders, and parents should welcome those seeking guidance: with peace, not severity.
Related verses
Q 6:12 ("He has prescribed mercy upon Himself" — same surah, same phrase). Q 39:53 (do not despair of Allah's mercy). Q 7:156 (My mercy encompasses all things). Q 4:86 (return greetings with better or the same).
Quran 6:70
Walk away from mockery Reminder
وَذَرِ الَّذِينَ اتَّخَذُوا دِينَهُمْ لَعِبًا وَلَهْوًا وَغَرَّتْهُمُ الْحَيَاةُ الدُّنْيَا ۚ وَذَكِّرْ بِهِ أَن تُبْسَلَ نَفْسٌ بِمَا كَسَبَتْ
"Leave those who take their religion as play and amusement, whom the worldly life has deluded. But remind by it (the Quran), lest a soul be ruined by what it has earned."
Plain meaning
Two complementary commands: (1) walk away from those who treat their own religion (or religion in general) as mockery, amusement, and play; (2) yet continue to remind through the Quran — because, even amid such company, some soul may still benefit and avoid ruin. The verse balances strategic distance with persistent invitation.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the principle of not engaging with those determined to mock — the Prophet ﷺ did not waste effort in heated argument with mockers; (2) the simultaneous obligation of reminder — even when individuals reject, the message persists for whoever else may hear; (3) the warning that treating religion as entertainment — turning it into spectacle, jokes, content — is a serious spiritual failure. Modern application: how to engage anti-religious mockery online — recommended response: withdraw, remind, do not feed cycles of provocation.
Related verses
Q 4:140 (do not sit with those who mock Allah's signs until they enter another conversation). Q 25:72 (those who do not bear false witness — when they pass by idle talk, they pass with dignity). Q 6:68 (when you see those mocking Our signs, turn away from them).
Quran 6:79
Ibrāhīm's tawḥīd Pure direction
إِنِّي وَجَّهْتُ وَجْهِيَ لِلَّذِي فَطَرَ السَّمَاوَاتِ وَالْأَرْضَ حَنِيفًا ۖ وَمَا أَنَا مِنَ الْمُشْرِكِينَ
"I have turned my face to the One who created the heavens and the earth — as a ḥanīf — and I am not of the polytheists."
Plain meaning
Ibrāhīm's (peace upon him) declaration at the resolution of his search for the Creator — after observing the stars, moon, and sun, he reasoned past created objects to the One who originated them. The verse is the model of ḥanīf — pure, upright, instinctively monotheistic — devotion. It is recited as part of the opening duʿāʾ in some schools of prayer (Shāfiʿī, in particular).
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the model of tawḥīd derived through reasoned reflection, not just inherited from tradition — Ibrāhīm did not accept his father's idols simply because they were inherited; (2) the recommended formula for opening prayer in the Shāfiʿī school — recited as part of the iftitāḥ; (3) the broader principle that Islam is fundamentally a directional orientation — turning the whole of one's being toward the Creator.
Related verses
Q 6:74–78 (Ibrāhīm's search culminating in this verse). Q 3:67 (Ibrāhīm was neither Jew nor Christian, but a ḥanīf, a Muslim). Q 16:120–123 (Ibrāhīm was a community in himself, a ḥanīf — follow his way).
Quran 6:103
Vision cannot encompass Transcendence
لَّا تُدْرِكُهُ الْأَبْصَارُ وَهُوَ يُدْرِكُ الْأَبْصَارَ ۖ وَهُوَ اللَّطِيفُ الْخَبِيرُ
"Vision cannot grasp Him, yet He grasps all vision. He is the Subtle, the All-Aware."
Plain meaning
A concise theological statement: Allah is not something human sight can contain or comprehend — yet His perception of all things is total. The asymmetry between Creator and creation is absolute. The verse closes with two of Allah's names: al-Laṭīf (the Subtle — knowing the inmost details) and al-Khabīr (the All-Aware).
Majority view
All four Sunni schools cite this verse in the context of the believer's vision of Allah in the Hereafter: classical Sunni doctrine — based on multiple authentic ḥadīth and Q 75:22–23 ("faces that Day will be radiant, looking at their Lord") — affirms that the believers will see Allah in Paradise. But this seeing is not comprehension or encompassing. Muʿtazila and some Shia traditions denied any vision of Allah even in the Hereafter, citing this verse exclusively. Sunni mainstream reconciliation: vision yes, encompassing no.
Related verses
Q 75:22–23 (faces on that Day will look at their Lord). Q 7:143 (Mūsā's request: show Yourself to me — "You will not see Me"). Q 42:11 (nothing is like Him). Q 112:4 (none is comparable to Him).
Quran 6:108
No insulting others' gods Civility in disagreement
وَلَا تَسُبُّوا الَّذِينَ يَدْعُونَ مِن دُونِ اللَّهِ فَيَسُبُّوا اللَّهَ عَدْوًا بِغَيْرِ عِلْمٍ
"Do not insult those (deities) they call upon besides Allah, lest they insult Allah out of hostility and ignorance."
Plain meaning
A surprising verse — the Quran forbids Muslims from cursing or insulting the gods of polytheists. The pragmatic reason: it will provoke reciprocal cursing of Allah, in their ignorance. The principled reason (developed by classical scholars): one does not refute falsehood through insult, and one does not provoke retaliatory blasphemy.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) prohibition of mocking, cursing, or insulting the religious symbols, prophets, sacred figures, or holy objects of any other faith; (2) the broader principle that Muslims must protect the dignity of all sincere religious devotion, even when in disagreement; (3) prohibition of provocative speech that triggers retaliatory blasphemy. Classical fiqh derives from this verse the principle that preventing greater harm (Allah being insulted) takes priority over satisfying righteous indignation.
Related verses
Q 16:125 (call to Allah with wisdom and beautiful preaching). Q 29:46 (argue with People of the Book except in the best way). Q 25:63 (servants of the Most Merciful — when ignorant address them, they say "peace"). Q 41:34 (repel evil with what is better).
Quran 6:121
Bismillāh on slaughter Forbidden meat
وَلَا تَأْكُلُوا مِمَّا لَمْ يُذْكَرِ اسْمُ اللَّهِ عَلَيْهِ وَإِنَّهُ لَفِسْقٌ ۗ وَإِنَّ الشَّيَاطِينَ لَيُوحُونَ إِلَىٰ أَوْلِيَائِهِمْ لِيُجَادِلُوكُمْ ۖ وَإِنْ أَطَعْتُمُوهُمْ إِنَّكُمْ لَمُشْرِكُونَ
"Do not eat that over which Allah's name has not been mentioned — it is sinful (fisq). The devils inspire their allies to dispute with you. If you obey them, you are polytheists."
Plain meaning
A clear command to eat only meat where Allah's name was invoked at the moment of slaughter. The verse closes with an unusually strong warning: those who would argue against this requirement are inspired by satanic suggestion. Obeying their argument is itself a path to shirk — because the act of dedicating an animal's life to anyone other than Allah is religiously charged.
Majority view
All four schools require bismillāh at the moment of tadhkiya (Islamic slaughter), but differ on whether forgetting invalidates the meat. The Prophet ﷺ explained: "The Muslim slaughters with Allah's name — whether he mentions it (verbally) or not" (Bukhārī, on companions asking about meat brought by bedouin who may have forgotten). This ḥadīth balances the verse: the requirement applies to deliberate omission.
Where the schools differ
Ḥanafī/MālikīRequired and deliberate omission invalidates the meat. Forgetting: meat remains halal — based on the Prophet's ﷺ ḥadīth.
ShāfiʿīRecommended (highly), not strictly required. Meat is halal even without bismillāh, though saying it is sunnah.
ḤanbalīStrictest: required, and deliberate omission invalidates. Forgetting is also problematic, though forgiven if without negligence.
Meat of People of the BookAll schools (per Q 5:5): permitted, provided proper slaughter. Modern industrial meat raises debate — see 5:3 entry.
Related verses
Q 5:3 (forbidden food categories). Q 5:5 (food of People of the Book is lawful). Q 6:118 (eat what Allah's name was mentioned upon). Q 22:34 (mention Allah's name over the cattle He has provided).
Quran 6:151–152
Ten Commandments Foundational ethics
قُلْ تَعَالَوْا أَتْلُ مَا حَرَّمَ رَبُّكُمْ عَلَيْكُمْ ۖ أَلَّا تُشْرِكُوا بِهِ شَيْئًا ۖ وَبِالْوَالِدَيْنِ إِحْسَانًا ۖ وَلَا تَقْتُلُوا أَوْلَادَكُم مِّنْ إِمْلَاقٍ ... وَلَا تَقْرَبُوا الْفَوَاحِشَ ... وَلَا تَقْتُلُوا النَّفْسَ الَّتِي حَرَّمَ اللَّهُ إِلَّا بِالْحَقِّ ... وَلَا تَقْرَبُوا مَالَ الْيَتِيمِ ... وَأَوْفُوا الْكَيْلَ وَالْمِيزَانَ بِالْقِسْطِ ... وَإِذَا قُلْتُمْ فَاعْدِلُوا ... وَبِعَهْدِ اللَّهِ أَوْفُوا
"Say: Come, let me recite what your Lord has forbidden you. Do not associate anything with Him. Be good to parents. Do not kill your children for poverty ... Do not approach indecencies ... Do not kill a soul Allah has forbidden, except by right ... Do not approach the orphan's property ... Give full measure and weight in justice ... When you speak, be just ... and fulfill the covenant of Allah."
Plain meaning
Sometimes called the "Ten Commandments of the Quran". A compact list of foundational prohibitions and obligations: (1) no shirk, (2) kindness to parents, (3) no killing children for fear of poverty, (4) no indecencies — open or hidden, (5) no killing of a soul except by right, (6) no approaching orphan's property, (7) just weights and measures, (8) just speech, (9) fulfillment of the covenant of Allah, (10) following Allah's straight path. Ibn ʿAbbās: "These ten verses contain the essence of all heavenly scriptures".
Majority view
All four schools and all Islamic ethical traditions treat this as a foundational moral charter — every later law links back to one of these principles. Notable inclusions: (a) economic justice (full measure) listed alongside theological commands; (b) speech ethics ("be just when you speak") — meaning truthfulness is required even in casual statements; (c) prohibition of female infanticide (a pre-Islamic practice) is explicitly named in v.151.
Related verses
Q 17:23–39 (extended parallel "ethics core" in Banū Isrāʾīl). Q 25:63–77 (the "qualities of the servants of the Most Merciful"). Q 2:177 (the comprehensive definition of birr).
Quran 6:160
Ten-fold reward Justice in punishment
مَن جَاءَ بِالْحَسَنَةِ فَلَهُ عَشْرُ أَمْثَالِهَا ۖ وَمَن جَاءَ بِالسَّيِّئَةِ فَلَا يُجْزَىٰ إِلَّا مِثْلَهَا وَهُمْ لَا يُظْلَمُونَ
"Whoever brings a good deed will have ten like it. Whoever brings an evil deed will not be recompensed except its equal — and they will not be wronged."
Plain meaning
The Quran's asymmetric formula of divine accounting. Good deeds: tenfold reward as the minimum (other verses describe 700-fold and beyond). Evil deeds: only equal recompense — never more. The verse closes with the reassurance that this entire system is built on justice, not even slight wronging.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) Allah's generosity in reward — even the smallest sincere good action receives multiplied weight; (2) the principle that mercy precedes wrath in divine accounting; (3) the limit on punishment — evil cannot be repaid by Allah with more than its equal, while good is always magnified. This gives Muslims foundational hope: a life with even modest sincere good deeds, in the divine accounting, can substantially outweigh occasional failures.
Related verses
Q 2:261 (the 700-fold parable for charity in Allah's way). Q 99:7–8 (atom-weight reckoning). Q 42:40 (recompense for evil is its equal — but forgiveness brings reward).
Quran 6:162–163
Total devotion No partner
قُلْ إِنَّ صَلَاتِي وَنُسُكِي وَمَحْيَايَ وَمَمَاتِي لِلَّهِ رَبِّ الْعَالَمِينَ ۝ لَا شَرِيكَ لَهُ ۖ وَبِذَٰلِكَ أُمِرْتُ وَأَنَا أَوَّلُ الْمُسْلِمِينَ
"Say: My prayer, my sacrifice, my life, and my death — are for Allah, Lord of the worlds. No partner has He. And so I was commanded — and I am the first of those who submit."
Plain meaning
A short but defining declaration. Four dimensions of human existence — worship, sacrifice/service, life, and even death — are dedicated entirely to Allah. The verse is recited as part of the standard opening duʿāʾ at the start of every prayer (in the Shāfiʿī school) and forms a Sunnah recommendation in others.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the foundational concept of ikhlāṣ (sincerity) — every act of worship and life-decision must be for Allah alone; (2) the prohibition of dedicating any sacrifice (animal or otherwise) to anything other than Allah — even invoking another name at slaughter renders it haram (per Q 5:3); (3) the model of total surrender — Islam literally means submission, and the Prophet ﷺ describes himself here as the first of those who submit.
Related verses
Q 39:11 (I have been commanded to worship Allah, making religion purely for Him). Q 108:2 (so pray to your Lord and sacrifice). Q 51:56 (I created jinn and mankind only to worship Me).
Quran 6:165
Stewardship Hierarchy as test
وَهُوَ الَّذِي جَعَلَكُمْ خَلَائِفَ الْأَرْضِ وَرَفَعَ بَعْضَكُمْ فَوْقَ بَعْضٍ دَرَجَاتٍ لِّيَبْلُوَكُمْ فِي مَا آتَاكُمْ ۗ إِنَّ رَبَّكَ سَرِيعُ الْعِقَابِ وَإِنَّهُ لَغَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ
"It is He who made you successors (khalāʾif) on the earth, and raised some of you over others in ranks — to test you in what He has given you. Your Lord is swift in punishment, and He is also forgiving, merciful."
Plain meaning
Three foundational statements: (1) humans are Allah's successors (khalāʾif) on earth — stewards, not owners; (2) the hierarchies of wealth, status, ability, position are not a reward but a test; (3) Allah's swift punishment and His mercy are paired — neither overrides the other. The verse reframes every advantage in life as responsibility, every disadvantage as opportunity.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the concept of khilāfa — humanity's stewardship of creation, with attendant obligations; (2) the recognition that inequality is real but not justification for arrogance — it's a test for the privileged and a test for the deprived; (3) the basis of environmental and social responsibility — those given more (wealth, knowledge, power) are tested more strictly. Modern Islamic ethics derives from this verse a strong critique of consumerism, environmental destruction, and inequality without redistribution.
Related verses
Q 2:30 (I am placing a successor on earth). Q 35:39 (He made you successors on earth — whoever disbelieves, his disbelief is on himself). Q 7:69 (consider when He made you successors after the people of Nūḥ). Q 24:55 (Allah has promised those who believe and do good — He will surely make them successors on earth).
Quran 7:23
Adam's prayer Foundational repentance
قَالَا رَبَّنَا ظَلَمْنَا أَنفُسَنَا وَإِن لَّمْ تَغْفِرْ لَنَا وَتَرْحَمْنَا لَنَكُونَنَّ مِنَ الْخَاسِرِينَ
"They said: 'Our Lord, we have wronged ourselves. If You do not forgive us and have mercy upon us, we shall surely be among the losers.'"
Plain meaning
Adam and Eve's response after eating from the forbidden tree. Three elements that define the model of Islamic repentance: (1) acknowledgment of wrong — owning the failure, not deflecting or blaming the serpent; (2) direct appeal to Allah — no intermediary, no priest; (3) recognition of one's loss if mercy is not granted. The verse contrasts sharply with the Iblīs response in 7:13 — Iblīs blamed Allah for his expulsion; Adam blamed himself and asked for mercy.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse as the model duʿāʾ of tawba — the foundational pattern of all repentance prayers. Sunni doctrine: Adam's act was a zalla (slip) — a momentary lapse — not a sin in the technical sense (prophets are protected from major sin), but the verse remains universally applicable as the template for what every wrong-doer should say. It is commonly recited at the end of prayers, in personal supplication, and in collective duʿāʾ. The verse demolishes the doctrine of inherited sin: Adam's failure was his own, addressed by his own repentance, and Allah accepted it.
Related verses
Q 2:37 (Adam received words from his Lord — He accepted his repentance). Q 20:121 (Adam disobeyed his Lord, and erred). Q 7:13 (Iblīs's response — by Your might, I will mislead them). Q 4:110 (whoever does evil and then asks Allah's forgiveness will find Allah forgiving, merciful).
Quran 7:31
No wasting Adornment for worship
يَا بَنِي آدَمَ خُذُوا زِينَتَكُمْ عِندَ كُلِّ مَسْجِدٍ وَكُلُوا وَاشْرَبُوا وَلَا تُسْرِفُوا ۚ إِنَّهُ لَا يُحِبُّ الْمُسْرِفِينَ
"O children of Adam, take your adornment at every place of prayer. Eat and drink, but do not be wasteful (isrāf). Indeed, He does not love the wasteful."
Plain meaning
Three rulings: (1) dress nicely for prayer — particularly congregational prayer (revealed in context of some who would circumambulate the Kaʿba undressed); (2) eat and drink — pleasure in lawful provision is permitted; (3) do not be wasteful — isrāf (excess, waste, extravagance) is disliked.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the Sunnah of dressing well, perfuming, and using miswāk before prayer — especially Jumuʿa; (2) prohibition of asceticism that rejects lawful pleasures (food, drink, marriage, dress) — the verse explicitly permits enjoyment; (3) the legal/ethical principle of iqtiṣād — moderation, mid-path between miserliness and waste. Modern jurists apply isrāf to environmental waste, food waste, excessive consumption, and extravagant weddings/funerals.
Related verses
Q 17:26–27 (do not be a spendthrift — spendthrifts are brothers of the devils). Q 25:67 (when they spend, they are neither extravagant nor stingy — but between the two). Q 7:32 (who has forbidden the adornments of Allah which He has produced for His servants?).
Quran 7:33
Universal prohibitions Categories of sin
قُلْ إِنَّمَا حَرَّمَ رَبِّيَ الْفَوَاحِشَ مَا ظَهَرَ مِنْهَا وَمَا بَطَنَ وَالْإِثْمَ وَالْبَغْيَ بِغَيْرِ الْحَقِّ وَأَن تُشْرِكُوا بِاللَّهِ مَا لَمْ يُنَزِّلْ بِهِ سُلْطَانًا وَأَن تَقُولُوا عَلَى اللَّهِ مَا لَا تَعْلَمُونَ
"Say: My Lord has only forbidden: indecencies (fawāḥish) — open or hidden; sin (ithm); aggression (baghy) without right; that you associate with Allah anything for which He has sent down no authority; and that you say about Allah what you do not know."
Plain meaning
A compact taxonomy of what Allah has actually forbidden — pushing back against pre-Islamic Arabs who had invented many false prohibitions on themselves (and on women, animals, food). Five categories: (1) fawāḥish — gross indecencies, open or hidden (zinā, sodomy, lying, betrayal of trust); (2) ithm — general sin and wrongdoing; (3) baghy — transgression against others' rights; (4) shirk — assigning partners to Allah; (5) speaking about Allah without knowledge — fabricating rulings, attributing things to Allah falsely.
Majority view
All four schools and the science of uṣūl al-fiqh cite this verse as the foundational categorization of major sins. From the fifth category — saying about Allah what you do not know — derives the entire scholarly discipline of rigorously sourcing every legal ruling. Issuing a fatwa without proper knowledge is itself one of the major sins identified here. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote a famous treatise — I'lām al-Muwaqqiʿīn — applying this verse to the responsibilities of jurists.
Related verses
Q 16:90 (Allah commands justice, excellence, and giving — forbids indecency, wrong, and aggression). Q 6:151–152 (the ten commandments of Al-Anʿām). Q 17:36 (do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge).
Quran 7:55–56
Humble supplication No corruption
ادْعُوا رَبَّكُمْ تَضَرُّعًا وَخُفْيَةً ۚ إِنَّهُ لَا يُحِبُّ الْمُعْتَدِينَ ۝ وَلَا تُفْسِدُوا فِي الْأَرْضِ بَعْدَ إِصْلَاحِهَا وَادْعُوهُ خَوْفًا وَطَمَعًا ۚ إِنَّ رَحْمَتَ اللَّهِ قَرِيبٌ مِّنَ الْمُحْسِنِينَ
"Call your Lord humbly and in secret. He does not love transgressors. Do not cause corruption (fasād) on the earth after its reformation, and call Him in fear and hope. Allah's mercy is near to the doers of good (muḥsinīn)."
Plain meaning
Two ethical principles in supplication and conduct: (1) supplicate Allah in humility and secrecy — public showy prayer is closer to iʿtidāʾ (transgression) than to genuine connection; (2) do not cause fasād in the earth — the principle of not damaging what is already in good order. Worship pairs with stewardship: humility before Allah and care for His creation.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the etiquette of duʿāʾ — preferred privately, with humility, not in display; (2) the foundational principle of environmental stewardship — preventing degradation, pollution, and harm to natural systems; (3) "after reformation" — once a community, ecosystem, or system has been brought to good order, actively damaging it is a major sin. Modern Islamic environmental ethics cites this verse heavily.
Related verses
Q 30:41 (corruption has appeared in the land and sea by what people's hands have earned). Q 2:11 (when told do not corrupt the earth — they say "we are only reformers"). Q 7:74 (do not exceed bounds in the earth, causing corruption). Q 19:7 (Zakariyyāh called his Lord in secret).
Quran 7:172
Primordial covenant Am I not your Lord?
وَإِذْ أَخَذَ رَبُّكَ مِن بَنِي آدَمَ مِن ظُهُورِهِمْ ذُرِّيَّتَهُمْ وَأَشْهَدَهُمْ عَلَىٰ أَنفُسِهِمْ أَلَسْتُ بِرَبِّكُمْ ۖ قَالُوا بَلَىٰ ۛ شَهِدْنَا ۛ أَن تَقُولُوا يَوْمَ الْقِيَامَةِ إِنَّا كُنَّا عَنْ هَٰذَا غَافِلِينَ
"When your Lord brought forth from the children of Adam, from their loins, their descendants, and made them testify against themselves: 'Am I not your Lord?' They said: 'Yes — we testify.' Lest you should say on the Day of Resurrection: 'We were unaware of this.'"
Plain meaning
The Quran's account of the primordial covenant (mīthāq). Before earthly life, Allah brought forth every soul and asked the foundational question. Every soul answered balā — "yes". This affirmation underlies the universal religious capacity — and removes the excuse of not knowing. The verse provides the theological context for human accountability: no one can claim they had no Creator-awareness embedded in them.
Majority view
All four schools and Islamic theology cite this verse for: (1) the fiṭra (innate orientation) discussed in Q 30:30 has historical-cosmic grounding here — every soul once directly testified; (2) the theological problem of religious-rejection is reframed: rejecting Allah is not discovering Him absent but denying what one already knows; (3) the eschatological consequence: no one will be able to say "we never knew". Sufi traditions developed elaborate spiritual theology around balā as the moment of every soul's primordial affirmation.
Related verses
Q 30:30 (fiṭra — natural disposition). Q 2:30 (the placement of humanity as khalīfa). Q 33:72 (We offered the trust to the heavens and earth — humans accepted). Q 91:7–8 (a soul and He who proportioned it — inspired it with its corruption and taqwā).
Quran 7:157
Prophet described Tayyibāt and khabāʾith Removing burden
الَّذِينَ يَتَّبِعُونَ الرَّسُولَ النَّبِيَّ الْأُمِّيَّ ... يَأْمُرُهُم بِالْمَعْرُوفِ وَيَنْهَاهُمْ عَنِ الْمُنكَرِ وَيُحِلُّ لَهُمُ الطَّيِّبَاتِ وَيُحَرِّمُ عَلَيْهِمُ الْخَبَائِثَ وَيَضَعُ عَنْهُمْ إِصْرَهُمْ وَالْأَغْلَالَ الَّتِي كَانَتْ عَلَيْهِمْ
"... those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered Prophet ... He commands them to do good and forbids them what is wrong, makes lawful for them the good things (ṭayyibāt), forbids them the impure (khabāʾith), and removes from them their burdens and shackles that were upon them."
Plain meaning
A description of the Prophet's ﷺ legal mission. Four things he does: (1) commands the maʿrūf (recognized good); (2) forbids the munkar (recognized wrong); (3) makes lawful the ṭayyibāt — the pure, wholesome, naturally good; (4) forbids the khabāʾith — the impure, harmful, naturally repugnant; and (5) removes burdens and shackles — undoing the over-strict rulings that had been imposed on earlier peoples.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the principle that Islam removes excessive burdens — many Jewish dietary prohibitions, for instance, are explicitly lifted; (2) the natural-law principle: things that are khabīth (intrinsically disgusting or harmful) are forbidden — even when not specifically named in the Quran. From this verse derives the legal category of "general prohibition by characteristic" — for example: scholars use this verse to prohibit modern intoxicants and harmful substances not specifically named.
Related verses
Q 2:172 (eat the good things). Q 5:4 (what is lawful for you of the good things). Q 22:78 (no ḥaraj in religion). Q 5:3 (forbidden categories).
Quran 7:199
Three commands Forgiveness Maʿrūf
خُذِ الْعَفْوَ وَأْمُرْ بِالْعُرْفِ وَأَعْرِضْ عَنِ الْجَاهِلِينَ
"Take to pardon (ʿafw), command the customary good (ʿurf), and turn away from the ignorant."
Plain meaning
A trio of commands sometimes called the jāmiʿ al-akhlāq ("the comprehensive verse on character"). When the verse was revealed, Jibrīl explained to the Prophet ﷺ: "Allah commands you to reconnect with one who has cut you off, give to one who deprives you, and pardon one who wrongs you" (Tirmidhī). Three concrete movements: (1) habitual pardoning; (2) inviting toward what is recognized as good; (3) walking away from those who insist on ignorance and provocation.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for the trio of: forgiveness, positive ethical encouragement, and strategic disengagement. The third is particularly important: not every quarrel deserves a response. The verse also legitimizes ʿurf (custom) — what is recognized as good in a community — as a valid source of law alongside Quran and Sunnah, provided it does not contradict them. This is the Quranic basis for the legal maxim al-ʿāda muḥakkama ("custom is a basis for ruling").
Related verses
Q 25:63 (servants of the Most Merciful — when ignorant address them, they say "peace"). Q 41:34 (repel evil with what is better). Q 42:40 (recompense for evil is equal — but whoever forgives, Allah's reward is greater). Q 16:90 (Allah commands justice and excellence).
Quran 8:1
War spoils State authority
يَسْأَلُونَكَ عَنِ الْأَنفَالِ ۖ قُلِ الْأَنفَالُ لِلَّهِ وَالرَّسُولِ ۖ فَاتَّقُوا اللَّهَ وَأَصْلِحُوا ذَاتَ بَيْنِكُمْ وَأَطِيعُوا اللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُ إِن كُنتُم مُّؤْمِنِينَ
"They ask you about war spoils (anfāl). Say: the spoils belong to Allah and the Messenger. So be mindful of Allah, reconcile what is between you, and obey Allah and His Messenger if you are believers."
Plain meaning
Revealed after Badr, when companions disputed over how to divide the spoils. The answer: distribution authority belongs to Allah and the Messenger — meaning, to the legitimate Islamic state. Individual soldiers do not have private claims on spoils; allocation is by the state per the rules later given in Q 8:41.
Majority view
All four schools: war spoils belong to the state, not to individual soldiers as personal property. The Imām (state authority) distributes per Q 8:41 — and may retain unusual items (e.g., the Prophet ﷺ kept a particular sword) for state use. The verse's closing — reconcile what is between you — establishes that internal disputes must not break the community even during military operations.
Related verses
Q 8:41 (one-fifth rule). Q 59:7 (spoils from peaceful capitulation — fayʾ — distinct from war spoils). Q 33:50 (the Prophet's ﷺ allocation of what his right hand possessed in Allah's fayʾ).
Quran 8:24
Called to what gives life Responsiveness
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا اسْتَجِيبُوا لِلَّهِ وَلِلرَّسُولِ إِذَا دَعَاكُمْ لِمَا يُحْيِيكُمْ ۖ وَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّ اللَّهَ يَحُولُ بَيْنَ الْمَرْءِ وَقَلْبِهِ وَأَنَّهُ إِلَيْهِ تُحْشَرُونَ
"O believers, respond to Allah and the Messenger when he calls you to what gives you life. Know that Allah comes between a person and his heart, and to Him you will be gathered."
Plain meaning
A striking description of revelation: what gives you life. The Quran and the Messenger ﷺ are not described as imposing burdens, but as life-giving. The verse then issues a warning: Allah is closer to a person than the person to their own heart — delay in responding is risky because hearts can change before you reach them.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the framing of religious response as life-giving — religion is gain, not loss; (2) the warning against procrastination in answering Allah's call — "I will pray, but later", "I will repent, but not now"; (3) the foundational theological claim that Allah controls hearts — the famous duʿāʾ "O Turner of hearts, keep my heart firm upon Your religion" derives from this verse and from the ḥadīth that elaborates it.
Related verses
Q 2:165 (love of Allah surpasses all other loves). Q 16:97 (whoever does good while believing — We will give them a good life). Q 3:8 (our Lord, do not let our hearts deviate after You have guided us). Q 7:179 (hearts they understand not with).
Quran 8:41
Khums State revenue Five recipients
وَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّمَا غَنِمْتُم مِّن شَيْءٍ فَأَنَّ لِلَّهِ خُمُسَهُ وَلِلرَّسُولِ وَلِذِي الْقُرْبَىٰ وَالْيَتَامَىٰ وَالْمَسَاكِينِ وَابْنِ السَّبِيلِ
"Know that whatever spoils you take — one-fifth (khums) belongs to Allah, the Messenger, the close relatives, orphans, the poor, and the wayfarer."
Plain meaning
The state takes one-fifth of war spoils for redistribution to five categories. The remaining four-fifths is divided among the soldiers who participated. The five recipients form a social-welfare safety net: (1) Allah/Messenger = state treasury, (2) the Messenger's clan (dhū l-qurbā), (3) orphans, (4) the poor, (5) wayfarers (travelers in need).
Majority view
All four Sunni schools: khums applies to war spoils (ghanīma) — meaning property captured in armed conflict — and, by extension, to rikāz (buried treasure). With the cessation of classical Islamic warfare and conquest, the verse has limited direct application in most modern contexts. After the Prophet's ﷺ death, the four schools differ on whether his portion and the clan's portion still apply.
Where the schools differ
ḤanafīAfter Prophet ﷺ: his share and the relatives' share lapse. The 1/5 is divided into 3 (orphans, poor, wayfarer). Spoils now distributed among orphans/poor/wayfarers + 4/5 to soldiers.
MālikīThe state has discretion (tawfīḍ) — Imām may distribute as he sees best for the public interest. No fixed continuation of the Prophet's share.
Shāfiʿī/ḤanbalīThe five-way division continues, with the Prophet's share now going to the state treasury / public interest, and the relatives' share to his descendants (Banū Hāshim).
Twelver Shia (Jaʿfarī)Extends khums beyond war spoils to ALL net annual surplus income (most distinctively Shia practice). Paid to maraji and to the poor of the Prophet's lineage. Not accepted by Sunni schools.
Related verses
Q 8:1 (spoils belong to Allah and Messenger). Q 59:6–10 (parallel principles for fayʾ, peaceful-capitulation property — six recipients, no soldier share).
Quran 8:46
Internal unity No dispute
وَأَطِيعُوا اللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُ وَلَا تَنَازَعُوا فَتَفْشَلُوا وَتَذْهَبَ رِيحُكُمْ ۖ وَاصْبِرُوا ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ مَعَ الصَّابِرِينَ
"Obey Allah and His Messenger. Do not dispute with one another, lest you fail and your strength (rīḥ) departs. Be patient. Allah is with the patient."
Plain meaning
A direct command revealed in military context but with broad application. Four imperatives: (1) obey Allah and Messenger; (2) do not dispute — internal conflict destroys both morale and unity; (3) do not let your rīḥ (wind/strength/morale) depart; (4) be patient — and the closing assurance: Allah is with the patient. The use of rīḥ (literally "wind") is striking — internal disunity is described as if it deflates the sail of collective effort.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the principle that internal Muslim disunity is a primary cause of communal weakness — in war, in scholarship, in social cohesion; (2) the obligation to resolve internal disputes swiftly and not let them metastasize; (3) the linkage between patience and divine support — Allah is with the patient. Modern application: Muslim community fragmentation, intra-religious polemic, sectarian conflict — all weakened by failure to heed this verse.
Related verses
Q 3:103 (hold to the rope of Allah, all together — do not be divided). Q 6:65 (or He tastes some of you the violence of others). Q 49:9–10 (reconcile between believers — they are brothers). Q 21:92 (this nation of yours is one nation).
Quran 8:61
Peace preferred Truce
وَإِن جَنَحُوا لِلسَّلْمِ فَاجْنَحْ لَهَا وَتَوَكَّلْ عَلَى اللَّهِ ۚ إِنَّهُ هُوَ السَّمِيعُ الْعَلِيمُ
"If they incline to peace, then incline to it — and place your trust in Allah. He is the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing."
Plain meaning
A short but crucial verse. If the enemy seeks peace, the Muslim community is commanded — not merely permitted — to respond in kind. The trust component is significant: a worry that they might use peace strategically is left to Allah, not used as grounds to refuse peace.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse as a foundational principle of jus ad bellum in Islamic law: peace is the default, war is the exception. Hudna (truce) is not only permitted but required when the enemy offers credible peace. The Treaty of Ḥudaybiyya — where the Prophet ﷺ accepted terms many companions saw as unfavorable — was vindicated by this verse. The principle bears especially on modern questions of international law and conflict resolution.
Where the schools differ
Maximum duration of truceShāfiʿī (strict): 10 years maximum (per Ḥudaybiyya analogy). Ḥanafī/Mālikī/Ḥanbalī: any duration the Imām judges good for the Muslim community — no fixed limit. Most modern jurists prefer indefinite peace where possible.
Q 9:5 vs Q 8:61Some classical jurists held Q 9:5 ("sword verse") abrogated peace-preference verses like 8:61. Modern majority and many classical scholars (Ibn Taymiyya, Ibn al-Qayyim): no — 9:5 addresses a specific group of treaty-breakers; the general rule remains 8:61's peace-preference.
Related verses
Q 2:208 (enter into peace fully). Q 60:8 (kindness to those who do not fight you). Q 49:9 (if two parties of believers fight, reconcile them).
Quran 9:5
"Sword verse" Heavily contextual
فَإِذَا انسَلَخَ الْأَشْهُرُ الْحُرُمُ فَاقْتُلُوا الْمُشْرِكِينَ حَيْثُ وَجَدتُّمُوهُمْ وَخُذُوهُمْ وَاحْصُرُوهُمْ وَاقْعُدُوا لَهُمْ كُلَّ مَرْصَدٍ ۚ فَإِن تَابُوا وَأَقَامُوا الصَّلَاةَ وَآتَوُا الزَّكَاةَ فَخَلُّوا سَبِيلَهُمْ ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ
"When the sacred months have passed, kill the polytheists wherever you find them, capture them, besiege them, and lie in wait for them at every place. If they repent, establish prayer, and give zakat — let them go free. Allah is forgiving, merciful."
Plain meaning
The infamous "sword verse" — but understood in context, it addresses a specific group: the Quraysh polytheists who had repeatedly broken treaties and were given four months' notice to either rejoin treaty terms, accept Islam, or face war. The verse does NOT address all non-Muslims — Q 9:4 explicitly exempts polytheists who had honored treaties; Q 9:6 commands granting asylum to any polytheist who seeks it; Q 9:7 commands honoring treaties as long as the other side does.
Majority view
All four schools agree this verse must be read in its full surah context — alongside 9:4, 9:6, 9:7, 9:13 — and is not a blanket command. Classical scholars (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr) read it as addressing specifically the Quraysh polytheists who had broken treaties. The verse's conditions are also rarely highlighted: if they repent (i.e., return to Islam) AND establish prayer AND give zakat, they go free. The "fight" is conditional and revocable. Q 60:8's command of kindness to peaceful non-Muslims is not abrogated.
Where the schools differ
Classical viewSpecific to the Arabian polytheists of the Prophet's ﷺ time after four months of grace. After full conquest of Arabia, the historical situation it addressed ceased to exist. The verse remains in the Quran as a principle: treaty-breakers face consequences.
Abrogation extremistsA few classical jurists held this verse abrogated 124 earlier "peace" verses (al-Ṣūfī ascription). Modern mainstream scholarship: this view is exegetically unsound; the verses operate in different contexts.
Modern readingThe verse confirms that breach of treaty + active war justifies armed response. It does NOT authorize unilateral aggression. Civilians, non-combatants, treaty partners, and people seeking asylum are all protected by surrounding verses and Sunnah.
Related verses
Q 9:4 (treaties with polytheists who honored their terms are to be completed). Q 9:6 (asylum for any polytheist seeking it). Q 9:7 (uphold treaties as long as the other side does). Q 60:8 (kindness to non-aggressive non-Muslims). Q 2:190 (do not transgress — Allah does not love transgressors).
Quran 9:6
Asylum Hearing the Quran
وَإِنْ أَحَدٌ مِّنَ الْمُشْرِكِينَ اسْتَجَارَكَ فَأَجِرْهُ حَتَّىٰ يَسْمَعَ كَلَامَ اللَّهِ ثُمَّ أَبْلِغْهُ مَأْمَنَهُ ۚ ذَٰلِكَ بِأَنَّهُمْ قَوْمٌ لَّا يَعْلَمُونَ
"If any of the polytheists seeks your protection, then grant him protection so that he may hear the word of Allah. Then escort him to a place of safety. That is because they are a people who do not know."
Plain meaning
Immediately after the "sword verse". A polytheist — even from the very group at war with the Muslim community — who comes seeking protection must be granted it, given the opportunity to hear the Quran, and then escorted to a place of safety even if he does not convert. This verse alone, classical scholars note, rules out any reading of 9:5 as blanket aggression.
Majority view
All four schools: any non-Muslim seeking peaceful entry to Muslim territory must be granted amān (safe passage). This amān may be granted by any Muslim — not just the ruler — and binds the entire community. The grant lasts until the visit's purpose is complete, then the person must be safely returned. From this verse derives the entire classical doctrine of amān and the modern principles of refuge and diplomatic immunity in Islamic law.
Related verses
Q 8:72 (those who seek refuge with you — protect them). Q 2:256 (no compulsion in religion). Q 16:125 (invite to your Lord's way with wisdom and good preaching). Q 4:90 (those who do not fight you — do not assault them).
Quran 9:29
Jizya Dhimmī status Heavily debated
قَاتِلُوا الَّذِينَ لَا يُؤْمِنُونَ بِاللَّهِ وَلَا بِالْيَوْمِ الْآخِرِ وَلَا يُحَرِّمُونَ مَا حَرَّمَ اللَّهُ وَرَسُولُهُ وَلَا يَدِينُونَ دِينَ الْحَقِّ مِنَ الَّذِينَ أُوتُوا الْكِتَابَ حَتَّىٰ يُعْطُوا الْجِزْيَةَ عَن يَدٍ وَهُمْ صَاغِرُونَ
"Fight those of the People of the Book who do not believe in Allah and the Last Day, do not forbid what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden, and do not adhere to the religion of truth — until they pay the jizya, willingly, while they are subdued."
Plain meaning
Revealed during the Tabūk campaign against the Byzantine empire. Jizya = a per-capita tax on adult, free, able-bodied non-Muslim men under Muslim political authority — in exchange for protection by the state and exemption from military service (which falls only on Muslims). The verse establishes the classical category of dhimmī — non-Muslims living under Muslim rule with protected legal status. Women, children, the elderly, the disabled, the poor, monastics, and non-combatants were exempt from jizya in classical practice.
Majority view
All four schools: jizya was a tax-for-protection contract — historically lower than the zakat and other taxes Muslims paid, and gave non-Muslims their own legal autonomy in personal-status matters (marriage, divorce, food, drink). The phrase "ʿan yad" — translated "willingly" or "in hand" — and "ṣāghirūn" — "subdued" — are contested. Some classical jurists read it as implying humiliation; others (Ibn Qudāma, modern majority) read it as simply "by the act of submission to the state" — meaning, paying as a subject of Muslim governance.
Where the schools differ
Who pays jizyaḤanafī/Mālikī: People of the Book + Zoroastrians + any non-Muslim except Arab polytheists (who must accept Islam). Shāfiʿī/Ḥanbalī (strict): only People of the Book and Zoroastrians.
AmountClassical: graduated by wealth — rich, middle, poor — roughly 48 / 24 / 12 dirhams/year. Less than the average Muslim's zakat + other taxes. Modern Muslim states have replaced jizya with general taxation.
Manner of collectionClassical strict view (some Shāfiʿīs): humiliating manner. Mainstream view (al-Mawardī, modern majority): respectful, like any tax — no humiliation in collection. Caliph ʿUmar II famously rebuked collectors who imposed humiliation.
Modern applicationMainstream contemporary scholars (Qaraḍāwī, al-Ghazali al-Ḥadīth, Ibn ʿĀshūr) hold that modern citizenship replaces the dhimma contract — all citizens pay equal taxes, and the protection-for-tax logic is no longer needed.
Related verses
Q 8:61 (incline to peace). Q 2:256 (no compulsion in religion). Q 60:8 (do not be aggressive to peaceful non-Muslims). Q 5:8 (justice even with those you dislike).
Quran 9:36
Sacred months Lunar calendar
إِنَّ عِدَّةَ الشُّهُورِ عِندَ اللَّهِ اثْنَا عَشَرَ شَهْرًا فِي كِتَابِ اللَّهِ يَوْمَ خَلَقَ السَّمَاوَاتِ وَالْأَرْضَ مِنْهَا أَرْبَعَةٌ حُرُمٌ ۚ ذَٰلِكَ الدِّينُ الْقَيِّمُ ۚ فَلَا تَظْلِمُوا فِيهِنَّ أَنفُسَكُمْ
"The number of months with Allah is twelve in the Book of Allah, since the day He created the heavens and the earth — four of them are sacred. That is the upright religion. So do not wrong yourselves during them."
Plain meaning
The Islamic year has twelve lunar months — established at creation, not by human convention. Four are sacred: Dhū al-Qaʿda, Dhū al-Ḥijja, Muḥarram, and Rajab. During these months, additional restraint is required — do not wrong yourselves. The verse also abolishes the pre-Islamic Arab practice of nasīʾ — arbitrarily shifting the sacred months to suit raid schedules.
Majority view
All four schools accept the lunar calendar as religious time. Acts of worship — Hajj, fasting, zakat-anniversary, ʿidda, marriage waiting periods — are calculated by lunar months. The four sacred months: combat is restricted (per classical rules, though many qualifications apply); sins committed during them are considered weightier; good deeds are multiplied. Rajab is particularly noted as the standalone sacred month — the other three are consecutive.
Related verses
Q 2:189 (the new moons are timing-markers for people and Hajj). Q 2:194 (the sacred month for the sacred month). Q 5:97 (Allah made the Kaʿba, the sacred month, the sacrificial animal — a benefit for people).
Quran 9:60
Zakat recipients Eight categories
إِنَّمَا الصَّدَقَاتُ لِلْفُقَرَاءِ وَالْمَسَاكِينِ وَالْعَامِلِينَ عَلَيْهَا وَالْمُؤَلَّفَةِ قُلُوبُهُمْ وَفِي الرِّقَابِ وَالْغَارِمِينَ وَفِي سَبِيلِ اللَّهِ وَابْنِ السَّبِيلِ ۖ فَرِيضَةً مِّنَ اللَّهِ ۗ وَاللَّهُ عَلِيمٌ حَكِيمٌ
"Zakat is only for: (1) the poor (fuqarāʾ), (2) the needy (masākīn), (3) those employed to administer it, (4) those whose hearts are to be reconciled, (5) freeing slaves, (6) those in debt, (7) in the way of Allah, and (8) the wayfarer — an obligation from Allah. Allah is all-knowing, wise."
Plain meaning
The definitive verse on zakat distribution — listing the eight and only eight legitimate categories of recipients. Word "innamā" ("only") makes the list exclusive: zakat may not be given outside these categories. Modern application: zakat to mosques is not directly named — but falls under fī sabīl Allāh per most schools' broad reading.
Majority view
All four schools accept the eight categories as exhaustive. Fuqarāʾ (poor with little/no income) and masākīn (needy with insufficient income) are the highest priority. ʿĀmilīn = zakat collectors/administrators (their salary comes from collected zakat). Muʾallafa qulūbuhum = new Muslims being supported, or community leaders being kept friendly. Riqāb = freeing slaves (today: extending to ransoming captives, freeing those in trafficking). Ghārimīn = those in unmanageable debt. Fī sabīl Allāh = literally "in Allah's way" — classical: jihad fighters; modern: any major public good (education, health, religious work). Ibn al-sabīl = stranded travelers.
Where the schools differ
Distribution methodShāfiʿī: all eight categories must receive some share — strict view. Other schools: the Imām/giver may give to any subset of the eight based on need.
"Muʾallafa qulūbuhum"ʿUmar suspended this category after Islam became strong. Ḥanafī follows ʿUmar — category lapsed. Mālikī/Shāfiʿī/Ḥanbalī: still active when needed.
"Fī sabīl Allāh" scopeClassical strict: only jihad. Modern majority (al-Qaraḍāwī, ʿAbduh): includes all public benefit — schools, hospitals, mosques, daʿwa, scholarly work.
Related verses
Q 2:177 (giving wealth to relatives, orphans, the needy, wayfarers, beggars, and for freeing slaves). Q 9:103 (zakat purifies). Q 2:267 (give of the good of what you have earned).
Quran 9:71
Believing men and women as allies Mutual ethical duty
وَالْمُؤْمِنُونَ وَالْمُؤْمِنَاتُ بَعْضُهُمْ أَوْلِيَاءُ بَعْضٍ ۚ يَأْمُرُونَ بِالْمَعْرُوفِ وَيَنْهَوْنَ عَنِ الْمُنكَرِ وَيُقِيمُونَ الصَّلَاةَ وَيُؤْتُونَ الزَّكَاةَ وَيُطِيعُونَ اللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُ
"The believing men and women — they are awliyāʾ (allies/supporters) of one another. They enjoin what is right, forbid what is wrong, establish prayer, give zakat, and obey Allah and His Messenger."
Plain meaning
The mirror image of Q 9:67 (which describes hypocrites as mutual allies in evil). Here, believing men and women are described as mutual allies in five concrete acts: (1) enjoining good, (2) forbidding wrong, (3) establishing prayer, (4) giving zakat, (5) obeying Allah and His Messenger. Note the equality: men and women share all five duties — including the public-facing duty of enjoining good.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the religious obligation of enjoining good and forbidding wrong applies equally to women — they are not exempt; (2) women may participate in religious teaching, scholarship, and public ethical voice — the verse explicitly includes them; (3) the basis of mutual support among Muslims — financial, emotional, social — as a religious obligation. Modern application: Muslim women's involvement in public ethical and educational work is fully Quranically grounded.
Related verses
Q 3:104 (a community calling to good). Q 3:110 (best community — commanding good, forbidding wrong). Q 33:35 (Muslim men and Muslim women, believing men and women — Allah has prepared forgiveness and great reward for them).
Quran 9:103
Zakat purifies Wealth purification
خُذْ مِنْ أَمْوَالِهِمْ صَدَقَةً تُطَهِّرُهُمْ وَتُزَكِّيهِم بِهَا وَصَلِّ عَلَيْهِمْ ۖ إِنَّ صَلَاتَكَ سَكَنٌ لَّهُمْ
"Take from their wealth a charity by which you purify (tuṭahhiruhum) and sanctify (tuzakkīhim) them, and pray for them — your prayer is a comfort to them."
Plain meaning
The verse gives the theology behind zakat — the word itself comes from this verse: tuzakkīhim, "purify/sanctify them". Zakat is not merely a tax. It does two things: (1) purifies the wealth — removing the spiritual taint of the portion that, withheld, would corrupt the whole; (2) purifies the giver — removing avarice from the heart. The verse also commands the state authority to pray for those who pay zakat — implying gratitude and respect, not condescension.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the state authority has the right to collect zakat — it's not purely voluntary or private; (2) zakat is owed on specific categories of wealth (gold/silver, agriculture, livestock, business merchandise, modern money equivalents) once they exceed niṣāb for one year; (3) the act has a spiritual dimension beyond redistribution.
Related verses
Q 2:267 (give of the good — not the bad). Q 9:60 (eight categories of recipients). Q 30:39 (zakat seeking Allah's face is multiplied; ribā for personal gain does not grow with Allah).
Quran 9:111
Lives and wealth for Paradise Divine purchase
إِنَّ اللَّهَ اشْتَرَىٰ مِنَ الْمُؤْمِنِينَ أَنفُسَهُمْ وَأَمْوَالَهُم بِأَنَّ لَهُمُ الْجَنَّةَ ۚ يُقَاتِلُونَ فِي سَبِيلِ اللَّهِ فَيَقْتُلُونَ وَيُقْتَلُونَ ۖ وَعْدًا عَلَيْهِ حَقًّا فِي التَّوْرَاةِ وَالْإِنجِيلِ وَالْقُرْآنِ ۚ وَمَنْ أَوْفَىٰ بِعَهْدِهِ مِنَ اللَّهِ ۚ فَاسْتَبْشِرُوا بِبَيْعِكُمُ الَّذِي بَايَعْتُم بِهِ ۚ وَذَٰلِكَ هُوَ الْفَوْزُ الْعَظِيمُ
"Allah has purchased from the believers their lives and their wealth in exchange for Paradise. They fight in Allah's way — they kill and are killed. A binding promise in the Torah, the Gospel, and the Quran. Who is more faithful to his covenant than Allah? Rejoice in the bargain you have made — that is the supreme triumph."
Plain meaning
The Quran uses commercial language: Allah buys the believers' lives and wealth, paying with Paradise. This frames the believer's entire life as already sold. The verse acknowledges the original context (combat in Allah's way) but the principle extends beyond: every act of service is part of the "purchased" life. The Quran lists this promise in three scriptures — emphasizing its universality across Abrahamic traditions.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the spiritual framing of commitment as completed transaction — the believer doesn't still own their life and wealth, they've been spent; (2) the consolation that the bargain is binding — Allah does not break covenants; (3) the broader application — fighting in Allah's way is one form of payment, but striving in education, charity, justice, and family duty also count. The verse pairs with Q 4:74 (sell the life of this world for the Hereafter).
Related verses
Q 4:74 (sell the worldly life for the Hereafter). Q 61:10–13 (a trade that saves from a painful punishment). Q 2:207 (some sell themselves seeking Allah's pleasure). Q 35:29 (those who trade their wealth and selves for what does not perish).
Quran 9:119
Be with the truthful Companionship
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا اتَّقُوا اللَّهَ وَكُونُوا مَعَ الصَّادِقِينَ
"O believers, be mindful of Allah and be with the truthful (al-ṣādiqīn)."
Plain meaning
A short, dense command. Two pillars of a believer's life: (1) inward taqwā — God-consciousness; (2) outward companionship with the truthful. One's chosen company shapes one's spiritual trajectory. The verse is plural — be with a community of the truthful, not isolated in supposed superiority.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the obligation of seeking righteous company — friends, scholars, mentors, communities — who anchor one in faith; (2) the warning against staying close to those who normalize sin or weaken faith. The Prophet ﷺ: "A person is on the religion of his close friend, so let each of you look at whom he befriends" (Abū Dāwūd, Tirmidhī). Modern application: choice of social circles, media intake, and online communities all fall under this verse.
Related verses
Q 5:35 (seek a wasīla). Q 18:28 (keep yourself patient with those who call upon their Lord). Q 25:27–28 (woe to me, would that I had not taken so-and-so as a friend!). Q 43:67 (close friends on that Day are enemies — except the God-conscious).
Quran 9:122
Learning religion Specialization
وَمَا كَانَ الْمُؤْمِنُونَ لِيَنفِرُوا كَافَّةً ۚ فَلَوْلَا نَفَرَ مِن كُلِّ فِرْقَةٍ مِّنْهُمْ طَائِفَةٌ لِّيَتَفَقَّهُوا فِي الدِّينِ وَلِيُنذِرُوا قَوْمَهُمْ إِذَا رَجَعُوا إِلَيْهِمْ لَعَلَّهُمْ يَحْذَرُونَ
"It is not for the believers all to go out (to fight). Why should not a group from every faction go out, so that they may gain understanding of the religion, and warn their people when they return — so that they may take heed?"
Plain meaning
After verses commanding combat in Tabūk, this verse provides a vital balance: not everyone goes to fight. A group must remain to study religion (tafaqquh) and then teach their people. The implication: jihād is one form of service; seeking knowledge is also a binding service to the community.
Majority view
All four schools take this verse as the foundational text for the obligation of ṭalab al-ʿilm — seeking sacred knowledge — being a communal obligation (farḍ kifāya). Every Muslim community must produce a sufficient number of scholars. The verse also establishes the bidirectional cycle: students leave for study, then return to teach their communities. This is the Quranic origin of the entire ṭalaba / scholar-circulation system in Islamic civilization.
Related verses
Q 39:9 (are those who know equal to those who do not know?). Q 35:28 (only the knowledgeable among His servants fear Allah). Q 58:11 (Allah will raise those who believe and those given knowledge in degrees).
Quran 9:128
Messenger's compassion From yourselves
لَقَدْ جَاءَكُمْ رَسُولٌ مِّنْ أَنفُسِكُمْ عَزِيزٌ عَلَيْهِ مَا عَنِتُّمْ حَرِيصٌ عَلَيْكُم بِالْمُؤْمِنِينَ رَءُوفٌ رَّحِيمٌ
"There has come to you a Messenger from among yourselves. What distresses you is grievous to him; he is concerned for you; with the believers he is kind and merciful."
Plain meaning
Among the last verses revealed. The Prophet ﷺ is described in four phrases: (1) from among yourselves — not from above, not foreign; (2) your suffering is heavy on him — he literally bore your pain; (3) concerned — invested in your wellbeing; (4) kind and merciful — the descriptors Allah uses for Himself are extended to His Messenger.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the foundational portrait of the Prophet ﷺ as a model of leadership — concerned, compassionate, accessible; (2) the standard against which any religious leader, scholar, or imam is measured; (3) the basis for treating the Prophet ﷺ with proper reverence — knowing how he loved his community. Two of Allah's own names (raʾūf, raḥīm) are extended to the Messenger in this single verse — a status not given to any other.
Related verses
Q 33:21 (in the Messenger of Allah is an excellent example). Q 33:56 (Allah and His angels send blessings on the Prophet). Q 21:107 (We have not sent you except as a mercy to all the worlds).
Quran 10:107
No one removes harm but Allah No one withholds bounty but He
وَإِن يَمْسَسْكَ اللَّهُ بِضُرٍّ فَلَا كَاشِفَ لَهُ إِلَّا هُوَ ۖ وَإِن يُرِدْكَ بِخَيْرٍ فَلَا رَادَّ لِفَضْلِهِ ۚ يُصِيبُ بِهِ مَن يَشَاءُ مِنْ عِبَادِهِ ۚ وَهُوَ الْغَفُورُ الرَّحِيمُ
"If Allah touches you with harm, none can remove it but He. If He intends good for you, none can withhold His bounty. He bestows it on whom He wills of His servants. He is the Forgiving, Merciful."
Plain meaning
A foundational tawḥīd verse on the source of harm and good. No human or angel or jinn can deliver harm Allah did not permit; none can withhold the good Allah intends. The verse closes — significantly — with Allah's mercy names. The intended effect: when harm strikes, turn directly to Allah without intermediary. When good comes, do not delude oneself that it came from human ability alone.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the prohibition of shirk in causality — believing that any created being can independently deliver harm or good; (2) the practical psychology of tawakkul (trust in Allah) — when in distress, the believer turns to the only One who can truly resolve it; (3) the inner peace that follows from this realization. The verse is paired with Q 6:17 (almost identical wording) — establishing the principle as foundational.
Related verses
Q 6:17 (if Allah touches you with harm — none can remove it but He). Q 35:2 (whatever mercy Allah opens, no one can withhold; whatever He withholds, no one can release). Q 65:3 (whoever trusts in Allah — He is sufficient for him).
Quran 11:114
Prayer at day's ends Good erases evil
وَأَقِمِ الصَّلَاةَ طَرَفَيِ النَّهَارِ وَزُلَفًا مِّنَ اللَّيْلِ ۚ إِنَّ الْحَسَنَاتِ يُذْهِبْنَ السَّيِّئَاتِ ۚ ذَٰلِكَ ذِكْرَىٰ لِلذَّاكِرِينَ
"Establish prayer at the two ends of the day and the approaches of the night. Indeed, good deeds erase evil deeds. That is a reminder for the mindful."
Plain meaning
A compact statement of prayer timing — the two ends of the day (Fajr and the afternoon prayers ʿAṣr/Maghrib) and approaches of the night (ʿIshāʾ). Revealed in the context of a man who had kissed a woman in the market and was deeply distressed; the Prophet ﷺ assured him that subsequent prayer erases such sins. The closing sentence — good deeds erase evil deeds — is one of the Quran's most consoling lines.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the obligation of the five daily prayers — supported by Q 2:238, Q 17:78, and the ascension ḥadīth specifying five; (2) the principle that good deeds erase minor sins (the major sins require specific repentance).
