The Prophet ﷺ: "The five daily prayers, Jumuʿa to Jumuʿa, and Ramadan to Ramadan — atone for the sins between them, as long as the major sins are avoided" (Muslim).
Related verses
Q 17:78 (establish prayer at the decline of the sun until the darkness of the night, and the Fajr recitation). Q 2:238 (preserve the prayers — especially the middle prayer). Q 20:14 (establish prayer for My remembrance). Q 4:103 (prayer is at fixed times for the believers).
Quran 12:53
Soul inclined to evil Mercy as exception
وَمَا أُبَرِّئُ نَفْسِي ۚ إِنَّ النَّفْسَ لَأَمَّارَةٌ بِالسُّوءِ إِلَّا مَا رَحِمَ رَبِّي ۚ إِنَّ رَبِّي غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ
"I do not exonerate myself. The soul is inclined to evil (ammāra bi-l-sūʾ) — except those upon whom my Lord has mercy. My Lord is forgiving, merciful."
Plain meaning
Spoken by Yūsuf (peace upon him) after the resolution of the affair with the wife of the Egyptian official. The most striking element: even Yūsuf — chastely tested and divinely protected — does not exonerate himself. The verse provides the foundational Quranic classification of the nafs: the lower self is ammāra bi-l-sūʾ — actively commanding wrongdoing — except where Allah's mercy intervenes.
Majority view
All four schools, and especially the spiritual tradition, cite this verse for the three-fold classification of the nafs: (1) nafs ammāra (12:53) — soul that commands evil; (2) nafs lawwāma (75:2) — the self-blaming, struggling soul; (3) nafs muṭmaʾinna (89:27) — the soul at peace. The journey of spiritual life is the migration from the first to the third. The verse also models realistic self-assessment — even the highly righteous do not declare themselves morally finished.
Related verses
Q 75:2 (the self-reproaching soul). Q 89:27 (O reassured soul — return to your Lord). Q 91:7–10 (a soul and He who proportioned it — inspired it with corruption and taqwā — successful is the one who purifies it).
Quran 13:11
Self-change Divine intervention
إِنَّ اللَّهَ لَا يُغَيِّرُ مَا بِقَوْمٍ حَتَّىٰ يُغَيِّرُوا مَا بِأَنفُسِهِمْ
"Indeed, Allah will not change the condition of a people until they change what is within themselves."
Plain meaning
A foundational principle of moral and historical causation. Divine assistance follows human effort. A community in decline will not be rescued without internal change — in values, conduct, structures. The phrase "mā bi-anfusihim" — "what is within themselves" — emphasizes inner moral state, not external circumstance.
Majority view
All four schools and the Islamic philosophy-of-history tradition cite this verse for: (1) the principle that responsibility for change rests with the community itself — blaming external enemies or "fate" is rejected; (2) revival of the Ummah requires reform within — moral, educational, institutional; (3) divine support is conditional upon the community's faithfulness. Ibn Khaldūn applied this verse extensively in his philosophy of civilizational rise and fall.
Related verses
Q 8:53 (Allah does not change a favor He bestowed on a people until they change what is in themselves). Q 47:7 (if you support Allah, He will support you). Q 22:40 (Allah will surely help those who help Him).
Quran 14:7
Gratitude Divine increase
وَإِذْ تَأَذَّنَ رَبُّكُمْ لَئِن شَكَرْتُمْ لَأَزِيدَنَّكُمْ ۖ وَلَئِن كَفَرْتُمْ إِنَّ عَذَابِي لَشَدِيدٌ
"When your Lord proclaimed: 'If you are grateful, I will surely increase you. But if you are ungrateful — My punishment is severe.'"
Plain meaning
A divine pledge with two clauses: gratitude triggers increase — both material and spiritual. Ingratitude (kufr — here, denial/rejection of blessing, not necessarily disbelief in faith) triggers severe consequence. The verse uses taʾadhdhana, a strong proclamation form — meaning Allah has solemnly declared this principle.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the religious obligation of shukr (thankfulness) — for health, wealth, family, faith, time, knowledge; (2) the mechanism of barakah — divine increase that follows gratitude; (3) the warning that repeated ingratitude for blessings may result in their removal. The Prophet ﷺ taught the duʿāʾ "O Allah, help me remember You, thank You, and worship You well" (Abū Dāwūd) — gratitude here paired with remembrance and worship.
Related verses
Q 31:12 (Luqmān: be grateful to Allah; whoever is grateful, it is for himself). Q 76:3 (We have shown them the way — grateful or ungrateful). Q 14:34 (verily, man is most unjust, ungrateful). Q 27:40 (Sulaymān: this is from my Lord's grace — whoever is grateful, it is for himself).
Quran 16:90
Comprehensive command Three goods, three evils
إِنَّ اللَّهَ يَأْمُرُ بِالْعَدْلِ وَالْإِحْسَانِ وَإِيتَاءِ ذِي الْقُرْبَىٰ وَيَنْهَىٰ عَنِ الْفَحْشَاءِ وَالْمُنكَرِ وَالْبَغْيِ ۚ يَعِظُكُمْ لَعَلَّكُمْ تَذَكَّرُونَ
"Allah commands justice (ʿadl), excellence (iḥsān), and giving to kin — and forbids indecency (faḥshāʾ), wrong (munkar), and aggression (baghy). He instructs you — that you may take heed."
Plain meaning
Often called "the most comprehensive verse in the Quran" — the early Caliph ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz had it read at the end of every Friday khuṭba. Three commanded virtues: (1) justice — giving each their due; (2) iḥsān — going beyond the minimum, doing the beautiful thing; (3) giving to kin — strengthening blood ties. Three forbidden vices: (1) indecency — gross moral violations; (2) munkar — what is recognized as wrong; (3) aggression — exceeding one's bounds against others.
Majority view
All four schools consider this verse a summary of the entire Sharīʿa. From it derive: (1) the entire ethics-of-government framework; (2) the concept that iḥsān is required, not just justice — meaning Islamic law sets a floor, not a ceiling, on conduct; (3) the centrality of kinship maintenance; (4) the universal categorization of all sins as faḥshāʾ (sexual/gross), munkar (general wrong), or baghy (transgression against others).
Related verses
Q 4:135 (stand firm in justice). Q 7:33 (forbidden: indecencies, sin, aggression, shirk, speaking about Allah without knowledge). Q 49:11–12 (prohibitions on mockery, suspicion, backbiting). Q 6:151–152 (the ten commandments of Al-Anʿām).
Quran 16:97
Good life Equal for men and women
مَنْ عَمِلَ صَالِحًا مِّن ذَكَرٍ أَوْ أُنثَىٰ وَهُوَ مُؤْمِنٌ فَلَنُحْيِيَنَّهُ حَيَاةً طَيِّبَةً ۖ وَلَنَجْزِيَنَّهُمْ أَجْرَهُم بِأَحْسَنِ مَا كَانُوا يَعْمَلُونَ
"Whoever does good — male or female — while a believer, We shall surely grant them a goodly life (ḥayāt ṭayyiba), and We shall reward them according to the best of what they used to do."
Plain meaning
A double promise. (1) ḥayāt ṭayyiba — a "good life" — promised in this world, before the Hereafter reward. Not necessarily wealthy or carefree, but inwardly content, blessed, meaningful; (2) full reward in the Hereafter, calculated by the best of one's deeds. The verse explicitly equates men and women in this promise — refusing any reading that limits spiritual reward by gender.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the principle that righteous action paired with faith produces both this-worldly blessing and Hereafter reward; (2) the gender equality of spiritual reward (paired with Q 33:35 and Q 4:124); (3) the concept of ḥayāt ṭayyiba — translated variously as "good life", "blessed life", "contented life" — understood as inner state more than material wealth. Modern application: this is the Quranic foundation for understanding why some practicing Muslims have inner peace amid hardship.
Related verses
Q 33:35 (ten-pair virtues with equal reward). Q 4:124 (whoever does good — male or female — and is a believer, will enter Paradise). Q 40:40 (whoever does righteousness — male or female — will enter Paradise).
Quran 16:125
Method of daʿwa Manner of debate
ادْعُ إِلَىٰ سَبِيلِ رَبِّكَ بِالْحِكْمَةِ وَالْمَوْعِظَةِ الْحَسَنَةِ ۖ وَجَادِلْهُم بِالَّتِي هِيَ أَحْسَنُ ۚ إِنَّ رَبَّكَ هُوَ أَعْلَمُ بِمَن ضَلَّ عَن سَبِيلِهِ وَهُوَ أَعْلَمُ بِالْمُهْتَدِينَ
"Call to the way of your Lord with wisdom, beautiful preaching, and reason with them in the best way. Your Lord is most knowing of who has strayed from His way and who is rightly guided."
Plain meaning
The verse legislating method of religious teaching and debate. Three levels of communication: (1) ḥikma — wisdom, for the intellectually receptive; (2) mawʿiẓa ḥasana — beautiful exhortation, for those moved by heart and conscience; (3) jadal — argumentation, for the resistant — but even this must be done in the best way. The verse closes by reminding the caller: the result is Allah's, not yours.
Majority view
All four schools and the entire Islamic tradition cite this verse as the manual for: (1) daʿwa (calling to Islam), (2) interfaith and intra-faith dialogue, (3) teaching and preaching style. From it: insulting, mocking, or attacking the opposition is forbidden — even in defense of truth. The verse models respect as inseparable from truth.
Related verses
Q 29:46 (do not argue with People of the Book except in the best way). Q 41:34 (repel evil with what is better). Q 25:63 (the servants of the Most Merciful — when the ignorant address them, they say "peace"). Q 2:256 (no compulsion in religion).
Quran 17:9
Most upright way Quran guides
إِنَّ هَٰذَا الْقُرْآنَ يَهْدِي لِلَّتِي هِيَ أَقْوَمُ وَيُبَشِّرُ الْمُؤْمِنِينَ الَّذِينَ يَعْمَلُونَ الصَّالِحَاتِ أَنَّ لَهُمْ أَجْرًا كَبِيرًا
"Indeed, this Quran guides to that which is most upright (aqwam) and gives glad tidings to the believers who do good — that for them is a great reward."
Plain meaning
A precise self-description of the Quran. It is not just "a guide" but a guide to aqwam — the most upright, most stable, most balanced. The verse implies: every legal, ethical, and spiritual question has multiple possible answers; the Quran consistently points to the most upright among them.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the foundational principle that the Quran is sufficient guide — any legitimate Sharīʿa-based ruling traces back to it directly or through Sunnah-as-elaboration; (2) the principle that Islamic law tends toward the balanced middle, not extreme positions — the Prophet ﷺ described his community as ummatan wasaṭā (a balanced community, Q 2:143); (3) the framing of religious life as ascending toward stable equilibrium, not zealous extremism.
Related verses
Q 2:143 (We made you a balanced community). Q 6:153 (this is My straight path — follow it). Q 1:6 (guide us to the straight path). Q 36:4 (you are on a straight path).
Quran 17:11
Man's haste Prays for evil thinking it good
وَيَدْعُ الْإِنسَانُ بِالشَّرِّ دُعَاءَهُ بِالْخَيْرِ ۖ وَكَانَ الْإِنسَانُ عَجُولًا
"Man prays for evil as he prays for good. Man is hasty (ʿajūl)."
Plain meaning
A short and striking verse on the dangers of haste. People often pray for things — for their child's success, for a particular spouse, for wealth — that may, in the long run, harm them. Allah, in His knowledge, sometimes withholds answers for the supplicant's sake. The verse names the underlying problem: ʿajūl — the human tendency to want everything now, before understanding consequences.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the wisdom in unanswered prayers — sometimes the highest mercy is "no" or "wait"; (2) the practical etiquette of duʿāʾ: ask Allah for what is best, even if you don't know what that is; (3) the broader principle that haste is a destructive trait in religion, finance, marriage, and decision-making. Pairs with Q 70:19 ("man was created impatient") and Q 41:51 (when good touches him he turns away, when harm touches him he is full of prayer).
Related verses
Q 21:37 (man was created from haste — soon I will show you My signs, so do not seek to hasten them). Q 70:19 (man was created impatient). Q 41:49 (man is not weary of praying for good).
Quran 17:23–24
Parents Iḥsān
وَقَضَىٰ رَبُّكَ أَلَّا تَعْبُدُوا إِلَّا إِيَّاهُ وَبِالْوَالِدَيْنِ إِحْسَانًا ۚ إِمَّا يَبْلُغَنَّ عِندَكَ الْكِبَرَ أَحَدُهُمَا أَوْ كِلَاهُمَا فَلَا تَقُل لَّهُمَا أُفٍّ وَلَا تَنْهَرْهُمَا وَقُل لَّهُمَا قَوْلًا كَرِيمًا ۝ وَاخْفِضْ لَهُمَا جَنَاحَ الذُّلِّ مِنَ الرَّحْمَةِ وَقُل رَّبِّ ارْحَمْهُمَا كَمَا رَبَّيَانِي صَغِيرًا
"Your Lord has decreed: worship none but Him, and be excellent (iḥsān) to parents. If one or both of them reaches old age with you, do not say to them 'uff' (the smallest expression of frustration), do not rebuke them, but speak to them noble words. Lower for them the wing of humility from mercy, and say: 'My Lord, have mercy on them as they raised me when I was small.'"
Plain meaning
Two of the most demanding verses in the Quran on parental rights. The connection between worshipping Allah alone and excellence to parents is striking — they are placed as the two principal commands. The bar is set astonishingly high: not even "uff" — the smallest sound of irritation — is permitted. Even in old age when parents may be difficult, the response is honor, humility, and prayer for them.
Majority view
All four schools cite these verses for: (1) birr al-wālidayn — kindness to parents — as one of the most binding duties after tawḥīd; (2) obligation to financially support aged parents if needed and one is able; (3) prohibition of verbal harshness, mockery, or neglect toward parents — even non-Muslim parents per Q 31:14–15. The Prophet ﷺ named "ʿuqūq al-wālidayn" (disobedience to parents) among the greatest sins (Bukhārī, Muslim).
Where the schools differ
Limits of obedienceAll four schools: parents are not to be obeyed in clear sin (Q 31:15) — but maintaining the relationship and being kind to them is still obligatory. Particularly: no compulsion against one's choice of spouse if there is no shariʿa-based reason.
Financial supportAll schools: an adult child with means must support parents in genuine need. This is enforced by judicial action in classical fiqh.
Related verses
Q 31:14 (carried in weakness upon weakness — be grateful to Me and your parents). Q 2:215 (give to parents, kin, orphans, needy, wayfarers). Q 46:15 (mother bore him with hardship and delivered him with hardship). Q 4:36 (be good to parents — opening verse-list of duties).
Quran 17:26–27
Give kin/poor No wastefulness
وَآتِ ذَا الْقُرْبَىٰ حَقَّهُ وَالْمِسْكِينَ وَابْنَ السَّبِيلِ وَلَا تُبَذِّرْ تَبْذِيرًا ۝ إِنَّ الْمُبَذِّرِينَ كَانُوا إِخْوَانَ الشَّيَاطِينِ ۖ وَكَانَ الشَّيْطَانُ لِرَبِّهِ كَفُورًا
"Give the relative his right, and the needy, and the wayfarer — and do not squander wastefully. The squanderers are brothers of the devils, and Satan is ungrateful to his Lord."
Plain meaning
Three categories receive a right from one's wealth: (1) the close kin, (2) the needy, (3) the wayfarer (stranded traveler). The verse pointedly calls these their right — not a favor. Then a sharp condemnation of tabdhīr (squandering, profligate waste) — squanderers are called "brothers of the devils". Wealth wasted on vanity is wealth withheld from those it was meant to reach.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) ranking of charity priorities — close kin first (the Prophet ﷺ: "the best charity is upon a relative who harbors enmity"); (2) the principle that tabdhīr = spending on what is haram, OR spending excessively on things that don't benefit; (3) the strong moral condemnation of wasteful expenditure — even on lawful items, if it crosses into excess.
Related verses
Q 25:67 (neither extravagant nor stingy — balanced). Q 7:31 (eat, drink, do not be wasteful). Q 2:177 (true piety includes giving wealth to relatives, orphans, needy, wayfarers).
Quran 17:32
Zinā prohibited Approach forbidden
وَلَا تَقْرَبُوا الزِّنَىٰ ۖ إِنَّهُ كَانَ فَاحِشَةً وَسَاءَ سَبِيلًا
"Do not approach zinā. It is an indecency and an evil way."
Plain meaning
The wording is precise — do not approach (lā taqrabū) rather than "do not commit". The Quran often uses this wider phrasing for sins that grow from gradual proximity: the prohibition includes anything that leads to zinā — private seclusion with non-maḥrams (khalwa), suggestive flirtation, indulging visual desire. Classical scholars derive from this verse the principle of sadd al-dharāʾiʿ — blocking the routes to a sin.
Majority view
All four schools take this verse as the moral and legal framework for the laws of ʿawra (covering), gaze restraint (Q 24:30–31), modesty in dress, and prohibition of seclusion with non-maḥrams. Faḥshāʾ (indecency) and sūʾ sabīl (evil way) describe both the act and its consequences — broken families, uncertain lineage, exploitation. The verse's preventive framing distinguishes Islamic law's approach from a purely punitive one.
Related verses
Q 24:2 (the ḥadd for zinā). Q 24:30–31 (gaze and modesty). Q 33:32–33 (do not speak with softness, stay in your homes — addressed first to wives of the Prophet, then in principle to all). Q 25:68 (those who do not commit zinā — qualities of true servants).
Quran 17:33–34
No killing without right Orphan's property Fulfilling promises
وَلَا تَقْتُلُوا النَّفْسَ الَّتِي حَرَّمَ اللَّهُ إِلَّا بِالْحَقِّ ۗ وَمَن قُتِلَ مَظْلُومًا فَقَدْ جَعَلْنَا لِوَلِيِّهِ سُلْطَانًا فَلَا يُسْرِف فِّي الْقَتْلِ ۖ إِنَّهُ كَانَ مَنصُورًا ۝ وَلَا تَقْرَبُوا مَالَ الْيَتِيمِ إِلَّا بِالَّتِي هِيَ أَحْسَنُ حَتَّىٰ يَبْلُغَ أَشُدَّهُ ۚ وَأَوْفُوا بِالْعَهْدِ ۖ إِنَّ الْعَهْدَ كَانَ مَسْئُولًا
"Do not kill the soul Allah has forbidden, except by right. Whoever is killed unjustly — We have given authority to his heir; but let him not be excessive in killing. He shall be helped. Do not approach the orphan's property except in the best way until he reaches maturity. Fulfill every promise — the promise will be asked about."
Plain meaning
Three related rulings: (1) sanctity of life — killing is forbidden except by lawful right (qiṣāṣ, ḥadd, legitimate defense); (2) heir's authority in qiṣāṣ — the victim's family has the legal standing to demand justice, but they must not exceed proportion (no torture, no killing the criminal's family); (3) orphan's property — guardians must manage it in the best way, releasing it at maturity; (4) promise-keeping — every promise will be questioned about on the Day of Judgment.
Majority view
All four schools cite these verses for: (1) qiṣāṣ proceduralism — the state, not the family, executes; the family decides whether to demand or pardon; (2) iṣrāf (excess) in killing is forbidden — meaning collective punishment, killing innocents, mutilation; (3) the entire fiqh of walāya (guardianship over minors' wealth); (4) ʿahd covers everything from sales to marriage to international treaties.
Related verses
Q 5:32 (whoever kills a soul, as if killed all mankind). Q 2:178 (qiṣāṣ established). Q 4:6 (test orphans, release at maturity). Q 5:1 (fulfill contracts). Q 16:91 (do not break oaths after confirmation).
Quran 17:35
Full measure Honest weight
وَأَوْفُوا الْكَيْلَ إِذَا كِلْتُمْ وَزِنُوا بِالْقِسْطَاسِ الْمُسْتَقِيمِ ۚ ذَٰلِكَ خَيْرٌ وَأَحْسَنُ تَأْوِيلًا
"Give full measure when you measure, and weigh with the upright balance. That is better and more excellent in outcome."
Plain meaning
A short, direct command on economic honesty. Two requirements: (1) when measuring out goods (volume), give the full amount; (2) when weighing, use a true balance. The verse ends with the promise: this is "better and more excellent in outcome" — meaning honest commerce builds long-term trust and prosperity, while deceit destroys both.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) prohibition of taṭfīf (cheating in measure or weight) — addressed at length in Sūrat al-Muṭaffifīn (83); (2) regulation of markets — historically, the muḥtasib (market inspector) was a state office, charged with verifying scales and measures; (3) extends in modern application to: false advertising, deceptive product descriptions, hidden fees, weight/volume manipulation in packaging. The principle is universal honesty in any quantifiable transaction.
Related verses
Q 83:1–3 (woe to those who give less when they take but full when they give). Q 6:152 (give full measure and weight in justice). Q 11:84–86 (Shuʿayb's people warned about the same). Q 55:9 (establish weight in justice, do not falsify the balance).
Quran 17:53
Best speech Satan sows discord
وَقُل لِّعِبَادِي يَقُولُوا الَّتِي هِيَ أَحْسَنُ ۚ إِنَّ الشَّيْطَانَ يَنزَغُ بَيْنَهُمْ ۚ إِنَّ الشَّيْطَانَ كَانَ لِلْإِنسَانِ عَدُوًّا مُّبِينًا
"Tell My servants to say what is best (al-latī hiya aḥsan). Satan sows discord between them. Satan is an open enemy to humankind."
Plain meaning
A short verse with practical weight. Allah commands believers to speak the best available phrasing — not the merely acceptable. Why? Because words become entry points: Satan exploits provocative speech to sow conflict (nazgha). Choosing better phrasing is not aesthetic — it's protective.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the obligation to choose kinder, less inflammatory phrasing — even when stating true things; (2) the recognition that provocation creates space for satanic exploitation in human relations; (3) the linguistic ethic that pairs with Q 49:11–12 (no mockery, suspicion, backbiting) — together they establish what we'd today call nonviolent communication. Modern application: in family, workplace, public discourse — choosing best phrasing is religious obligation, not optional politeness.
Related verses
Q 2:83 (speak to people in goodly speech). Q 4:5 (speak to them words of kindness). Q 33:70 (speak words of truth — Allah will rectify your deeds). Q 41:34 (repel evil with what is better).
Quran 17:70
Honored children of Adam Human dignity
وَلَقَدْ كَرَّمْنَا بَنِي آدَمَ وَحَمَلْنَاهُمْ فِي الْبَرِّ وَالْبَحْرِ وَرَزَقْنَاهُم مِّنَ الطَّيِّبَاتِ وَفَضَّلْنَاهُمْ عَلَىٰ كَثِيرٍ مِّمَّنْ خَلَقْنَا تَفْضِيلًا
"We have honored the children of Adam — carried them on land and sea, provided them with good things, and preferred them above many We created with great preference."
Plain meaning
A foundational statement on universal human dignity. Notice the wording: "children of Adam" — not "believers", not "Muslims", not "the righteous". The honor is conferred on all humanity by virtue of being human. Three forms named: (1) given the capacity for mobility on land and sea; (2) provided with the wholesome things of creation; (3) preferred over many other beings.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the principle that all human beings — believer or not — have intrinsic dignity (karāma) by virtue of their humanity; (2) the legal-ethical basis for the universal prohibitions on torture, slavery (gradually phased out), mistreatment of bodies (dead or alive), and degrading treatment; (3) the framework for Muslim human-rights advocacy. Sayyid Quṭb noted: this verse alone refutes any reading of Islam as tribal or sectarian — human dignity precedes religious classification.
Related verses
Q 95:4 (We created man in the best stature). Q 32:9 (gave you hearing, sight, and hearts). Q 2:30 (the placement of humanity as Allah's khalīfa). Q 49:13 (made you peoples and tribes to know one another).
Quran 17:80–81
Truthful entry/exit Truth vs falsehood
وَقُل رَّبِّ أَدْخِلْنِي مُدْخَلَ صِدْقٍ وَأَخْرِجْنِي مُخْرَجَ صِدْقٍ وَاجْعَل لِّي مِن لَّدُنكَ سُلْطَانًا نَّصِيرًا ۝ وَقُلْ جَاءَ الْحَقُّ وَزَهَقَ الْبَاطِلُ ۚ إِنَّ الْبَاطِلَ كَانَ زَهُوقًا
"And say: 'My Lord, cause me to enter a truthful entry and exit me with a truthful exit, and grant me from Your presence a supporting authority.' And say: 'Truth has come, and falsehood has vanished. Surely, falsehood is bound to vanish.'"
Plain meaning
Two prayers paired. (1) Mudkhal ṣidq / mukhraj ṣidq: every entry (into a place, role, situation, conversation) and every exit be one of truthfulness — not contrived, not deceptive. (2) Truth has come, falsehood has vanished: the Prophet ﷺ recited this verse at the conquest of Makkah as he destroyed the idols around the Kaʿba; falsehood, however entrenched, cannot endure when truth arrives with full force.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the supplication of truthfulness in every transition of life — beginning a job, marriage, journey, public role — with the goal of integrity throughout; (2) the historical principle that falsehood, however dominant, has an expiration date — truth eventually overthrows it; (3) the model duʿāʾ for the Prophet ﷺ in Hijra (entering Madina, leaving Makkah). Many recite Q 17:80 as a duʿāʾ for travel, new endeavor, or transition.
Related verses
Q 21:18 (We hurl truth against falsehood and it destroys it — and falsehood is gone). Q 34:49 (truth has come — falsehood does not begin or repeat). Q 17:105 (We sent down the Quran with truth — and with truth it descended).
Quran 18:23–24
In shāʾ Allāh Humility about the future
وَلَا تَقُولَنَّ لِشَيْءٍ إِنِّي فَاعِلٌ ذَٰلِكَ غَدًا ۝ إِلَّا أَن يَشَاءَ اللَّهُ ۚ وَاذْكُر رَّبَّكَ إِذَا نَسِيتَ وَقُلْ عَسَىٰ أَن يَهْدِيَنِ رَبِّي لِأَقْرَبَ مِنْ هَٰذَا رَشَدًا
"Do not say of anything: 'I will surely do this tomorrow' — except (saying) 'if Allah wills' (in shāʾ Allāh). And remember your Lord if you forget, and say: 'Perhaps my Lord will guide me to what is nearer to right than this.'"
Plain meaning
A direct command on the linguistic-spiritual habit of conditional speech about the future. Revealed when the Prophet ﷺ promised the next day's answer about the Companions of the Cave without saying in shāʾ Allāh — and the revelation was delayed in correction. The verse establishes: human plans are conditional on divine will. Saying in shāʾ Allāh when speaking of future intent is a binding etiquette.
Majority view
All four schools: saying in shāʾ Allāh for future statements is sunnah muʾakkadah — strongly emphasized practice. Saying it on oaths has a specific legal effect: if one swears "by Allah I will do X — in shāʾ Allāh", breaking the oath does not incur kaffāra. The verse also commands: if you forgot to say it, mention Allah whenever you remember and ask for guidance to "what is nearer to right".
Related verses
Q 37:102 (Ibrāhīm's son: you will find me, in shāʾ Allāh, among the patient). Q 12:99 (enter Egypt, in shāʾ Allāh, in safety). Q 48:27 (you will surely enter the Sacred Mosque — in shāʾ Allāh — in safety).
Quran 18:46
Lasting good deeds Wealth and children
الْمَالُ وَالْبَنُونَ زِينَةُ الْحَيَاةِ الدُّنْيَا ۖ وَالْبَاقِيَاتُ الصَّالِحَاتُ خَيْرٌ عِندَ رَبِّكَ ثَوَابًا وَخَيْرٌ أَمَلًا
"Wealth and children are the adornment of the worldly life. But the lasting good deeds (al-bāqiyāt al-ṣāliḥāt) are better with your Lord in reward, and better in (founding) hope."
Plain meaning
A short verse contrasting two kinds of value: (1) wealth and children — real zīna (adornment) but temporary; (2) al-bāqiyāt al-ṣāliḥāt — "the lasting good deeds" — better both in reward AND in hope (what one can confidently anticipate). The Prophet ﷺ specified that al-bāqiyāt al-ṣāliḥāt includes the four phrases: subḥān Allāh, al-ḥamdu li-llāh, lā ilāha illā Allāh, Allāhu akbar.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the broader category of lasting good deeds — acts whose benefit continues after the doer dies: ongoing charity (ṣadaqa jāriya), beneficial knowledge taught, righteous children making duʿāʾ for parents; (2) the spiritual dhikr phrases as preeminent examples; (3) the practical guidance on where to invest: not entirely in wealth-accumulation or even child-raising for status, but in deeds whose value endures past death. The Prophet ﷺ named three things that survive a person: ongoing charity, beneficial knowledge, and righteous offspring who pray for them.
Related verses
Q 19:76 (the lasting good deeds are best in reward). Q 102:1–2 (the rivalry in accumulation diverted you). Q 28:60 (what is with Allah is better and more lasting). Q 16:96 (what is with you ends; what is with Allah remains).
Quran 20:114
Increase me in knowledge Patience with revelation
فَتَعَالَى اللَّهُ الْمَلِكُ الْحَقُّ ۗ وَلَا تَعْجَلْ بِالْقُرْآنِ مِن قَبْلِ أَن يُقْضَىٰ إِلَيْكَ وَحْيُهُ ۖ وَقُل رَّبِّ زِدْنِي عِلْمًا
"Exalted is Allah, the True Sovereign. Do not hasten with the Quran before its revelation is completed to you. Say: 'My Lord, increase me in knowledge.'"
Plain meaning
Three elements: (1) Allah's sovereign exaltation; (2) command to the Prophet ﷺ not to rush with the Quran before its revelation is complete — patience in receiving and conveying; (3) the only thing the Quran commands the Prophet ﷺ to ask Allah to increase him in is knowledge. Not wealth, not power, not status — but ʿilm.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the religious obligation to seek knowledge — the only thing Allah commanded the Prophet ﷺ himself to ask for additional measure of; (2) the etiquette of patience in receiving teaching — not rushing ahead of what is being taught; (3) the foundational duʿāʾ "Rabbi zidnī ʿilman" — recommended after every learning session, before every study, throughout the seeking of knowledge.
Related verses
Q 96:1–5 (read in the name of your Lord — He taught man what he knew not). Q 35:28 (only those of knowledge fear Allah). Q 39:9 (are those who know equal to those who do not know?). Q 75:16–17 (do not move your tongue with it to hasten it — its collection and recitation is upon Us).
Quran 21:107
Mercy to the worlds Mission of Prophet
وَمَا أَرْسَلْنَاكَ إِلَّا رَحْمَةً لِّلْعَالَمِينَ
"We have not sent you except as a mercy to all the worlds (al-ʿālamīn)."
Plain meaning
A single short verse that defines the Prophet's ﷺ entire mission. Mercy — not just guidance, not just warning, not just law — but raḥma as the unifying purpose. The scope is universal: al-ʿālamīn — all worlds, all peoples, all times. The verse rules out exclusive or sectarian readings of the Prophet's ﷺ mission.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the universal scope of the Prophet's ﷺ message — not just for Arabs, not just for Muslims, but for all humanity (and all of creation in some interpretations); (2) the framing of every Sharīʿa command as ultimately a vehicle of raḥma — even commands that appear restrictive are understood as mercy in disguise; (3) the model for those who follow him: they too must be agents of mercy in the world. Modern application: this verse is central to Muslim peacebuilding, humanitarian work, and inter-civilizational engagement.
Related verses
Q 9:128 (the Messenger is concerned for you, kind, merciful). Q 7:156 (My mercy encompasses all things). Q 33:46 (a caller to Allah by His permission, a luminous lamp).
Quran 22:39–40
First permission to fight Self-defense Protecting houses of worship
أُذِنَ لِلَّذِينَ يُقَاتَلُونَ بِأَنَّهُمْ ظُلِمُوا ۚ وَإِنَّ اللَّهَ عَلَىٰ نَصْرِهِمْ لَقَدِيرٌ ۝ الَّذِينَ أُخْرِجُوا مِن دِيَارِهِم بِغَيْرِ حَقٍّ إِلَّا أَن يَقُولُوا رَبُّنَا اللَّهُ ۗ وَلَوْلَا دَفْعُ اللَّهِ النَّاسَ بَعْضَهُم بِبَعْضٍ لَّهُدِّمَتْ صَوَامِعُ وَبِيَعٌ وَصَلَوَاتٌ وَمَسَاجِدُ يُذْكَرُ فِيهَا اسْمُ اللَّهِ كَثِيرًا
"Permission (to fight) is given to those upon whom war is made, because they were wronged ... those who were driven from their homes without right, only because they said 'Our Lord is Allah.' Had Allah not driven back some people by others, monasteries, churches, synagogues, and mosques — in which Allah's name is much remembered — would have been destroyed."
Plain meaning
The first verse permitting Muslims to take up arms. Crucially, it is defensive: upon whom war is made. The grounds are explicit: wronged, driven from homes, persecuted for faith. The verse closes with a stunning statement: protecting all houses of worship — including Christian monasteries (ṣawāmiʿ), Christian churches (biyaʿ), Jewish places of prayer (ṣalawāt), and mosques — is the higher purpose of legitimate combat.
Majority view
All four schools take this verse as the foundational text for Islamic jus ad bellum: war is permitted, not initiated. The grounds named — being wronged, being expelled, being persecuted for faith — are the only legitimate triggers. The verse equates protection of Jewish and Christian places of worship with protection of mosques — establishing the universal sanctity of religious sites. Classical jurists (Ibn Taymiyya prominently): "Allah supports the just state even if non-Muslim, and overthrows the unjust state even if Muslim."
Related verses
Q 2:190 (fight in Allah's way those who fight you — do not transgress). Q 2:193 (fight until persecution ceases). Q 8:61 (incline to peace if they do). Q 60:8 (Allah does not forbid kindness to those who do not fight you).
Quran 22:78
No undue hardship Religion of Abraham Striving
وَجَاهِدُوا فِي اللَّهِ حَقَّ جِهَادِهِ ۚ هُوَ اجْتَبَاكُمْ وَمَا جَعَلَ عَلَيْكُمْ فِي الدِّينِ مِنْ حَرَجٍ ۚ مِّلَّةَ أَبِيكُمْ إِبْرَاهِيمَ ۚ هُوَ سَمَّاكُمُ الْمُسْلِمِينَ مِن قَبْلُ وَفِي هَٰذَا
"Strive in Allah as is His due. He has chosen you and has not placed upon you in religion any difficulty (ḥaraj). It is the religion of your father Abraham. He named you Muslims before and in this (Quran)."
Plain meaning
Three statements in one verse: (1) jihād in its broadest sense — striving for Allah in every dimension of life; (2) Islam contains no undue hardship (ḥaraj) — meaning whenever a ruling would cause unbearable difficulty, the Sharīʿa itself provides ease; (3) Muslim identity is rooted in Abraham — not a new religion but a return to the original.
Majority view
All four schools and the entire science of uṣūl al-fiqh cite this verse for the foundational principle rafʿ al-ḥaraj — "lifting hardship". From it derive: (1) all rukhaṣ (legal concessions) — short prayer, tayammum, breaking the fast in illness/travel; (2) the maxim al-mashaqqa tajlibu l-taysīr — "hardship brings ease"; (3) the maxim al-ḍarūrāt tubīḥu l-maḥẓūrāt — "necessity makes the forbidden lawful". Islamic law is structurally designed to be livable, not punishing.
Related verses
Q 2:185 (Allah wants ease for you, not hardship). Q 5:6 (Allah does not wish to put you in difficulty). Q 4:28 (Allah wishes to lighten your burden). Q 2:286 (Allah does not burden a soul beyond its capacity).
Quran 23:1–11
Successful believer Seven qualities
قَدْ أَفْلَحَ الْمُؤْمِنُونَ ۝ الَّذِينَ هُمْ فِي صَلَاتِهِمْ خَاشِعُونَ ۝ وَالَّذِينَ هُمْ عَنِ اللَّغْوِ مُعْرِضُونَ ۝ وَالَّذِينَ هُمْ لِلزَّكَاةِ فَاعِلُونَ ۝ وَالَّذِينَ هُمْ لِفُرُوجِهِمْ حَافِظُونَ ... وَالَّذِينَ هُمْ لِأَمَانَاتِهِمْ وَعَهْدِهِمْ رَاعُونَ ۝ وَالَّذِينَ هُمْ عَلَىٰ صَلَوَاتِهِمْ يُحَافِظُونَ ۝ أُولَٰئِكَ هُمُ الْوَارِثُونَ
"Successful are the believers — those who are humble in their prayer; who turn away from idle speech; who give zakat; who guard their chastity ... who keep their trusts and covenants; who guard their prayers. They are the inheritors."
Plain meaning
The Quran's portrait of al-mufliḥūn — those who genuinely succeed. Six concrete qualities: (1) khushūʿ in prayer (presence of heart, not just bodily motion); (2) avoiding laghw (idle, useless speech); (3) zakat; (4) chastity; (5) honoring trusts and covenants; (6) guarding prayer (preserving its times and conditions). The Prophet ﷺ said these verses describe believers "as they truly are".
Majority view
All four schools cite these verses for: (1) the holistic vision of Muslim character — ritual, ethical, social, and economic dimensions all named together; (2) khushūʿ as the soul of prayer — without it, prayer is incomplete (though valid); (3) the linkage of private virtues (chastity, modesty of speech) with public virtues (zakat, honoring trusts) — neither alone constitutes success.
Related verses
Q 25:63–77 (qualities of the servants of the Most Merciful). Q 70:22–35 (those constant in prayer — the same ethical complex). Q 2:177 (the comprehensive definition of birr).
Quran 24:2
Zinā ḥadd 100 lashes Public witnessing
الزَّانِيَةُ وَالزَّانِي فَاجْلِدُوا كُلَّ وَاحِدٍ مِّنْهُمَا مِائَةَ جَلْدَةٍ ۖ وَلَا تَأْخُذْكُم بِهِمَا رَأْفَةٌ فِي دِينِ اللَّهِ إِن كُنتُمْ تُؤْمِنُونَ بِاللَّهِ وَالْيَوْمِ الْآخِرِ ۖ وَلْيَشْهَدْ عَذَابَهُمَا طَائِفَةٌ مِّنَ الْمُؤْمِنِينَ
"The fornicating woman and the fornicating man — flog each of them one hundred lashes. Let no pity for them take you in the religion of Allah, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. Let a group of the believers witness their punishment."
Plain meaning
The Quranic ḥadd for zinā: 100 lashes — for both man and woman, for the unmarried. Public witnessing is part of the punishment's deterrent purpose. Note: the Quran nowhere mentions stoning (rajm) — that comes only from ḥadīth, and applies (per all four schools) only to the married person who commits zinā.
Majority view
All four schools impose extremely strict conditions before this ḥadd can be applied — designed to make application very rare: (1) four eyewitnesses to the act of penetration itself (not foreplay, not undressing); (2) all four must testify in exactly identical detail; (3) any inconsistency invalidates all four testimonies AND the witnesses are themselves punished for slander (Q 24:4); (4) confession may also establish the offense, but must be repeated four times in court; (5) the accused may retract confession at any point. Historically, the ḥadd was applied extremely rarely — perhaps a handful of cases under the Prophet ﷺ.
Where the schools differ
Stoning for the marriedAll four schools accept rajm for the married (muḥṣan) — based on multiple ḥadīth and the practice of the Prophet ﷺ and ʿUmar. Some early non-mainstream views (Khārijīs, Muʿtazila) rejected stoning on the grounds it isn't in the Quran's text.
100 lashes for the married also?Ḥanafī/Mālikī/Shāfiʿī: only stoning if married — lashes apply to unmarried only. Ḥanbalī: 100 lashes then stoning if married (combined). The latter is a strict minority position even within Ḥanbalī school.
ModernMany contemporary scholars stress the near-impossibility of the four-witness requirement: the ḥadd functions as a powerful public statement, not an everyday punishment.
Related verses
Q 4:15–16 (earlier zinā ruling — house confinement; superseded by 24:2). Q 24:4 (false accusation: 80 lashes). Q 17:32 (do not approach zinā). Q 24:6–9 (liʿān — when a husband alone accuses his wife).
Quran 24:4
Slander of chaste women Qadhf 80 lashes
وَالَّذِينَ يَرْمُونَ الْمُحْصَنَاتِ ثُمَّ لَمْ يَأْتُوا بِأَرْبَعَةِ شُهَدَاءَ فَاجْلِدُوهُمْ ثَمَانِينَ جَلْدَةً وَلَا تَقْبَلُوا لَهُمْ شَهَادَةً أَبَدًا ۚ وَأُولَٰئِكَ هُمُ الْفَاسِقُونَ
"Those who accuse chaste women (of zinā), then do not bring four witnesses — flog them eighty lashes, and do not accept their testimony ever after. Those are the corrupt (fāsiqūn)."
Plain meaning
Three layered penalties for false accusation of zinā (qadhf): (1) 80 lashes, (2) permanent disqualification from giving testimony in court, (3) fāsiq status before Allah. Notice the design: this is more than the slander penalty in any other context — because accusing a chaste woman of zinā is treated as one of the worst social crimes.
Majority view
All four schools impose qadhf ḥadd whenever a person accuses a chaste Muslim of zinā without four witnesses. The penalty applies even if the accuser believes the accusation true — without four eyewitnesses, the accusation is legally false. From this verse derives the protection of reputation (ʿirḍ) as one of the five maqāṣid of Sharīʿa (life, faith, intellect, lineage, property, honor). The same penalty applies to slandering a chaste man.
Where the schools differ
Repentance and testimonyThe next verse (24:5) makes an exception: if the slanderer repents and reforms, his testimony may be accepted again. Shāfiʿī/Ḥanbalī: yes — testimony resumes after repentance. Ḥanafī: no — disqualification is permanent; only the lashing is removed by repentance.
"Chaste" definitionAll schools: muḥṣan here means free, sane, adult, never convicted of zinā. Accusing a known fornicator does not incur this ḥadd, though it remains sinful and may incur taʿzīr.
Related verses
Q 24:11–20 (the slander incident with ʿĀʾisha — and the warning against spreading it). Q 24:23 (those who slander chaste, believing, unaware women — cursed in this world and the next). Q 49:12 (do not backbite each other).
Quran 24:6–9
Liʿān Husband's accusation
وَالَّذِينَ يَرْمُونَ أَزْوَاجَهُمْ وَلَمْ يَكُن لَّهُمْ شُهَدَاءُ إِلَّا أَنفُسُهُمْ فَشَهَادَةُ أَحَدِهِمْ أَرْبَعُ شَهَادَاتٍ بِاللَّهِ ۙ إِنَّهُ لَمِنَ الصَّادِقِينَ ... وَيَدْرَأُ عَنْهَا الْعَذَابَ أَن تَشْهَدَ أَرْبَعَ شَهَادَاتٍ بِاللَّهِ ۙ إِنَّهُ لَمِنَ الْكَاذِبِينَ
"Those who accuse their wives and have no witnesses except themselves: each shall testify four times by Allah that he is truthful, and a fifth time that Allah's curse is upon him if he lies ... The wife is spared the punishment by testifying four times by Allah that he is lying, and a fifth time that Allah's wrath is upon her if he speaks truth."
Plain meaning
A procedure called liʿān ("mutual cursing"). A husband who accuses his wife of zinā but has no witnesses faces an impossible dilemma: stay silent and live with her, or speak and face the qadhf ḥadd (80 lashes). Liʿān opens a third way: the husband swears four times by Allah and once invoking Allah's curse on himself if he lies. The wife then has the same opportunity — swearing four times that he's lying, with Allah's wrath on herself if he's truthful. Neither faces the ḥadd; the marriage is dissolved.
Majority view
All four schools accept this procedure. Liʿān exists for two related situations: (1) accusing the wife of zinā, (2) denying paternity of her child. Once both spouses complete the oaths, the marriage is dissolved permanently — they can never remarry each other. If the husband refuses to swear, he is liable for qadhf; if the wife refuses, she is liable for zinā. In practice, the procedure is extremely rare and reserved for unusual cases.
Related verses
Q 24:4 (qadhf generally — 80 lashes if no four witnesses). Q 24:2 (zinā ḥadd). Q 4:15 (former zinā ruling — superseded). Q 4:34 (treatment of wife — context of marital tensions).
Quran 24:11–12
Think well of fellow believers Ifk incident
إِنَّ الَّذِينَ جَاءُوا بِالْإِفْكِ عُصْبَةٌ مِّنكُمْ ۚ لَا تَحْسَبُوهُ شَرًّا لَّكُم ۖ بَلْ هُوَ خَيْرٌ لَّكُمْ ... لَّوْلَا إِذْ سَمِعْتُمُوهُ ظَنَّ الْمُؤْمِنُونَ وَالْمُؤْمِنَاتُ بِأَنفُسِهِمْ خَيْرًا وَقَالُوا هَٰذَا إِفْكٌ مُّبِينٌ
"Those who came with the slander are a faction from among you. Do not consider it bad for you — it is good for you ... When you heard it, why did not the believing men and women think the best of one another and say: 'This is an obvious slander'?"
Plain meaning
The opening of the ifk (slander) passage — context of the false accusation against ʿĀʾisha. Two principles: (1) even great trial can be turned to good — Allah's wisdom in tests is not always immediately visible; (2) when believers hear a damaging report about another believer, the default response should be: "this is slander". The verse establishes ḥusn al-ẓann (good opinion) as a religious obligation toward fellow believers.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the legal-ethical principle of ḥusn al-ẓann — defaulting to good opinion of believers; (2) the obligation, when hearing damaging news, to suspect the report rather than the believer; (3) the prohibition on amplifying or repeating such reports (Q 24:15 follows: "you spoke with your tongues what you had no knowledge of"). Modern application: this is the Quranic anti-gossip, anti-rumor-spreading framework — particularly weighty in social media age.
Related verses
Q 49:6 (verify news from corrupt sources). Q 49:12 (avoid much suspicion). Q 24:15–17 (the rebuke for spreading what one has no knowledge of). Q 24:19 (those who love that immorality should spread).
Quran 24:23
Slandering chaste women Curse in both worlds
إِنَّ الَّذِينَ يَرْمُونَ الْمُحْصَنَاتِ الْغَافِلَاتِ الْمُؤْمِنَاتِ لُعِنُوا فِي الدُّنْيَا وَالْآخِرَةِ وَلَهُمْ عَذَابٌ عَظِيمٌ
"Those who accuse chaste, unaware, believing women — they are cursed in this world and the Hereafter, and theirs is a great punishment."
Plain meaning
A particularly severe condemnation of slandering chaste, believing women. Three qualities of the victim are emphasized: muḥṣanāt (chaste), ghāfilāt (unaware — meaning innocent of even the suggestion), muʾmināt (believing). The punishment is rarely-paired language: curse in this world AND the next, plus great torment. The Prophet ﷺ said slandering a chaste woman is among the seven destroying sins (Bukhārī, Muslim).
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse as the strongest scriptural condemnation of false accusation against believing women — the verse complements the legal penalty of Q 24:4 (80 lashes + disqualification of testimony) with eternal consequence. The verse identifies slander against women as a particularly grave offense, beyond ordinary slander, because the social damage is irreversible — a woman's reputation, once damaged, is rarely fully restored. The Prophet ﷺ: "Whoever attributes to a believer what is not in him, Allah will keep him in the mud (radghatu l-khabāl) until he extricates himself" (Aḥmad).
Related verses
Q 24:4 (legal penalty for slander). Q 24:11–15 (the ifk incident against ʿĀʾisha — direct context). Q 49:12 (do not backbite one another).
Quran 24:27–28
Privacy of homes Permission to enter
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا لَا تَدْخُلُوا بُيُوتًا غَيْرَ بُيُوتِكُمْ حَتَّىٰ تَسْتَأْنِسُوا وَتُسَلِّمُوا عَلَىٰ أَهْلِهَا ۚ ... وَإِن قِيلَ لَكُمُ ارْجِعُوا فَارْجِعُوا ۖ هُوَ أَزْكَىٰ لَكُمْ
"O believers, do not enter houses other than your own until you have asked permission and greeted those within them ... If you are told 'go back', then go back — that is purer for you."
Plain meaning
Three rules for entering another's home: (1) seek permission (istiʾnās) — by knocking, calling out, or now ringing the bell; (2) greet (salām) — making one's identity and intentions clear; (3) if turned away, leave without offense. The Prophet ﷺ taught that one should not knock more than three times — if no response by the third, depart.
Majority view
All four schools: a house is sacred space for those who dwell in it. From this verse derive the laws on: (1) right of privacy — including the prohibition of peering into others' homes; (2) etiquette of entry — three knocks, identifying oneself, leaving if not received; (3) parents and children mutual permission norms (extended in 24:58–59). Western privacy law concepts are anticipated here by 14 centuries.
Related verses
Q 49:12 (do not spy on one another). Q 24:58 (children/servants must seek permission at three times of privacy). Q 24:29 (no blame on entering uninhabited public spaces).
Quran 24:30–31
Gaze Ḥijāb Modesty
قُل لِّلْمُؤْمِنِينَ يَغُضُّوا مِنْ أَبْصَارِهِمْ وَيَحْفَظُوا فُرُوجَهُمْ ۚ ذَٰلِكَ أَزْكَىٰ لَهُمْ ... وَقُل لِّلْمُؤْمِنَاتِ يَغْضُضْنَ مِنْ أَبْصَارِهِنَّ وَيَحْفَظْنَ فُرُوجَهُنَّ وَلَا يُبْدِينَ زِينَتَهُنَّ إِلَّا مَا ظَهَرَ مِنْهَا ۖ وَلْيَضْرِبْنَ بِخُمُرِهِنَّ عَلَىٰ جُيُوبِهِنَّ
"Tell the believing men to lower their gazes and guard their private parts — that is purer for them ... Tell the believing women to lower their gazes, guard their private parts, and not display their adornment except what (necessarily) appears. Let them draw their head-coverings (khumur) over their bosoms (juyūb)."
Plain meaning
The Quranic foundation of modesty (ḥayāʾ) and dress. Note the order: men are addressed first with the command to lower the gaze and guard chastity. Women's command begins identically — same two commands — and adds the dress prescription: existing head-coverings (already commonly worn) should be drawn down over the chest/bosom area, which was often left exposed in pre-Islamic dress. The phrase "except what necessarily appears" creates room for interpretation that has been debated for 14 centuries.
Majority view
All four classical schools: (1) the ʿawra for men in public is from navel to knee; (2) the ʿawra for women in public covers everything except face and hands (Mālikī, Shāfiʿī, Ḥanafī) — or face and hands and feet (Ḥanafī alone). A small minority of classical scholars (some Ḥanbalīs) hold that face must also be covered in public — but the majority of all schools considers the face and hands not part of ʿawra. Loose, opaque clothing that does not display the body's shape is the standard.
Where the schools differ
"What necessarily appears"Majority of Companions and four schools: face and hands. A minority: only the eyes for navigation. Modern majority (al-Qaraḍāwī, Ibn ʿĀshūr, traditional Azhar scholars): face and hands per the dominant classical view.
Niqāb (face cover)Recommended (Shāfiʿī, some Ḥanbalī) as extra modesty for those who choose; not obligatory per the four-school majority. Required by some strict minority views in specific cultural contexts.
Hair coveringUniversal in classical fiqh — all four schools require head covering in public for adult Muslim women. The modern debate over whether this is a culturally-conditioned ruling is a recent argument; not held by any classical school.
Related verses
Q 33:59 (let them draw their jalābīb close around them — outer garments). Q 33:53 (speak to the Prophet's ﷺ wives from behind a screen). Q 24:60 (older women past marriage age may relax these rules — without making themselves attractive).
Quran 24:32–33
Marry the single Chastity until marriage
وَأَنكِحُوا الْأَيَامَىٰ مِنكُمْ وَالصَّالِحِينَ مِنْ عِبَادِكُمْ وَإِمَائِكُمْ ۚ إِن يَكُونُوا فُقَرَاءَ يُغْنِهِمُ اللَّهُ مِن فَضْلِهِ ۗ ... وَلْيَسْتَعْفِفِ الَّذِينَ لَا يَجِدُونَ نِكَاحًا حَتَّىٰ يُغْنِيَهُمُ اللَّهُ مِن فَضْلِهِ
"Marry off the single among you and the righteous among your male and female servants. If they are poor, Allah will enrich them from His bounty ... Let those who do not find marriage live in chastity until Allah enriches them from His bounty."
Plain meaning
Two related commands: (1) the community has a collective duty to facilitate marriage for those eligible — providing financial assistance, social support, networks for matchmaking; poverty should not be a barrier; (2) those unable to marry must remain chaste — Allah will eventually provide. The verse rejects two extremes: forced celibacy (monasticism) and unrestrained sexuality.
Majority view
All four schools: marriage is half of religion (per the Prophet's ﷺ ḥadīth) and the community is responsible for enabling it. The Prophet ﷺ: "O young men, whoever among you is able, let him marry — for it lowers the gaze and guards chastity. Whoever cannot, let him fast — for fasting is a shield" (Bukhārī). The verse also grounds the obligation of guardians (especially fathers) to assist with marriage and the community's role in providing dowry assistance for those who cannot afford it.
Related verses
Q 4:25 (those who cannot afford to marry free believing women — marry from believing maids). Q 30:21 (mates from yourselves, between you love and mercy). Q 16:72 (Allah made for you mates of your own kind).
Quran 24:58–59
Privacy times Children and household
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا لِيَسْتَأْذِنكُمُ الَّذِينَ مَلَكَتْ أَيْمَانُكُمْ وَالَّذِينَ لَمْ يَبْلُغُوا الْحُلُمَ مِنكُمْ ثَلَاثَ مَرَّاتٍ ۚ مِّن قَبْلِ صَلَاةِ الْفَجْرِ وَحِينَ تَضَعُونَ ثِيَابَكُم مِّنَ الظَّهِيرَةِ وَمِن بَعْدِ صَلَاةِ الْعِشَاءِ ۚ ثَلَاثُ عَوْرَاتٍ لَّكُمْ
"O believers, let those whom your right hands possess and those of you who have not reached puberty ask permission of you at three times: before fajr prayer, when you remove your garments at midday, and after ʿishāʾ prayer. (These are) three (times of) privacy for you ..."
Plain meaning
An astonishingly specific verse on family privacy. Even children of the household and intimate servants must seek permission to enter the parents'/master's private space at three specific times: pre-dawn (waking, dressing), midday (rest, undressing), and after the night prayer (private time, marital intimacy). Outside these times, the household moves freely. The verse closes by saying once the children reach puberty, they must ask permission at all times like adult guests.
Majority view
All four schools recognize this verse as a fundamental statement of privacy within the family. The verse explicitly teaches children to respect parental private spaces and time — particularly around the times when parents may be undressed, asleep, or alone together. Classical scholars note: even Islamic family ethics, which strongly emphasize closeness and mutual responsibility, recognizes boundaries. Modern child-development advocates often cite this verse as a model for teaching consent and personal boundaries from a young age.
Related verses
Q 24:27 (ask permission and greet before entering homes). Q 33:53 (when invited by the Prophet ﷺ, enter when permitted — and disperse when meal is done). Q 49:12 (do not spy on one another).
Quran 25:63–66
ʿIbād ar-Raḥmān Servants of the Most Merciful
وَعِبَادُ الرَّحْمَٰنِ الَّذِينَ يَمْشُونَ عَلَى الْأَرْضِ هَوْنًا وَإِذَا خَاطَبَهُمُ الْجَاهِلُونَ قَالُوا سَلَامًا ۝ وَالَّذِينَ يَبِيتُونَ لِرَبِّهِمْ سُجَّدًا وَقِيَامًا ۝ وَالَّذِينَ يَقُولُونَ رَبَّنَا اصْرِفْ عَنَّا عَذَابَ جَهَنَّمَ
"The servants of the Most Merciful are those who walk on the earth in humility (hawn); when the ignorant address them, they say: 'Peace.' Those who spend the night in prostration and standing before their Lord; those who say: 'Our Lord, divert from us the punishment of Hell — its punishment is a constant torment.'"
Plain meaning
The opening of the famous portrait of ʿibād ar-Raḥmān — "servants of the Most Merciful" — running through 25:63–77. Three opening qualities: (1) walk in humility — gait reflects character; (2) respond to ignorance with peace — disengaging from provocation rather than escalating; (3) night prayer — secret devotion balanced with public conduct; (4) fear of the Fire — moral seriousness, not casual religiosity.
Majority view
All four schools cite this entire passage (continuing through 25:77) as the complete portrait of a Muslim's character. Qualities listed across all verses: humility, peaceful response, night prayer, fear of consequence, moderation in spending, no shirk, no killing, no zinā, no false testimony, walking past idle talk with dignity, sincere response to reminders, prayer for righteous family. The classical pedagogical practice was to teach children these verses as a self-development curriculum.
Related verses
Q 25:67 (moderation in spending — next in the series). Q 25:72 (do not bear false witness — also in the series). Q 31:18–19 (parallel character qualities). Q 23:1–11 (qualities of al-mufliḥūn — the successful).
Quran 25:67
Moderation in spending Middle path
وَالَّذِينَ إِذَا أَنفَقُوا لَمْ يُسْرِفُوا وَلَمْ يَقْتُرُوا وَكَانَ بَيْنَ ذَٰلِكَ قَوَامًا
"And those who, when they spend, are neither extravagant nor stingy — but between the two is a balanced way (qawām)."
Plain meaning
A clear formula for the Quranic ethic of spending: between two vices — isrāf (extravagance, waste) on one side and iqtār (miserliness, stinginess) on the other. The virtue is the middle — described as qawām, a steady/upright balance. Part of the surah's "qualities of the servants of the Most Merciful" (ʿibād al-Raḥmān) — a portrait of the ideal Muslim life.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse as the basis for personal finance ethics: (1) save, don't waste; (2) spend, don't hoard; (3) budget within one's means. Together with Q 17:26–27 (do not be a spendthrift) and Q 7:31 (eat, drink, do not be wasteful), it forms a complete picture. Modern application: avoid both luxury debt and cruel asceticism; spend on family, charity, and lawful enjoyment with intentionality.
Related verses
Q 17:26–27 (do not be a spendthrift — spendthrifts are brothers of the devils). Q 17:29 (do not let your hand be tied to your neck nor wholly outstretched). Q 7:31 (eat, drink, do not be wasteful).
Quran 26:88–89
Sound heart Final saving qualification
يَوْمَ لَا يَنفَعُ مَالٌ وَلَا بَنُونَ ۝ إِلَّا مَنْ أَتَى اللَّهَ بِقَلْبٍ سَلِيمٍ
"A Day when neither wealth nor children will avail — except one who comes to Allah with a sound heart (qalb salīm)."
Plain meaning
A Day is described — Resurrection — when every form of worldly advantage fails. Wealth cannot buy escape. Children cannot intercede. The only thing that helps is a qalb salīm — a heart that is sound, free of disease (hypocrisy, arrogance, envy, hidden shirk, accumulated grudge). The verse appears in Ibrāhīm's prayer/sermon to his people in Sūrat al-Shuʿarāʾ.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the eschatological centerpiece — the heart's condition is what matters at the final accounting, not external accomplishments; (2) the framework of spiritual diseases of the heart — hypocrisy, arrogance, miserliness, hidden polytheism, love of fame — each of which compromises the sound heart; (3) the foundation of all Sufi/spiritual purification literature. Ibn al-Qayyim's Iʿlām al-Muwaqqiʿīn and al-Ghazālī's Iḥyāʾ both build major sections on this verse.
Related verses
Q 91:7–10 (a soul and He who proportioned it — successful is the one who purifies it). Q 13:28 (in the remembrance of Allah do hearts find rest). Q 50:33 (whoever fears the Most Merciful unseen, and comes with a contrite heart). Q 39:22 (whose heart Allah opens to Islam — woe to those whose hearts harden).
Quran 28:77
Balance world and Hereafter No corruption
وَابْتَغِ فِيمَا آتَاكَ اللَّهُ الدَّارَ الْآخِرَةَ ۖ وَلَا تَنسَ نَصِيبَكَ مِنَ الدُّنْيَا ۖ وَأَحْسِن كَمَا أَحْسَنَ اللَّهُ إِلَيْكَ ۖ وَلَا تَبْغِ الْفَسَادَ فِي الْأَرْضِ ۖ إِنَّ اللَّهَ لَا يُحِبُّ الْمُفْسِدِينَ
"Seek through what Allah has given you the abode of the Hereafter — but do not forget your share of this world. Do good (iḥsān) as Allah has done good to you. Do not seek corruption in the land. Allah does not love the corrupters."
Plain meaning
Spoken to Qārūn — a wealthy man swallowed by the earth for his pride. The verse contains four principles in tension and balance: (1) wealth is for the Hereafter, not just enjoyment; (2) but do not forget your worldly share — Islam is not asceticism; (3) iḥsān to others matches the iḥsān Allah has shown you; (4) wealth must never produce fasād (corruption — exploitation, environmental damage, social harm).
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for the Islamic middle path on wealth: (1) productive wealth-building is permitted and even praiseworthy if used for good; (2) asceticism that rejects worldly enjoyment is condemned; (3) wealth carries a social duty — kindness to those who serve you, generosity to the poor; (4) environmental and economic justice — no profit through exploitation, fraud, or destruction.
Related verses
Q 2:201 (our Lord, give us good in this world and good in the Hereafter). Q 7:31–32 (do not be wasteful; who has forbidden the adornments of Allah?). Q 30:41 (corruption appears on land and sea by what people's hands have earned).
Quran 30:21
Marriage purpose Love and mercy
وَمِنْ آيَاتِهِ أَنْ خَلَقَ لَكُم مِّنْ أَنفُسِكُمْ أَزْوَاجًا لِّتَسْكُنُوا إِلَيْهَا وَجَعَلَ بَيْنَكُم مَّوَدَّةً وَرَحْمَةً ۚ إِنَّ فِي ذَٰلِكَ لَآيَاتٍ لِّقَوْمٍ يَتَفَكَّرُونَ
"Of His signs is that He created for you mates from yourselves — that you may find tranquility (sakan) in them — and He placed between you affection (mawadda) and mercy (raḥma). In that are signs for people who reflect."
Plain meaning
The Quranic statement of the purpose of marriage. Three named goods: (1) sakan — tranquility, mutual repose; (2) mawadda — affection, the warmth of romantic love; (3) raḥma — mercy, the deeper compassion that endures beyond infatuation. Marriage is presented not as a legal transaction but as one of Allah's signs (āyāt).
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) marriage as spiritual partnership, not just legal contract; (2) emotional rights of spouses — companionship, intimacy, kindness — that go beyond strict legal obligations; (3) the basis for treating marriage as a sacred sign, with divorce as a regrettable last resort. The Prophet ﷺ: "The most complete believer in faith is the one with the best character, and the best of you is the best to his wife" (Tirmidhī).
Related verses
Q 4:19 (live with them in kindness). Q 7:189 (created you from one soul and made from it its mate that he might find tranquility in her). Q 2:187 (they are a garment for you, and you are a garment for them). Q 25:74 (our Lord, give us from our spouses and offspring comfort to our eyes).
Quran 30:30
Fiṭra Natural disposition
فَأَقِمْ وَجْهَكَ لِلدِّينِ حَنِيفًا ۚ فِطْرَتَ اللَّهِ الَّتِي فَطَرَ النَّاسَ عَلَيْهَا ۚ لَا تَبْدِيلَ لِخَلْقِ اللَّهِ ۚ ذَٰلِكَ الدِّينُ الْقَيِّمُ
"So set your face firmly to the religion, upright (ḥanīf) — the fiṭra of Allah upon which He created humankind. There is no altering Allah's creation. That is the upright religion."
Plain meaning
The verse of fiṭra — humanity's innate disposition toward worship of Allah and recognition of moral truth. Islam is described not as a foreign imposition but as the religion aligned with how Allah created us. The Prophet ﷺ: "Every child is born upon the fiṭra; then his parents make him a Jew, Christian, or Magian" (Bukhārī).
Majority view
All four schools and Islamic theology overall cite this verse for: (1) the foundational concept that human beings have built-in spiritual orientation — innate recognition of one Creator and basic moral truths; (2) the framework for understanding infant burials, children dying before maturity, and people in remote contexts who never heard the message — they remain on fiṭra; (3) the practical principle: returning to Islam is described as returning to one's natural state, not adopting something alien. Ibn Taymiyya developed an entire theology of fiṭra on this verse.
Related verses
Q 7:172 (the primordial covenant — "Am I not your Lord?" "Yes — we bear witness"). Q 6:79 (Ibrāhīm: I have set my face toward the One who created the heavens and the earth, as a ḥanīf). Q 2:138 (the color/dye of Allah — and who is better in color than Allah?).
Quran 31:13–14
Luqmān's advice No shirk Gratitude
وَإِذْ قَالَ لُقْمَانُ لِابْنِهِ وَهُوَ يَعِظُهُ يَا بُنَيَّ لَا تُشْرِكْ بِاللَّهِ ۖ إِنَّ الشِّرْكَ لَظُلْمٌ عَظِيمٌ ۝ وَوَصَّيْنَا الْإِنسَانَ بِوَالِدَيْهِ حَمَلَتْهُ أُمُّهُ وَهْنًا عَلَىٰ وَهْنٍ وَفِصَالُهُ فِي عَامَيْنِ أَنِ اشْكُرْ لِي وَلِوَالِدَيْكَ إِلَيَّ الْمَصِيرُ
"When Luqmān said to his son, instructing him: 'O my son, do not associate (anything) with Allah — surely shirk is the greatest wrong (ẓulm ʿaẓīm).' We enjoined upon man (concern) for his parents — his mother carried him in weakness upon weakness, and his weaning is in two years. Be grateful to Me and to your parents — to Me is the return."
Plain meaning
Luqmān's advice opens the surah named for him — Islam's model of fatherly wisdom. Two interwoven themes: (1) theological — no shirk, the greatest wrong; (2) relational — gratitude to Allah paired with gratitude to parents (especially the mother, whose physical sacrifice the verse explicitly names). The verse also legislates the natural weaning period at two years.
Majority view
All four schools draw from this verse: (1) the two years of recommended breastfeeding (Q 2:233 sets it as a maximum); (2) the obligation to be grateful and good to parents — placed on the same line as gratitude to Allah; (3) the principle that shirk is the greatest wrong because it gives what belongs to Allah alone to another. Q 31:15 immediately following clarifies: even if parents urge you to commit shirk, do not obey them — but still treat them with kindness in this world.
Related verses
Q 17:23–24 (parents — do not say "uff" to them). Q 46:15 (mother bore him in hardship). Q 2:233 (mothers may nurse for two full years). Q 4:48, 116 (shirk is the unforgivable sin).
Quran 31:18–19
No arrogance Modest walk and voice
وَلَا تُصَعِّرْ خَدَّكَ لِلنَّاسِ وَلَا تَمْشِ فِي الْأَرْضِ مَرَحًا ۖ إِنَّ اللَّهَ لَا يُحِبُّ كُلَّ مُخْتَالٍ فَخُورٍ ۝ وَاقْصِدْ فِي مَشْيِكَ وَاغْضُضْ مِن صَوْتِكَ ۚ إِنَّ أَنكَرَ الْأَصْوَاتِ لَصَوْتُ الْحِمَارِ
"Do not turn your cheek (in scorn) from people, nor walk on the earth boastfully — Allah does not love any self-deluded boaster. Be moderate in your pace, and lower your voice. The most disagreeable of voices is that of a donkey."
Plain meaning
Continuation of Luqmān's advice to his son. Three practical instructions on bearing: (1) do not turn your face away from people in disdain; (2) do not walk boastfully — pace reflects character; (3) do not shout — the verse compares loud, harsh voices to the braying of a donkey, one of the most strikingly direct similes in the Quran.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the etiquette of tawāḍuʿ (humility) in everyday bearing — including walk, posture, eye contact, voice volume; (2) the religious classification of arrogance (kibr) as one of the most dangerous spiritual diseases — the Prophet ﷺ said: "no one with a mustard seed of arrogance in their heart will enter Paradise" (Muslim); (3) the Sunnah of moderate voice — neither inaudible whispering nor overpowering shouting. The verse is the Quran's most direct condemnation of social arrogance.
Related verses
Q 17:37 (do not walk on earth proudly — you cannot rend the earth or reach the mountains in height). Q 25:63 (servants of the Most Merciful walk humbly on the earth). Q 49:2 (do not raise voices above the Prophet's ﷺ voice).
Quran 33:21
Excellent example Uswa ḥasana
لَّقَدْ كَانَ لَكُمْ فِي رَسُولِ اللَّهِ أُسْوَةٌ حَسَنَةٌ لِّمَن كَانَ يَرْجُو اللَّهَ وَالْيَوْمَ الْآخِرَ وَذَكَرَ اللَّهَ كَثِيرًا
"In the Messenger of Allah you have an excellent example (uswa ḥasana) — for one who hopes in Allah and the Last Day, and remembers Allah often."
Plain meaning
A foundational verse establishing the Prophet ﷺ as model for Muslim life. Uswa ḥasana = "an excellent / beautiful example". The verse names who finds him a model: those who (1) hope in Allah and the Last Day, (2) remember Allah abundantly. The implication: those who lack these qualities will not see the value of his example.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse as the textual foundation of the entire Sunnah-as-source-of-law system: the Prophet ﷺ is uswa (model) not just in his recited teachings but in his complete life — character, decisions, manner, conduct. The verse pairs with Q 4:80 and Q 33:36 to establish that following the Prophet's ﷺ example is religious obligation, not optional admiration. Sufi tradition adds: spiritual transformation comes through imitation of the Prophet's ﷺ character.
Related verses
Q 4:80 (whoever obeys the Messenger has obeyed Allah). Q 33:36 (no choice for the believer when Allah and Messenger have decided). Q 60:4 (in Ibrāhīm and those with him you had an excellent example). Q 60:6 (in them there is an excellent example for whoever hopes in Allah and the Last Day).
Quran 33:5
Adoption / lineage Call by father's name
ادْعُوهُمْ لِآبَائِهِمْ هُوَ أَقْسَطُ عِندَ اللَّهِ ۚ فَإِن لَّمْ تَعْلَمُوا آبَاءَهُمْ فَإِخْوَانُكُمْ فِي الدِّينِ وَمَوَالِيكُمْ ۚ وَلَيْسَ عَلَيْكُمْ جُنَاحٌ فِيمَا أَخْطَأْتُم بِهِ
"Call them (the adopted) by (the names of) their (biological) fathers — that is more just before Allah. If you do not know their fathers, they are your brothers in religion and your mawālī (close associates). There is no blame on you for what you have done unintentionally."
Plain meaning
The verse abolished the pre-Islamic Arab practice of tabannī — formally adopting a child and treating him as a biological son with full rights of inheritance and lineage transfer. After this verse, an adopted child keeps their biological father's name. If the biological father is unknown, the child is simply a "brother in faith" — not given a fictitious lineage. This change applied retroactively: the Prophet's ﷺ adopted son Zayd ibn Ḥāritha had been called "Zayd ibn Muḥammad"; he reverted to "Zayd ibn Ḥāritha".
Majority view
All four schools: Islamic adoption — called kafāla ("sponsorship/fostering") — is different from Western legal adoption. The child is fully cared for, loved, and treated as family — but: (1) keeps biological father's name; (2) inherits from biological, not adoptive, parents; (3) marriage rules follow biological lineage; (4) maḥram status (covering, seclusion rules) is by biological lineage or nursing, not adoption. The classical Islamic system of kafāla provides full family love without erasing biological identity — a strong response to debates about adopted children's right to know their origins.
Related verses
Q 33:4 (Allah has not made your adopted sons your sons). Q 33:37 (the case of Zayd and Zaynab — full abolition of adoption-as-lineage). Q 4:23 (forbidden marriages — only biological/nursing relations).
Quran 33:33
Wives of the Prophet Home as base Tabarruj forbidden
وَقَرْنَ فِي بُيُوتِكُنَّ وَلَا تَبَرَّجْنَ تَبَرُّجَ الْجَاهِلِيَّةِ الْأُولَىٰ ۖ وَأَقِمْنَ الصَّلَاةَ وَآتِينَ الزَّكَاةَ وَأَطِعْنَ اللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُ
"Stay rooted in your homes, and do not display yourselves (tabarruj) as in the former times of ignorance. Establish prayer, give zakat, and obey Allah and His Messenger."
Plain meaning
Addressed directly to the wives of the Prophet ﷺ — but classical scholars derive broader principles applicable to all Muslim women. Tabarruj = displaying oneself excessively in dress, walk, or manner to attract attention. The verse positively frames a home-centered orientation — without forbidding outside activity (the Prophet's ﷺ wives went to mosque, taught, narrated thousands of ḥadīth).
Majority view
All four schools: this verse addresses the Prophet's ﷺ wives primarily, with secondary application to all Muslim women. Qarna fī buyūtikunna ("stay in your homes") is not understood as house arrest — the Prophet's ﷺ wives moved freely for legitimate purposes (Hajj, ʿUmra, teaching, mosque, market for necessities). It establishes home as primary base, not exclusive location. Tabarruj is universally forbidden — for all women — and includes excessive perfume in public, attention-seeking walk, and immodest dress beyond just exposure.
Where the schools differ
Strict viewSome classical scholars (and some modern conservatives) extend the verse to encourage women's maximal home-centeredness — discouraging unnecessary going-out, mixed workplaces.
MainstreamThe four schools' majority: women may work, study, conduct business — provided modesty norms (Q 24:30–31, 33:59) are observed. The Prophet's ﷺ first wife Khadīja was a major businesswoman before and during marriage. ʿĀʾisha taught publicly.
Related verses
Q 24:31 (women's modesty). Q 33:59 (jalābīb — outer garment). Q 33:53 (privacy of Prophet's household). Q 4:34 (mutual rights of spouses).
Quran 33:35
Ten paired qualities Equal reward
إِنَّ الْمُسْلِمِينَ وَالْمُسْلِمَاتِ وَالْمُؤْمِنِينَ وَالْمُؤْمِنَاتِ وَالْقَانِتِينَ وَالْقَانِتَاتِ وَالصَّادِقِينَ وَالصَّادِقَاتِ وَالصَّابِرِينَ وَالصَّابِرَاتِ وَالْخَاشِعِينَ وَالْخَاشِعَاتِ وَالْمُتَصَدِّقِينَ وَالْمُتَصَدِّقَاتِ وَالصَّائِمِينَ وَالصَّائِمَاتِ وَالْحَافِظِينَ فُرُوجَهُمْ وَالْحَافِظَاتِ وَالذَّاكِرِينَ اللَّهَ كَثِيرًا وَالذَّاكِرَاتِ ۙ أَعَدَّ اللَّهُ لَهُم مَّغْفِرَةً وَأَجْرًا عَظِيمًا
"The Muslim men and women; the believing men and women; the devout men and women; the truthful men and women; the patient men and women; the humble men and women; the charitable men and women; the fasting men and women; the chaste men and women; the men and women who remember Allah often — Allah has prepared for them forgiveness and a great reward."
Plain meaning
Revealed (per Umm Salama) when she asked the Prophet ﷺ why the Quran addresses men so often and women so rarely. This verse is the response. Ten qualities, each paired masculine + feminine, with identical reward promised. The verse establishes a foundational principle of spiritual and ethical equality in Islam: women bear the same religious responsibilities and receive the same reward as men.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the principle that religious obligations apply equally to men and women — both pray, fast, give zakat, perform Hajj, observe chastity; (2) spiritual reward is gender-equal — there is no Islamic hierarchy of spiritual potential by gender; (3) the verse demolishes pre-Islamic and many contemporary cultural assumptions that women's religiosity is secondary. Differences in legal procedure (inheritance shares, witness procedures) reflect functional roles in the legal system, not differences in spiritual worth.
Related verses
Q 9:71 (believing men and women are allies — enjoining good). Q 4:124 (whoever does good — male or female — and is a believer, will enter Paradise). Q 16:97 (whoever does good — male or female — We will give them a good life). Q 40:40 (whoever does righteousness — male or female — they will enter Paradise).
Quran 33:36
No choice after divine decree Submission
وَمَا كَانَ لِمُؤْمِنٍ وَلَا مُؤْمِنَةٍ إِذَا قَضَى اللَّهُ وَرَسُولُهُ أَمْرًا أَن يَكُونَ لَهُمُ الْخِيَرَةُ مِنْ أَمْرِهِمْ ۗ وَمَن يَعْصِ اللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُ فَقَدْ ضَلَّ ضَلَالًا مُّبِينًا
"It is not for a believing man or woman — when Allah and His Messenger have decided a matter — to have any choice (khiyara) in their affair. Whoever disobeys Allah and His Messenger has clearly gone astray."
Plain meaning
A direct statement of submission as the defining mark of belief. Once Allah and His Messenger ﷺ have ruled on a matter, the believer does not retain veto power. This applies equally to men and women — the verse explicitly names both. The verse was revealed in the context of Zaynab bint Jaḥsh's marriage, where she initially preferred a different match before the Prophet ﷺ's instruction.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) Sunnah is binding alongside the Quran — the Messenger's ﷺ rulings are co-equal in legal force; (2) true belief includes accepting rulings that may not personally appeal; (3) Q 4:65 reinforces — true belief includes inward acceptance, not just outward compliance. The verse also balances: it requires submission to clearly-established matters, but does not forbid scholarly inquiry, ijtihad in ambiguous cases, or critical reflection on contested interpretations.
Related verses
Q 4:65 (no faith without submitting to the Prophet's judgment). Q 4:59 (obey Allah, Messenger, those in authority). Q 24:51 (the saying of believers is "we hear and we obey"). Q 24:63 (do not regard the Messenger's call as an ordinary call).
Quran 33:59
Jalābīb Outer garment Recognition in public
يَا أَيُّهَا النَّبِيُّ قُل لِّأَزْوَاجِكَ وَبَنَاتِكَ وَنِسَاءِ الْمُؤْمِنِينَ يُدْنِينَ عَلَيْهِنَّ مِن جَلَابِيبِهِنَّ ۚ ذَٰلِكَ أَدْنَىٰ أَن يُعْرَفْنَ فَلَا يُؤْذَيْنَ
"O Prophet, tell your wives, your daughters, and the believing women: they should draw their outer garments (jalābīb) close around themselves. That is more likely they will be recognized (as believing women) and not be harassed."
Plain meaning
A second modesty verse — but the addressee expands: all believing women, not only Prophet's family. Jilbāb = outer garment, cloak, or wrap covering the upper body and head when going out. The verse explicitly states the reason: distinctive Muslim women's dress identifies them as honored and respectable, reducing harassment (the Madinan streets were often unsafe at night for unaccompanied women).
Majority view
All four schools agree: this verse, with Q 24:31, establishes the legal obligation of distinctive public modesty for women — including head and bodily covering. Classical scholars disagree on whether this means covering everything except eyes (strict minority), face and hands shown but rest covered (mainstream four-school majority), or only head and bosom (small modern reformist view, rejected by classical scholars). Almost all classical and modern mainstream agree the head is covered.
Where the schools differ
Face coverRecommended (Ḥanbalī, some Shāfiʿī, some Mālikī when men are nearby); not obligatory in the mainstream four-school view.
Color/styleNo specific color or style mandated. Cultural variation (black abaya, colorful kaftan, looser dress + scarf) all satisfy the verse's requirements provided they are loose and opaque.
Context of "recognition"Classical view: distinguishing free Muslim women from slaves (who did not cover the head in early Islamic society) and from non-Muslim women's dress. Modern view: the reason has broader application — distinctive dress as marker of Muslim identity and protection against harassment.
Related verses
Q 24:30–31 (gaze + women's modesty). Q 33:33 (do not display yourselves). Q 33:55 (women may relax in front of certain maḥram categories). Q 24:60 (older women past marriage age).
Quran 35:15
Allah is free of need Humanity's dependence
يَا أَيُّهَا النَّاسُ أَنتُمُ الْفُقَرَاءُ إِلَى اللَّهِ ۖ وَاللَّهُ هُوَ الْغَنِيُّ الْحَمِيدُ
"O mankind, you are the poor (in need) toward Allah, and Allah is the Self-Sufficient, the Praiseworthy."
Plain meaning
A startlingly direct verse addressed to all of humanity. The asymmetry is absolute: humans are the poor (fuqarāʾ) — every breath, every heartbeat, every condition is a dependency on Allah. Allah is al-Ghaniyy, the Self-Sufficient — nothing about His being requires anything from creation. Worship benefits the worshipper, not the Worshipped.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the foundational theological principle of human fundamental dependence — even the wealthiest human is structurally a faqīr before Allah; (2) the corrective to all forms of religious arrogance — no one's piety benefits Allah; (3) the framing of worship as privilege granted, not service rendered. The Prophet ﷺ taught believers to say: "Allah, You are the Self-Sufficient, and we are the poor before You."
Related verses
Q 47:38 (Allah is the Self-Sufficient and you are the poor). Q 14:8 (if you and all on earth disbelieved, Allah is independent and praiseworthy). Q 31:26 (Allah is the Self-Sufficient, Praiseworthy). Q 22:64 (Allah is the Self-Sufficient).
Quran 36:82
Kun fa-yakūn Divine command
إِنَّمَا أَمْرُهُ إِذَا أَرَادَ شَيْئًا أَن يَقُولَ لَهُ كُن فَيَكُونُ
"When He intends something, His command is only to say to it: 'Be' — and it is."
Plain meaning
The single most concise statement of Allah's creative power. No process, no intermediate cause, no resistance: kun ("Be") and the thing exists. Resurrection, the deepest skeptical objection in the surrounding verses, is dismissed by this principle: One who creates by command can recreate by command.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the foundational theological principle of creation by command — distinguishing the Creator's mode of creation from any process humans can imagine; (2) the answer to skeptical questions about resurrection, healing, divine intervention, miraculous provision; (3) the practical-spiritual encouragement: nothing one prays for is "too hard" for Allah. The phrase "kun fa-yakūn" appears six times in the Quran (2:117, 3:47, 3:59, 6:73, 16:40, 36:82) — establishing it as a Quranic refrain.
Related verses
Q 2:117 (when He decrees something, He only says "Be" and it is). Q 16:40 (when We intend a thing — Our word for it is only "Be" and it is). Q 54:50 (Our command is but one — like the twinkling of an eye).
Quran 39:53
No despair of mercy All sins forgivable
قُلْ يَا عِبَادِيَ الَّذِينَ أَسْرَفُوا عَلَىٰ أَنفُسِهِمْ لَا تَقْنَطُوا مِن رَّحْمَةِ اللَّهِ ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ يَغْفِرُ الذُّنُوبَ جَمِيعًا ۚ إِنَّهُ هُوَ الْغَفُورُ الرَّحِيمُ
"Say: O My servants who have exceeded against themselves — do not despair of Allah's mercy. Allah forgives all sins. He is the All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful."
Plain meaning
One of the most consoling verses in the Quran. Allah addresses sinners directly with the phrase "my servants" — even those who have exceeded against themselves are still His servants. The promise: all sins are forgivable upon repentance (paired with Q 4:48: with the exception of dying on shirk).
Majority view
