All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the foundational principle of tawba (repentance) — no sin places one beyond Allah's mercy if there is sincere turning; (2) prohibition of despair (qunūṭ) — despair of Allah's mercy is itself listed among major sins (Q 12:87); (3) pastoral tone for teachers, scholars, and counselors — meet the wayward with mercy and the promise of return, not with crushing condemnation. Ibn ʿAbbās: "This is the most hopeful verse in the Quran."
Related verses
Q 4:48 (Allah does not forgive shirk — but forgives anything less for whom He wills). Q 25:68–70 (whoever repents — Allah replaces their sins with good deeds). Q 12:87 (do not despair of the comfort of Allah). Q 7:156 (My mercy encompasses all things).
Quran 41:34
Repel evil with good Enmity to friendship
وَلَا تَسْتَوِي الْحَسَنَةُ وَلَا السَّيِّئَةُ ۚ ادْفَعْ بِالَّتِي هِيَ أَحْسَنُ فَإِذَا الَّذِي بَيْنَكَ وَبَيْنَهُ عَدَاوَةٌ كَأَنَّهُ وَلِيٌّ حَمِيمٌ
"The good and the evil are not equal. Repel (evil) with what is better — and behold! He between whom and you was enmity becomes as if a warm, intimate friend."
Plain meaning
A psychological-spiritual instruction. When wronged, the natural impulse is retaliation in kind. The verse commands a higher response: respond with what is better. The promised effect is striking: enmity dissolves into warm friendship. The verse acknowledges this is not easy — Q 41:35 immediately adds: "no one is granted this except those who are patient, and those endowed with great fortune".
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the higher Islamic ethics of iḥsān — going beyond justice to mercy; (2) the principle that responding to wrong with kindness is the most effective long-term strategy in human relations; (3) the recognition that this is difficult — requires patience and grace. The Prophet ﷺ embodied this verse repeatedly: forgiving Quraysh at the conquest of Makkah, the man who tried to assassinate him, his Tāʾif tormentors.
Related verses
Q 23:96 (repel evil with what is better — We know best what they describe). Q 28:54 (they repel evil with good). Q 13:22 (they repel evil with good — for them is the final home). Q 42:40 (recompense for evil is its equal — but whoever forgives, his reward is with Allah).
Quran 47:7
Help Allah, He helps you Reciprocal aid
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا إِن تَنصُرُوا اللَّهَ يَنصُرْكُمْ وَيُثَبِّتْ أَقْدَامَكُمْ
"O believers, if you support Allah (His cause), He will support you and make your steps firm."
Plain meaning
A short verse with profound implications. Allah's support is described as conditional upon the believers' active effort: if you support Him — meaning support His religion, His commands, His servants — He will support you. The reward is two-fold: divine support, AND thabāt al-aqdām (firmness of footing) — steadiness in faith and trial.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the principle of reciprocal divine support — Allah's help is not transactional, but it does follow human commitment; (2) the comprehensive nature of "supporting Allah" — it includes upholding His Sunnah, defending the truth, helping His servants, teaching His religion, building Muslim institutions; (3) thabāt as the most valuable spiritual gift — being unshaken by trial, doubt, or persecution. Modern application: faith communities thrive in proportion to their active service, not in proportion to their complaints.
Related verses
Q 22:40 (Allah will surely support those who support Him). Q 13:11 (Allah will not change a people's condition until they change themselves). Q 8:62 (it is He who supported you with His help and the believers).
Quran 47:24
Reflect on the Quran Locked hearts
أَفَلَا يَتَدَبَّرُونَ الْقُرْآنَ أَمْ عَلَىٰ قُلُوبٍ أَقْفَالُهَا
"Do they not reflect on the Quran — or are there locks (aqfāl) upon their hearts?"
Plain meaning
A short, piercing rhetorical question. Allah names two possible reasons for missing the Quran's transformative effect: (1) they do not reflect — tadabbur (deep contemplation) is absent, replaced by surface reading or mechanical recitation; (2) locks on their hearts — accumulated sin, pride, or rejection has sealed the door of reception.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the religious obligation of tadabbur — meditating on the Quran's meanings, not just its sounds; (2) the warning that certain spiritual conditions block the Quran's effect — pride, persistent sin, attachment to falsehood; (3) the basis of the entire tafsīr tradition — the Quran demands engagement, not just recitation. The verse contrasts with Q 38:29 ("a blessed Book — that they may reflect on its verses"). Modern application: passive consumption of recitation without comprehension is an incomplete relationship with the Quran.
Related verses
Q 38:29 (a Book We have sent down — that they may reflect on its verses). Q 4:82 (do they not reflect on the Quran? Had it been from other than Allah, they would have found in it much discrepancy). Q 23:68 (do they not reflect on the speech?).
Quran 47:33
Do not nullify deeds Obedience
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا أَطِيعُوا اللَّهَ وَأَطِيعُوا الرَّسُولَ وَلَا تُبْطِلُوا أَعْمَالَكُمْ
"O believers, obey Allah and obey the Messenger — and do not invalidate your deeds."
Plain meaning
A short, jarring verse. Beyond just doing good deeds, one must preserve them. Many things nullify prior good deeds: riyāʾ (showing off), boasting about charity (Q 2:264), reverting to disbelief, persistent major sin without repentance, harming someone after benefiting them. The verse implies that the spiritual life is a careful balance — not just accumulation, but also protection.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the principle that good deeds can be cancelled — not just by apostasy but by specific sins (iḥbāṭ); (2) the legal-spiritual concept of preserving reward through sincerity, humility, and avoidance of self-promotion; (3) the warning of ʿujb (self-admiration) — feeling proud of one's worship is itself one of the dangers that can void it. Q 2:264 explicitly: "do not nullify your charity by reminders of it or by injury".
Related verses
Q 2:264 (do not nullify your charity by reminding or harming). Q 49:2 (do not raise voices above the Prophet's ﷺ — your deeds may be voided unaware). Q 9:54 (their spending is not accepted — because they disbelieved). Q 18:104 (those whose efforts go astray in worldly life while they think they are doing good).
Quran 49:2–5
Etiquette with the Prophet Voice / patience
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا لَا تَرْفَعُوا أَصْوَاتَكُمْ فَوْقَ صَوْتِ النَّبِيِّ ... إِنَّ الَّذِينَ يَغُضُّونَ أَصْوَاتَهُمْ عِندَ رَسُولِ اللَّهِ أُولَٰئِكَ الَّذِينَ امْتَحَنَ اللَّهُ قُلُوبَهُمْ لِلتَّقْوَىٰ ... إِنَّ الَّذِينَ يُنَادُونَكَ مِن وَرَاءِ الْحُجُرَاتِ أَكْثَرُهُمْ لَا يَعْقِلُونَ ۝ وَلَوْ أَنَّهُمْ صَبَرُوا حَتَّىٰ تَخْرُجَ إِلَيْهِمْ لَكَانَ خَيْرًا لَّهُمْ
"O believers, do not raise your voices above the voice of the Prophet ... Those who lower their voices before the Messenger of Allah — they are the ones whose hearts Allah has tested for taqwā ... Those who call out to you from behind the chambers — most of them lack reason. Had they waited patiently until you came out to them, it would have been better for them."
Plain meaning
A series of etiquette rules in the presence of the Prophet ﷺ. Revealed when a delegation from Banū Tamīm called out impatiently from outside the Prophet's ﷺ chambers. The verses establish: (1) do not raise voices above his; (2) lowering one's voice before him is a sign of tested taqwā; (3) impatience in summoning him is a sign of lack of reason; (4) patience would have been better.
Majority view
All four schools cite these verses for: (1) the etiquette of reverence for the Prophet ﷺ — by extension, in his masjid and at his grave; (2) the broader principle of respect for religious authority, teachers, scholars, and elders — not raising voices, not pestering, not interrupting; (3) the linkage of outer adab (manners) with inner taqwā — adab is not separable from sincere faith. Imām Mālik famously refused to ride a horse in Madina out of reverence for where the Prophet ﷺ walked.
Related verses
Q 24:63 (do not regard the Messenger's call as you regard one another's call). Q 33:53 (do not enter the Prophet's homes without permission). Q 7:204 (when the Quran is recited, listen and be silent).
Quran 49:6
Verify news Reliability of source
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا إِن جَاءَكُمْ فَاسِقٌ بِنَبَإٍ فَتَبَيَّنُوا أَن تُصِيبُوا قَوْمًا بِجَهَالَةٍ فَتُصْبِحُوا عَلَىٰ مَا فَعَلْتُمْ نَادِمِينَ
"O believers, if a corrupt person (fāsiq) brings you news — verify (tabayyanū), lest you harm a people in ignorance and become regretful over what you have done."
Plain meaning
A foundational verse on epistemic responsibility. Revealed when al-Walīd ibn ʿUqba brought a false report that the tribe of Banū al-Muṣṭaliq had apostatized and refused to pay zakat — the Prophet ﷺ was about to send a military force when this verse was revealed. The verse forbids acting on news from unreliable sources without verification. Three required steps: identify the source, assess their reliability, verify the report.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) requirement of credible sources in legal evidence — both for judicial action and for spreading information; (2) prohibition of acting on rumors, gossip, or unverified social media; (3) the entire science of ʿilm al-rijāl (the science of ḥadīth narrator reliability) — only narrations from trustworthy persons are accepted. Modern application: this is the Quran's "fact-check" verse — especially relevant in the age of viral misinformation.
Related verses
Q 17:36 (do not follow that of which you have no knowledge). Q 24:11–15 (the slander of ʿĀʾisha — why didn't believers think well?). Q 4:83 (when news of peace or fear comes, they spread it — instead of taking it to those in authority).
Quran 49:9–10
Reconcile factions Believers as brothers
وَإِن طَائِفَتَانِ مِنَ الْمُؤْمِنِينَ اقْتَتَلُوا فَأَصْلِحُوا بَيْنَهُمَا ۖ فَإِن بَغَتْ إِحْدَاهُمَا عَلَى الْأُخْرَىٰ فَقَاتِلُوا الَّتِي تَبْغِي حَتَّىٰ تَفِيءَ إِلَىٰ أَمْرِ اللَّهِ ۚ فَإِن فَاءَتْ فَأَصْلِحُوا بَيْنَهُمَا بِالْعَدْلِ ۚ ... إِنَّمَا الْمُؤْمِنُونَ إِخْوَةٌ فَأَصْلِحُوا بَيْنَ أَخَوَيْكُمْ
"If two groups of the believers fight, reconcile between them. If one of them transgresses against the other, fight the transgressing one until it returns to Allah's command. If it returns, reconcile between them with justice ... The believers are but brothers; so reconcile between your two brothers."
Plain meaning
The procedure for civil war or fitna between Muslim groups. The community is responsible: (1) first attempt reconciliation, (2) if one side persists in aggression, the legitimate state forces them to stop, (3) once they relent, reconciliation must be just — not a victor's-peace. The verse closes with the famous declaration: believers are brothers. This is not optional sentiment — it is the basis of all peace-making.
Majority view
All four schools take this verse as the legal framework for the rules of baghy (rebellion) and intra-Muslim conflict: (1) rebels who fight against legitimate authority may be fought, but not killed if they surrender or flee; (2) wounded rebels are not killed, prisoners are released, captured property is returned; (3) the goal is reconciliation, not destruction. Caliph ʿAlī applied this principle in the wars against rebellious groups.
Related verses
Q 4:114 (no good in secret talk except for reconciliation between people). Q 8:1 (reconcile what is between you). Q 4:35 (marital arbitration — parallel principle). Q 49:11–12 (do not mock — practical brotherhood).
Quran 49:11–12
Mockery Suspicion Backbiting
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا لَا يَسْخَرْ قَوْمٌ مِّن قَوْمٍ ... وَلَا تَلْمِزُوا أَنفُسَكُمْ وَلَا تَنَابَزُوا بِالْأَلْقَابِ ... اجْتَنِبُوا كَثِيرًا مِّنَ الظَّنِّ إِنَّ بَعْضَ الظَّنِّ إِثْمٌ ۖ وَلَا تَجَسَّسُوا وَلَا يَغْتَب بَّعْضُكُم بَعْضًا ۚ أَيُحِبُّ أَحَدُكُمْ أَن يَأْكُلَ لَحْمَ أَخِيهِ مَيْتًا فَكَرِهْتُمُوهُ
"O believers, let not a people mock another people ... Do not insult one another, nor call each other by offensive nicknames ... Avoid much suspicion (ẓann) — some suspicion is sin. Do not spy. Do not backbite one another. Would any of you like to eat the flesh of his dead brother? You would loathe it."
Plain meaning
Six prohibitions in two verses — a complete charter of communal civility: (1) no mockery of any group; (2) no insulting; (3) no offensive nicknames; (4) no excessive suspicion; (5) no spying; (6) no backbiting. The verse's metaphor for ghība (backbiting) — eating the flesh of one's dead brother — is among the most vivid and shocking in scripture.
Majority view
All four schools and the entire Sunni tradition cite this as the foundational text on ādāb al-suḥba — manners of human company. The Prophet ﷺ explained ghība: "Mentioning your brother in a way he would dislike — even if true. If false, that's worse — it's buhtān (slander)" (Muslim). The verse forbids: (1) mocking weight, height, skin color, social class, accent, ethnicity; (2) gendered nicknames intended to demean; (3) jumping to conclusions about people's motives; (4) reading others' messages, watching them surreptitiously; (5) repeating private faults to others.
Where the schools differ
Exceptions to ghībaAll schools recognize narrow exceptions: warning someone about a dishonest business partner, complaining to authority about oppression, asking for advice, identifying a person by an obvious feature when necessary. Outside these, all gossip is haram.
"Some suspicion is sin"Scholars distinguish: passing thoughts not held to are not sinful; holding bad suspicion as belief, or acting on it, is sinful.
Related verses
Q 24:11–15 (the great slander of ʿĀʾisha). Q 24:19 (those who love that immorality spread among believers — painful punishment). Q 33:58 (those who harm believers without cause — slander and sin). Q 104:1 (woe to every backbiter and slanderer).
Quran 49:13
Human equality Honor by taqwā Anti-racism
يَا أَيُّهَا النَّاسُ إِنَّا خَلَقْنَاكُم مِّن ذَكَرٍ وَأُنثَىٰ وَجَعَلْنَاكُمْ شُعُوبًا وَقَبَائِلَ لِتَعَارَفُوا ۚ إِنَّ أَكْرَمَكُمْ عِندَ اللَّهِ أَتْقَاكُمْ ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ عَلِيمٌ خَبِيرٌ
"O mankind, We created you from a male and a female, and made you peoples and tribes — that you may know one another. Indeed, the most honored of you before Allah is the most God-conscious. Allah is all-knowing, well-acquainted."
Plain meaning
Addressed not to "believers" but to all of humanity. Three universal statements: (1) one origin — all humans from a single male and female; (2) diversity is divinely intended — peoples and tribes exist for mutual knowing, not domination; (3) the only legitimate measure of human worth is taqwā — God-consciousness — which is inward and known only to Allah.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse as Islam's foundational anti-racism statement. From it derive: (1) prohibition of any form of ethnic/racial supremacy or discrimination; (2) the principle that nobility (karam) is by character, not by birth, color, language, or wealth; (3) cultural diversity is celebrated as by design. The Prophet ﷺ in his Farewell Sermon: "O people, your Lord is one and your father (Adam) is one. No Arab is superior to a non-Arab, nor non-Arab to Arab; no white to black, nor black to white — except by taqwā."
Related verses
Q 4:1 (mankind from one soul). Q 30:22 (among His signs is the variety of your languages and colors). Q 7:189 (created you from one soul and made from it its mate). Q 11:118 (had your Lord willed, He would have made mankind one community).
Quran 49:14
Islām vs Īmān Outer vs inner
قَالَتِ الْأَعْرَابُ آمَنَّا ۖ قُل لَّمْ تُؤْمِنُوا وَلَٰكِن قُولُوا أَسْلَمْنَا وَلَمَّا يَدْخُلِ الْإِيمَانُ فِي قُلُوبِكُمْ
"The bedouins say: 'We have believed.' Say: 'You have not (yet) believed. But say: We have submitted (aslamnā) — for faith has not yet entered your hearts.'"
Plain meaning
A verse distinguishing outer submission (islām) from inner faith (īmān). Some bedouins who had joined Islam claimed full belief; Allah corrects them: outward acceptance is a start, but īmān requires the heart's internalization. The verse models intellectual honesty about one's spiritual state — claiming higher than one has reached is the error being corrected.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the classical distinction between islām (formal submission, the outward pillars) and īmān (faith that has entered the heart); (2) the Prophet's ﷺ famous "ḥadīth of Jibrīl" reinforces this distinction with three levels: islām (the five pillars), īmān (the six articles of belief), iḥsān (worshipping as if you see Him); (3) the warning against spiritual self-deception — claiming the inner station while only having reached the outer. The verse is a humility tonic for the religious.
Related verses
Q 9:124 (when a sūra is revealed, some say "whose faith does this increase?"). Q 8:2 (true believers — when Allah is mentioned, their hearts tremble). Q 49:15 (the believers are only those who believed in Allah and His Messenger, then doubted not, and strove with their wealth and selves).
Quran 50:16
Allah is near Inner observation
وَلَقَدْ خَلَقْنَا الْإِنسَانَ وَنَعْلَمُ مَا تُوَسْوِسُ بِهِ نَفْسُهُ ۖ وَنَحْنُ أَقْرَبُ إِلَيْهِ مِنْ حَبْلِ الْوَرِيدِ
"We created man and We know what his soul whispers within him. We are closer to him than his jugular vein."
Plain meaning
A short, intimate statement of Allah's closeness. Allah knows not just human actions but the whispers of the soul itself — the half-formed temptations, intrusive thoughts, secret desires. The metaphor "closer than the jugular vein" places Allah's awareness inside the person, not outside watching.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the theological-ethical reality of iḥsān — worshipping Allah as if you see Him, knowing that even if you don't see Him, He sees you (per the famous Jibrīl ḥadīth); (2) the practical motivation for private piety — Allah knows what no one else does; (3) the comfort during distress — the closest Helper is not somewhere far, but closer than the body's own circulation. Sufi traditions developed entire spiritual technologies of presence around this verse.
Related verses
Q 2:186 (when My servants ask you about Me — I am near). Q 8:24 (Allah comes between a person and his heart). Q 57:4 (He is with you wherever you are). Q 58:7 (no whispered conversation of three but He is the fourth).
Quran 57:20
Worldly life as play Forgiveness in Hereafter
اعْلَمُوا أَنَّمَا الْحَيَاةُ الدُّنْيَا لَعِبٌ وَلَهْوٌ وَزِينَةٌ وَتَفَاخُرٌ بَيْنَكُمْ وَتَكَاثُرٌ فِي الْأَمْوَالِ وَالْأَوْلَادِ ۖ ... وَفِي الْآخِرَةِ عَذَابٌ شَدِيدٌ وَمَغْفِرَةٌ مِّنَ اللَّهِ وَرِضْوَانٌ ۚ وَمَا الْحَيَاةُ الدُّنْيَا إِلَّا مَتَاعُ الْغُرُورِ
"Know that the worldly life is only play, amusement, adornment, mutual boasting between you, and accumulation in wealth and children ... In the Hereafter is severe punishment — and forgiveness from Allah and good pleasure. The worldly life is only delusive enjoyment."
Plain meaning
A precision diagnosis of how worldly life traps people. Five stages: (1) laʿib — childlike play; (2) lahw — adolescent amusement; (3) zīna — adult adornment, dressing up; (4) tafākhur — mutual boasting, status competition; (5) takāthur — accumulation of wealth and children as scorecard. The progression describes a typical human life — each stage absorbs more of the soul. The Hereafter offers two outcomes: severe punishment OR Allah's forgiveness and pleasure.
Majority view
All four schools and the Islamic spiritual tradition cite this verse for: (1) the principled critique of materialism and status-seeking; (2) the framework of "what is worldly life for?" — not denial of pleasure, but recognition of its limited scope; (3) the binary of the Hereafter — severe punishment or Allah's pleasure — with no middle ground of permanent neutrality. Modern application: social media, consumerism, conspicuous consumption, performative parenting, status games — all interface with this verse's diagnosis.
Related verses
Q 3:185 (worldly life is the enjoyment of delusion). Q 29:64 (worldly life is only amusement and play — and the home of the Hereafter is the (true) life). Q 102:1–2 (rivalry in worldly increase diverted you until you visit the graves). Q 87:16–17 (you prefer the worldly life — yet the Hereafter is better).
Quran 58:1–4
Ẓihār Pre-Islamic divorce Graduated kaffāra
قَدْ سَمِعَ اللَّهُ قَوْلَ الَّتِي تُجَادِلُكَ فِي زَوْجِهَا ... الَّذِينَ يُظَاهِرُونَ مِنكُم مِّن نِّسَائِهِم مَّا هُنَّ أُمَّهَاتِهِمْ ... وَالَّذِينَ يُظَاهِرُونَ مِن نِّسَائِهِمْ ثُمَّ يَعُودُونَ لِمَا قَالُوا فَتَحْرِيرُ رَقَبَةٍ مِّن قَبْلِ أَن يَتَمَاسَّا ۚ ... فَمَن لَّمْ يَجِدْ فَصِيَامُ شَهْرَيْنِ مُتَتَابِعَيْنِ ... فَمَن لَّمْ يَسْتَطِعْ فَإِطْعَامُ سِتِّينَ مِسْكِينًا
"Allah has heard the speech of the woman who pleaded with you concerning her husband ... Those who pronounce ẓihār against their wives — they are not their mothers ... Those who pronounce ẓihār and then go back on what they said: freeing of a slave before they touch. Whoever cannot — two consecutive months of fasting. Whoever cannot — feeding sixty poor."
Plain meaning
The verse abolishing ẓihār — a pre-Islamic divorce form in which a man would say to his wife: "You are to me like my mother's back" — making her forbidden to him as if his actual mother. This created a torturous limbo: not divorced (she couldn't remarry), not married (he wouldn't touch her). Khawla bint Thaʿlaba came to the Prophet ﷺ pleading her case — her plea is the cause of this surah's revelation. The verse declares: ẓihār is a "vile and false" statement; the marriage remains; if the man wants to resume relations, a graduated kaffāra must be paid first: free a slave, OR fast two consecutive months, OR feed 60 poor.
Majority view
All four schools agree on the procedure. The kaffāra of ẓihār establishes the most demanding kaffāra pattern in Sharīʿa — and the same three-tier pattern (free slave, fast two months, feed sixty) appears in two other contexts: (1) accidental killing (Q 4:92), (2) breaking the Ramadan fast deliberately (per ḥadīth). This shows the gravity of ẓihār in Islamic law.
Where the schools differ
"Going back" (ʿawd)Ḥanafī: intention to return to marital relations triggers the kaffāra. Mālikī: the husband's resolve to keep her as wife and not implement the ẓihār. Shāfiʿī: a period of time passes after the ẓihār statement — the silence itself constitutes "going back". Ḥanbalī: similar to Ḥanafī.
Practical relevance todayThe pre-Islamic form of ẓihār is essentially extinct. The verses remain as a Quranic statement of how oppressive customs against women are abolished, replaced with structured pathways, and how a single woman's complaint can change divine law.
Related verses
Q 33:4 (Allah has not made your adopted sons your sons — same surah-of-abolition pattern). Q 2:226–227 (īlāʾ — another pre-Islamic problematic vow form, similarly regulated). Q 4:92 (kaffāra of manslaughter — parallel structure).
Quran 60:8
Kindness to non-aggressive non-Muslims Justice
لَّا يَنْهَاكُمُ اللَّهُ عَنِ الَّذِينَ لَمْ يُقَاتِلُوكُمْ فِي الدِّينِ وَلَمْ يُخْرِجُوكُم مِّن دِيَارِكُمْ أَن تَبَرُّوهُمْ وَتُقْسِطُوا إِلَيْهِمْ ۚ إِنَّ اللَّهَ يُحِبُّ الْمُقْسِطِينَ
"Allah does not forbid you — concerning those who did not fight you in religion nor expel you from your homes — from being good (tabarrū) and dealing justly with them. Allah loves those who are just."
Plain meaning
A critical verse for the ethics of Muslim-non-Muslim relations. Allah explicitly does not forbid: (1) birr — goodness, kindness, generosity; (2) qisṭ — justice, fairness in dealings. Toward whom? Non-Muslims who have not made war on Muslims or driven them out. The verse is the definitive textual answer to those who misread Q 5:51 (the awliyāʾ verse) as forbidding all friendship across faith lines.
Majority view
All four schools and modern scholarship cite this verse as the standard for Muslim-non-Muslim social ethics. Non-Muslims who are peaceful neighbors, colleagues, friends, in-laws, business partners — Muslims are commanded to deal with them in birr (goodness) and qisṭ (justice). Asmāʾ bint Abī Bakr asked the Prophet ﷺ whether she could maintain ties with her non-Muslim mother who visited her in Madina — he ﷺ replied: "Yes, maintain ties with your mother". This verse was cited as a basis.
Related verses
Q 60:9 (Allah does forbid taking as allies those who fought you in religion and helped expel you). Q 5:8 (justice even with those you dislike). Q 5:51 (military allegiance — contextual). Q 4:90 (those who do not fight you — do not assault them). Q 49:13 (humanity made into peoples and tribes to know one another).
Quran 61:2–3
Integrity of speech Practice what you preach
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا لِمَ تَقُولُونَ مَا لَا تَفْعَلُونَ ۝ كَبُرَ مَقْتًا عِندَ اللَّهِ أَن تَقُولُوا مَا لَا تَفْعَلُونَ
"O believers, why do you say what you do not do? It is most hateful before Allah that you should say what you do not do."
Plain meaning
A condemnation of hypocritical speech — the gap between word and action. The wording maqt (intense hatred) is one of the strongest expressions in the Quran. The verse particularly addresses those who claim readiness for sacrifice, fighting, or righteousness — but fail to follow through.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the obligation to practice what one preaches — particularly for scholars, teachers, and religious leaders; (2) the warning to those who exhort others to good while neglecting it themselves (echoed in Q 2:44: "Do you order righteousness for the people and forget yourselves?"); (3) the obligation to fulfill promises, contracts, public commitments. The Prophet ﷺ explicitly named three signs of hypocrisy — including "when he promises, he breaks" and "when entrusted, he betrays" (Bukhārī).
Related verses
Q 2:44 (do you command righteousness while forgetting yourselves?). Q 5:1 (fulfill contracts). Q 16:91 (do not break oaths after confirmation). Q 9:75–77 (some made covenant — if Allah gives us bounty we will give charity — but when bounty came, they were stingy and turned away).
Quran 62:9–10
Jumuʿa Stop trade Return to work
يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا إِذَا نُودِيَ لِلصَّلَاةِ مِن يَوْمِ الْجُمُعَةِ فَاسْعَوْا إِلَىٰ ذِكْرِ اللَّهِ وَذَرُوا الْبَيْعَ ۚ ذَٰلِكُمْ خَيْرٌ لَّكُمْ ۝ فَإِذَا قُضِيَتِ الصَّلَاةُ فَانتَشِرُوا فِي الْأَرْضِ وَابْتَغُوا مِن فَضْلِ اللَّهِ
"O believers, when the call to prayer is made on Friday, hasten to the remembrance of Allah and leave off trade — that is better for you. When the prayer is finished, disperse throughout the land and seek of Allah's bounty."
Plain meaning
The verse legislating Jumuʿa (Friday prayer). Two phases: (1) when called, drop business entirely and proceed to prayer — even ongoing transactions are paused; (2) after the prayer, return to work, business, and seeking provision — Islam is not anti-commerce. Jumuʿa replaces Ẓuhr on Fridays for those obligated.
Majority view
All four schools: Jumuʿa is obligatory on every adult, sane, male, free Muslim who is resident (not traveling) and physically able. Women, children, travelers, the sick, and the seriously inconvenienced are exempt (but may attend). Jumuʿa consists of: a sermon (khuṭba) in two parts, followed by a 2-rakʿa prayer in congregation. Missing three Jumuʿas in a row without excuse is considered a major sin — the Prophet ﷺ said: "Allah seals their hearts" (Aḥmad).
Where the schools differ
Minimum attendeesḤanafī: 3 plus the imam. Mālikī: 12 plus imam. Shāfiʿī/Ḥanbalī: 40 plus imam (strictest).
Validity in multiple mosquesḤanafī (classical): only one Jumuʿa per city. Modern: relaxed due to large urban populations. Other schools: more flexible.
Language of khuṭbaClassical Mālikī/Shāfiʿī/Ḥanbalī: Arabic only (with possible introduction in local language). Ḥanafī: local language acceptable, but at least one phrase of remembrance in Arabic. Modern practice varies widely.
Related verses
Q 2:43 (establish prayer and bow with those who bow). Q 4:103 (prayer is at fixed times). Q 24:37 (men whom neither trade nor sale distracts from remembrance of Allah). Q 7:31 (take your adornment for every place of prayer).
Quran 65:1–2
Ṭalāq procedure ʿIdda No expulsion
يَا أَيُّهَا النَّبِيُّ إِذَا طَلَّقْتُمُ النِّسَاءَ فَطَلِّقُوهُنَّ لِعِدَّتِهِنَّ وَأَحْصُوا الْعِدَّةَ ۖ وَاتَّقُوا اللَّهَ رَبَّكُمْ ۖ لَا تُخْرِجُوهُنَّ مِن بُيُوتِهِنَّ وَلَا يَخْرُجْنَ إِلَّا أَن يَأْتِينَ بِفَاحِشَةٍ مُّبَيِّنَةٍ ۚ ... فَإِذَا بَلَغْنَ أَجَلَهُنَّ فَأَمْسِكُوهُنَّ بِمَعْرُوفٍ أَوْ فَارِقُوهُنَّ بِمَعْرُوفٍ
"O Prophet, when you divorce women, divorce them at the start of their (waiting) period, count the period accurately, and be mindful of Allah ... Do not expel them from their houses, nor should they leave — unless they commit a clear indecency ... When they reach the end of their term, retain them in kindness or part with them in kindness."
Plain meaning
The detailed procedure for ṭalāq: (1) timing — at the start of a menstrual cycle's clean phase, not during menses; (2) counting the ʿidda — three menstrual cycles for non-pregnant women; (3) residence — the divorced wife stays in the marital home; the husband must not expel her, and she must not leave — both are commanded; (4) final stage — at the end of the ʿidda, the husband either returns to her (revocation) or releases her completely, in both cases with kindness. The verse models divorce as a process of cooling, reconsideration, and humane separation — not a one-time impulse.
Majority view
All four schools accept this verse as the procedural template for ṭalāq. Critical features: (1) divorce cannot be issued during menstruation (per the Prophet's ﷺ correction of Ibn ʿUmar); (2) the wife stays in the marital home during ʿidda — the husband cannot evict her; (3) financial maintenance continues during ʿidda; (4) sudden, one-time "triple ṭalāq" violates this verse's structure — most modern Muslim states have legislated against it.
Where the schools differ
Triple ṭalāq in one sittingClassical four schools: counts as three irrevocable divorces. Ibn Taymiyya and most modern Muslim states (Egypt, Pakistan, Tunisia, etc.): counts as one divorce — the verse requires three separate divorces.
Witnessing ṭalāqSunni schools: witnessing is recommended but not strictly required for validity (though some require it for revocation). Twelver Shia: two witnesses required for ṭalāq validity itself.
Related verses
Q 2:228–232 (ʿidda details and post-divorce relations). Q 65:4 (ʿidda for those who do not menstruate — three months; pregnant — until delivery). Q 65:6 (give them lodging according to your means, do not harass them). Q 2:229 (divorce is twice — after that retain or release in kindness).
Quran 70:19–21
Impatient human Anxious / stingy
إِنَّ الْإِنسَانَ خُلِقَ هَلُوعًا ۝ إِذَا مَسَّهُ الشَّرُّ جَزُوعًا ۝ وَإِذَا مَسَّهُ الْخَيْرُ مَنُوعًا
"Man was created impatient (halūʿ) — anxious when evil touches him, withholding when good comes."
Plain meaning
A precision diagnosis of halūʿ — anxious impatience — broken into two symptoms: (1) jazūʿ: when difficulty strikes, despair and panic; (2) manūʿ: when ease comes, miserliness — hoarding instead of sharing. The same person who collapses under stress closes their hand when relief arrives. The verses that follow (70:22–35) describe the exceptions: those who pray constantly, give what is known as the poor's right, believe in the Last Day, fear Allah, guard chastity, honor trusts, and witness truthfully.
Majority view
All four schools cite this passage for: (1) realistic anthropology — the Quran does not flatter humans; the default emotional state is anxious instability; (2) the religious correction: the qualities of constant prayer, generosity, and trust reverse the natural human disposition; (3) the connection between prayer and generosity: people who pray consistently tend to give consistently; people who give freely tend to pray with depth. The two correct each other.
Related verses
Q 100:6–8 (man is ungrateful). Q 17:11 (man prays for evil as for good — man is hasty). Q 41:51 (when good touches him, he turns away; when harm touches him, he is full of prayer). Q 23:1–11 (qualities of the successful believer).
Quran 70:24–25
Right of the poor Known share
وَالَّذِينَ فِي أَمْوَالِهِمْ حَقٌّ مَّعْلُومٌ ۝ لِّلسَّائِلِ وَالْمَحْرُومِ
"And those in whose wealth is a known right (ḥaqq maʿlūm) — for the petitioner and the deprived."
Plain meaning
Two short verses with major implications. The wealth of believers contains a known right (ḥaqq maʿlūm) for two groups: (1) the sāʾil — the one who asks; (2) the maḥrūm — the deprived who does not ask (and is therefore overlooked). The poor's share is described as a known right, not a discretionary gift — meaning the wealthy do not "give" charity, they "discharge a debt".
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) zakat as a right, not a favor — the language reframes the entire ethics of giving; (2) responsibility to seek out the maḥrūm — those whose dignity prevents them from asking; (3) basis for organized state-level wealth redistribution beyond individual choice. Some scholars (Ibn Ḥazm, classical literalists) read this as referring to obligations beyond zakat — a separate, undefined "right of the poor" beyond the 2.5% zakat.
Related verses
Q 51:19 (in their wealth was a right for the petitioner and deprived). Q 9:60 (eight categories of zakat). Q 2:177 (true piety includes giving wealth — for relatives, orphans, the needy, the wayfarer, beggars, and freeing slaves).
Quran 73:20
Easing of night prayer Beautiful loan to Allah
... فَاقْرَءُوا مَا تَيَسَّرَ مِنَ الْقُرْآنِ ۚ عَلِمَ أَن سَيَكُونُ مِنكُم مَّرْضَىٰ وَآخَرُونَ يَضْرِبُونَ فِي الْأَرْضِ يَبْتَغُونَ مِن فَضْلِ اللَّهِ وَآخَرُونَ يُقَاتِلُونَ فِي سَبِيلِ اللَّهِ ۖ فَاقْرَءُوا مَا تَيَسَّرَ مِنْهُ ۚ وَأَقِيمُوا الصَّلَاةَ وَآتُوا الزَّكَاةَ وَأَقْرِضُوا اللَّهَ قَرْضًا حَسَنًا
"... So recite what is easy of the Quran. He knows there will be among you the sick, others traveling the earth seeking Allah's bounty, and others fighting in Allah's way. So recite what is easy of it. Establish prayer, give zakat, and lend Allah a beautiful loan (qarḍ ḥasan)."
Plain meaning
The verse relaxed the earlier command (in Q 73:1–4) for the Prophet ﷺ and the believers to stand much of the night in prayer. As the community grew and life became more demanding — work, travel, war — the obligation eased to "what is easy". The closing introduces a powerful concept: qarḍ ḥasan — a "beautiful loan" to Allah. Charity is reframed as a loan Allah Himself promises to repay multiplied.
Majority view
All four schools: this verse abrogated the strict overnight-prayer requirement on the Prophet ﷺ and believers. Tahajjud (night prayer) became nāfila (supererogatory) — strongly encouraged but not obligatory. The qarḍ ḥasan concept has become the basis for modern Islamic interest-free lending — both at personal and institutional levels. The principle: any loan given purely for the borrower's benefit, with no interest, is virtuous; the lender's "profit" is with Allah.
Related verses
Q 2:245 (who will lend Allah a beautiful loan — He will multiply it many times). Q 57:18 (those who give charity and lend Allah a beautiful loan — multiplied for them). Q 17:79 (and during the night, hold vigil with it — an additional act for you). Q 51:17–18 (they used to sleep little of the night).
Quran 76:8–9
Feed the needy For Allah's face
وَيُطْعِمُونَ الطَّعَامَ عَلَىٰ حُبِّهِ مِسْكِينًا وَيَتِيمًا وَأَسِيرًا ۝ إِنَّمَا نُطْعِمُكُمْ لِوَجْهِ اللَّهِ لَا نُرِيدُ مِنكُمْ جَزَاءً وَلَا شُكُورًا
"They give food, out of love for it, to the needy, the orphan, and the captive — (saying): 'We feed you only for the face of Allah. We seek no recompense from you, nor any thanks.'"
Plain meaning
A description of the righteous (al-abrār) in action. Three details worth weighing: (1) they give food out of love for it — they part with what they themselves desire; (2) recipients include the captive (asīr) — even prisoners of war, even those who fought against them; (3) they explicitly disclaim any expected return — not reward, not even thanks.
Majority view
All four schools cite these verses for: (1) the gold standard of ikhlāṣ (sincerity) in charity — giving for Allah alone; (2) the legal-ethical principle that even enemy captives have a right to food and humane treatment; (3) the highest level of giving — from what one loves (Q 3:92), with no expectation. The verse historically grounds the Islamic prohibition of mistreating prisoners of war and the affirmative obligation to feed and shelter them.
Related verses
Q 3:92 (you will never attain birr until you spend from what you love). Q 90:14–16 (feeding the kin orphan, the destitute). Q 2:177 (giving from love of Him — to relatives, orphans, needy, wayfarers). Q 8:67 (you have prisoners of war — Allah's intent is for you to be merciful).
Quran 87:14–15
Purification Remembrance and prayer
قَدْ أَفْلَحَ مَن تَزَكَّىٰ ۝ وَذَكَرَ اسْمَ رَبِّهِ فَصَلَّىٰ
"Successful is the one who purifies (himself), remembers the name of his Lord, and prays."
Plain meaning
A compact formula for spiritual success in three movements: (1) tazakkā — purifies oneself (often understood as the giving of zakat at one level, and the deeper purification of the soul at another); (2) dhakara isma rabbihi — remembers Allah's name; (3) fa-ṣallā — and prays. The sequence is significant: purification precedes remembrance, which culminates in prayer.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the dual sense of tazkiyya — financial purification (zakat) and spiritual purification (purifying the soul of vices); (2) the principle that prayer is preceded by remembrance — entering prayer with a heart oriented toward Allah produces qualitatively different worship than mechanical entry; (3) the Prophet ﷺ explained "successful is the one who purifies" as related to both zakat and to tazkiyat al-nafs (soul-purification). Some narrations link this verse particularly to ʿĪd al-Fiṭr — giving zakat al-fiṭr before the ʿĪd prayer.
Related verses
Q 91:9 (successful is the one who purifies it). Q 9:103 (take from their wealth a charity — purifying them). Q 92:5–7 (whoever gives and is mindful — We will ease his way to ease). Q 20:14 (establish prayer for My remembrance).
Quran 90:12–18
Steep path Freeing a slave Feeding orphan/poor kin
وَمَا أَدْرَاكَ مَا الْعَقَبَةُ ۝ فَكُّ رَقَبَةٍ ۝ أَوْ إِطْعَامٌ فِي يَوْمٍ ذِي مَسْغَبَةٍ ۝ يَتِيمًا ذَا مَقْرَبَةٍ ۝ أَوْ مِسْكِينًا ذَا مَتْرَبَةٍ ۝ ثُمَّ كَانَ مِنَ الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا وَتَوَاصَوْا بِالصَّبْرِ وَتَوَاصَوْا بِالْمَرْحَمَةِ ۝ أُولَٰئِكَ أَصْحَابُ الْمَيْمَنَةِ
"What will make you know what the steep ascent is? Freeing a slave, or feeding on a day of starvation an orphan near of kin, or a poor person in the dust. Then he was among those who believed and counseled patience and mutual mercy. Those are the companions of the right."
Plain meaning
The Quran defines al-ʿaqaba — "the steep ascent" — as concrete actions: freeing a slave, feeding in famine, prioritizing orphan-kin and the destitute. Then the inner state: belief plus mutual counsel in patience and mercy. The verse pairs material sacrifice with spiritual brotherhood — neither alone makes the ascent.
Majority view
All four schools cite this surah for: (1) the moral primacy of freeing slaves — a recurring Quranic concern, gradually phasing out the institution; (2) prioritization of kin orphans before strangers (consistent with the Sunnah hierarchy); (3) the binary frame — believers either climb the steep ascent or fail. Modern application: redirecting wealth toward freeing modern victims of trafficking, debt slavery, and human-rights violations.
Related verses
Q 2:177 (freeing slaves in the definition of true piety). Q 24:33 (assist your slaves who seek manumission). Q 76:8–9 (they feed the needy and orphan and captive for the love of Allah). Q 103 (mutual counsel in truth and patience).
Quran 95:4
Best stature Human nature
لَقَدْ خَلَقْنَا الْإِنسَانَ فِي أَحْسَنِ تَقْوِيمٍ
"We have created man in the best stature (aḥsan taqwīm)."
Plain meaning
One of the Quran's most succinct affirmations of human dignity and excellence as creation. Aḥsan taqwīm can be translated as "the best mould" or "the best stature" — referring to humanity's physical, mental, moral, and spiritual capacities. The verse refuses any picture of humanity as a fallen, debased species. The next verses (95:5–6) qualify: this excellence can be lost through disbelief and evil action — but the original creation is excellence.
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for: (1) the foundational anthropology of Islam — humanity is created in the highest natural rank, with potential for both the highest spiritual heights AND the lowest depths (95:5); (2) the basis for valuing human life, body, and faculties as gifts of Allah's craftsmanship; (3) the corrective to nihilistic or self-loathing self-images. Modern application: ethical questions on biotechnology, body modification, and self-harm all interface with this verse's affirmation.
Related verses
Q 17:70 (We have honored the children of Adam). Q 32:9 (gave you hearing, sight, and hearts). Q 95:5–6 (then We reduced him to the lowest of the low, except those who believed and did good).
Quran 99:7–8
Atom-weight accountability Final reckoning
فَمَن يَعْمَلْ مِثْقَالَ ذَرَّةٍ خَيْرًا يَرَهُ ۝ وَمَن يَعْمَلْ مِثْقَالَ ذَرَّةٍ شَرًّا يَرَهُ
"Whoever does an atom's weight of good will see it. And whoever does an atom's weight of evil will see it."
Plain meaning
Two of the most quoted verses in the Quran. Nothing — however small — is lost. Every good act, however tiny, is seen and rewarded. Every evil, however trivial, is also seen. The verse rejects both: (1) despair — "my small good doesn't matter"; (2) complacency — "my small wrongs don't count". Mithqāl dharra ("atom-weight") is the smallest unit imaginable.
Majority view
All four schools cite these verses for: (1) the underlying logic of every Islamic obligation — meaning even minor sunnahs and minor sins have real eschatological weight; (2) the encouragement to not dismiss small acts — a smile is charity, removing harm from the road is charity; (3) the warning to not dismiss small wrongs — backbiting "just a little", lying "just once", looking at a forbidden image "just briefly" all register. The Prophet ﷺ: "Beware of trivializing sins — they gather like firewood and burn the carrier" (Aḥmad).
Related verses
Q 4:40 (Allah does not wrong an atom's weight — if it be good He multiplies it). Q 21:47 (We set up scales of justice on the Day — not a soul is wronged in the slightest). Q 18:49 (the record — woe to us, it leaves nothing small or large but accounts for it).
Quran 100:6–8
Ungrateful man Love of wealth
إِنَّ الْإِنسَانَ لِرَبِّهِ لَكَنُودٌ ۝ وَإِنَّهُ عَلَىٰ ذَٰلِكَ لَشَهِيدٌ ۝ وَإِنَّهُ لِحُبِّ الْخَيْرِ لَشَدِيدٌ
"Verily, man is ungrateful to his Lord. And he himself is a witness to that. And he is severe in his love of (worldly) good."
Plain meaning
A three-stage diagnosis of the human spiritual condition: (1) kanūd — ungrateful, forgetful of blessings, focused on what's missing; (2) shahīd ʿalā dhālik — and the person knows it: their conscience is a witness against them; (3) severe in love of khayr — here khayr means "the good" in the sense of worldly wealth and possessions. The verse names the basic human pattern that religion is meant to correct.
Majority view
All four schools and the spiritual tradition cite this verse for: (1) realistic anthropology — the Quran does not flatter humans; ingratitude is the default, gratitude requires intentional cultivation; (2) the role of conscience as internal witness — even those who deny gratitude feel its absence; (3) the diagnosis of attachment to wealth as the most common spiritual disorder. The verse pairs with Q 14:34 ("man is unjust, ungrateful") and Q 70:19–21 ("man was created impatient — anxious when evil touches him, withholding when good comes").
Related verses
Q 14:34 (man is most unjust, ungrateful). Q 70:19–21 (man was created impatient). Q 17:11 (man is hasty). Q 14:7 (if you are grateful, I will increase you — but if you are ungrateful, My punishment is severe).
Quran 102:8
Questioning about blessings Gratitude
ثُمَّ لَتُسْأَلُنَّ يَوْمَئِذٍ عَنِ النَّعِيمِ
"Then you will surely be questioned that Day about all the blessings (naʿīm)."
Plain meaning
A short, devastating verse at the end of Sūrat al-Takāthur. Whatever wealth, comfort, health, family, time, or opportunity Allah gave — questioning awaits about each. The Prophet ﷺ explained naʿīm includes things often taken for granted: clean water to drink, food, shelter, the security of one's home, health, free time (Tirmidhī).
Majority view
All four schools cite this verse for the comprehensive Islamic doctrine of accountability. Every blessing carries an obligation: gratitude in word, gratitude in conduct (using the blessing within Allah's permissions), gratitude in sharing. From this verse derive: (1) the religious obligation to express thanks (shukr); (2) prohibition of waste — wasting blessings is a form of ingratitude; (3) the broader ethics of stewardship — wealth, time, health are entrusted, not owned.
Related verses
Q 14:7 (if you are grateful, I will surely increase you). Q 16:18 (if you tried to count Allah's blessings, you could never enumerate them). Q 14:34 (verily, man is most unjust, ungrateful). Q 55 (Surah Ar-Raḥmān — repeated refrain "which of your Lord's favors will you deny?").
Quran 103:1–3
Humanity in loss Four conditions
وَالْعَصْرِ ۝ إِنَّ الْإِنسَانَ لَفِي خُسْرٍ ۝ إِلَّا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا وَعَمِلُوا الصَّالِحَاتِ وَتَوَاصَوْا بِالْحَقِّ وَتَوَاصَوْا بِالصَّبْرِ
"By time. Indeed, humanity is in loss — except those who believe, do good, and counsel each other to truth and counsel each other to patience."
Plain meaning
The shortest surah of summary — Imām al-Shāfiʿī said: "Had only this surah been revealed, it would have sufficed humanity." The default state of humans is khusr (loss). Escape from loss requires four cumulative qualities: (1) faith, (2) righteous action, (3) mutual counsel in truth — actively reminding others, (4) mutual counsel in patience — supporting each other in endurance. Notice the third and fourth: religion is communal, not just personal.
Majority view
All four schools and the entire Islamic ethical tradition consider this surah a comprehensive summary of the path to salvation: faith and good action are foundational, but insufficient alone — one must also actively encourage others in truth and patience. The pairing of truth and patience is significant: speaking truth requires the patience to bear consequence; patience without truth is mere endurance of injustice. Many Companions would meet, then before parting one would recite this surah to the other — as mutual counsel.
Related verses
Q 90:17 (mutual counsel in patience and mercy). Q 5:2 (cooperate in righteousness and taqwā). Q 49:9–10 (reconcile between brothers). Q 41:33 (who is better in speech than one who calls to Allah and does good and says: I am of those who submit?).
Quran 107:1–7
True religion Orphan Small kindnesses
أَرَأَيْتَ الَّذِي يُكَذِّبُ بِالدِّينِ ۝ فَذَٰلِكَ الَّذِي يَدُعُّ الْيَتِيمَ ۝ وَلَا يَحُضُّ عَلَىٰ طَعَامِ الْمِسْكِينِ ۝ فَوَيْلٌ لِّلْمُصَلِّينَ ۝ الَّذِينَ هُمْ عَن صَلَاتِهِمْ سَاهُونَ ۝ الَّذِينَ هُمْ يُرَاءُونَ ۝ وَيَمْنَعُونَ الْمَاعُونَ
"Have you seen the one who denies the religion? It is the one who repels the orphan, and does not urge the feeding of the poor. Woe to those who pray — those who are heedless of their prayer; those who pray to show off, and refuse small kindnesses (al-māʿūn)."
Plain meaning
A short surah that redefines religion. The denier of dīn is identified not as the disbeliever per se, but as: (1) someone who pushes away the orphan, (2) someone who doesn't even urge others to feed the poor. Then a sharper turn: woe to those who do pray — but pray heedlessly, for show, and refuse al-māʿūn (small everyday kindnesses — lending a bucket, a tool, a kind word).
Majority view
All four schools and the ethical tradition treat this surah as the supreme Quranic statement that ritual without ethical substance is rejected. The verse is shocking: it identifies prayer-performers — not non-prayers — as the targets of "woe". Three failures listed: (1) heedlessness in prayer, (2) showing off (riyāʾ) — universally identified as al-shirk al-aṣghar ("lesser shirk"), (3) refusing al-māʿūn — the small daily kindnesses that fabric of community depends on.
Related verses
Q 2:177 (true piety is not turning your face east or west). Q 4:142 (the hypocrites — when they stand for prayer, they stand lazily, showing off to people). Q 76:9 (we feed you only for Allah's face — we want from you no reward or thanks). Q 90:14–16 (feeding on a day of starvation — orphan or destitute).

261 of ~500 verses covered.
